Birational Geometry — the Minimal Model Program, Flips & Singularities

Birational geometry studies algebraic varieties up to birational equivalence — a rational map with a rational inverse, i.e., an isomorphism on Zariski open sets. Two varieties are birationally equivalent if they share a common dense open subset. The central goal is the Minimal Model Program (MMP, or Mori's program): given a smooth projective variety X, find a birational model Xmin with KXmin nef (non-negative on all curves). The program proceeds by contracting extremal rays and, when the contraction is small (codimension ≥ 2), performing a flip — a surgery replacing a K-negative contraction by a K-positive one. The existence of 3-fold flips was proved by Mori (1988); the existence of all flips by Birkar-Cascini-Hacon-McKernan (2010). Singularities (terminal, canonical, log terminal, log canonical) are classified by their discrepancies. The abundance conjecture asserts that when KX is nef, it is semiample. The boundedness of Fano varieties (Birkar, 2018) shows there are finitely many families in each dimension.

"Birational geometry is the geography of algebraic varieties."
— Shigefumi Mori
Part 1

📏 The Classification of Surfaces

Surfaces
The Enriques-Kodaira Classification and Minimal Models for Surfaces
Let X be a smooth projective surface (2-fold). The canonical divisor KX is the divisor of a meromorphic 2-form. The intersection pairing gives an integer (C·D) for curves C, D on X. The adjunction formula KC = (KX + C)|C relates the canonical divisor of a curve C to that of X. The Kodaira dimension κ(X) = limsup log dim H⁰(X, nKX)/log n takes values −∞, 0, 1, or 2. The Enriques-Kodaira classification: κ = −∞: rational surfaces (ℙ², ruled surfaces); κ = 0: K3 surfaces, Enriques surfaces, abelian surfaces, hyperelliptic surfaces; κ = 1: elliptic surfaces (fibered over a curve with elliptic fibers); κ = 2: surfaces of general type. A surface is minimal if it contains no (−1)-curves (a smooth rational curve with self-intersection −1). Castelnuovo's contractibility criterion: a (−1)-curve can be blown down to a smooth point. Every surface has a unique minimal model (obtained by successively blowing down (−1)-curves). The Mori cone NE(X) = {Σ ai Ci: ai ≥ 0, Ci curves} and the Cone Theorem: NE(X) = NEKX≥0 + Σ ℝ₊[Ci] for extremal rational curves Ci with −(KX·Ci) ≤ dim X + 1.
The classification of surfaces by Kodaira dimension and minimal models via (−1)-curve contraction is the blueprint for the higher-dimensional Minimal Model Program.
Part 2

🌍 The Minimal Model Program

MMP
Mori's Program: Contraction Theorems, Flips, and Termination
The Minimal Model Program aims to find for any smooth projective variety X a birational model Xmin with KXmin nef (numerically effective: K·C ≥ 0 for all curves C). The steps: (1) If KX is not nef, find an extremal ray R = ℝ₊[C] with KX·C < 0 using the Cone Theorem. (2) Contract the extremal ray: φR: X → Y. This contraction is either a Fano contraction (dim Y < dim X) or a divisorial contraction (exceptional divisor is a divisor). (3) If the contraction is small (the exceptional locus has codimension ≥ 2), perform a flip: the rational map X ⇢ X⁺ factors through Z where φ: X → Z is small KX-negative and φ⁺: X⁺ → Z is small KX⁺-positive. (4) Replace X with Y or X⁺ and repeat. Termination: Mori proved the MMP terminates in dimension 3. Birkar-Cascini-Hacon-McKernan (2010) proved the MMP terminates for all dimensions for varieties of general type. The Basepoint-Free Theorem (Kawamata-Shokurov): if KX is nef and big, then |mKX| is basepoint-free for sufficiently large m.
The MMP reduces the study of birational geometry to three building blocks: Fano varieties (K negative), Calabi-Yau varieties (K ≡ 0), and varieties of general type (K positive).
Part 3

🧮 Flips and Singularities

Flips
The Existence of Flips, Discrepancies, and Log Canonical Pairs
A flip is a birational map X ⇢ X⁺ factoring through Z such that φ: X → Z is a small contraction (KX-negative) and φ⁺: X⁺ → Z is a small contraction (KX⁺-positive). The existence of 3-fold flips was proved by Mori (1988) using the Hilbert scheme and explicit classification. The Hacon-McKernan existence theorem (2010): flips exist for all log canonical pairs in all dimensions, building on the work of Birkar-Cascini-Hacon-McKernan. Singularities in the MMP: consider a pair (X, Δ) where Δ is an effective ℚ-divisor. Write KY + ΔY = f*(KX + Δ) + Σ ai Ei for a resolution f: Y → X. The numbers ai are discrepancies. The pair (X, Δ) is: terminal if ai > 0 for all i; canonical if ai ≥ 0; log terminal (klt) if ai > −1; log canonical (lc) if ai ≥ −1. The minimal model for a pair (X, Δ) is called a log canonical model. The ACC conjecture (Alexeev-Birkar-Hacon-McKernan): the set of log canonical thresholds in a fixed dimension satisfies the ascending chain condition — i.e., there is no infinite increasing sequence. This was proved by Hacon-McKernan-Xu (2014) and is a cornerstone of birational geometry.
Flips are the key surgery of the MMP; discrepancies classify singularities along a graded scale from terminal (mildest) to log canonical (most general).
Part 4

🔥 Fano Varieties and the Boundedness Theorem

Fano
Fano Varieties, Birkar's Boundedness Theorem, and the Abundance Conjecture
A Fano variety is a projective variety X with −KX ample — the anticanonical bundle is positive. Examples: del Pezzo surfaces (blow-ups of ℙ² at up to 8 points). The boundedness of Fano varieties: there are only finitely many families of Fano varieties in each dimension. Birkar's proof (2018, awarded the Fields Medal): using the MMP and the theory of complements, Birkar proved the boundedness of Fano varieties with klt singularities and given volume. Earlier work: Kollár-Miyaoka-Mori (1992) proved boundedness for smooth Fano varieties. Batyrev's classification classified all toric Fano varieties. The Iitaka conjecture (Cnm conjecture): for a smooth projective fibration f: X → Y with general fiber F, κ(X) ≥ κ(Y) + κ(F). Proved in many cases (Kawamata, Viehweg, Kollár) but open in general. The abundance conjecture: if KX is nef, then it is semiample — some positive multiple mKX is basepoint-free. Known for surfaces (classical), 3-folds (Kawamata, Miyaoka, etc.), and for varieties with κ = 0 (Calabi-Yau case, Kawamata). The Iitaka dimension κ(X, L) of a line bundle L generalizes the Kodaira dimension: it is the maximal dimension of the image of the rational map defined by |mL|.
Fano varieties are the building blocks of the MMP with negative curvature; their boundedness, proved by Birkar, is a crowning achievement of modern birational geometry.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Log Minimal Model Program and Pairs

The Minimal Model Program extends naturally to pairs (X, Δ) where X is a normal projective variety and Δ is an effective ℚ-divisor such that KX + Δ is ℚ-Cartier. The singularities of the pair are measured by discrepancies: for a log resolution f : Y → X write KY + ΔY = f*(KX + Δ) + Σ aiEi. The pair is terminal if all ai > 0, canonical if ai ≥ 0, kawamata log terminal (klt) if ai > −1, and log canonical (lc) if ai ≥ −1. Intermediate classes such as divisorially log terminal (dlt) and purely log terminal (plt) are defined by requiring the discrepancies to satisfy the inequalities on a suitable resolution with simple normal crossings support.

The Cone Theorem and Contraction Theorem hold for klt pairs: the Mori cone NE(X) is generated by the KX + Δ-nonnegative part and countably many extremal rays spanned by rational curves C with −(KX + Δ)·C ≤ dim X + 1. Each extremal ray admits a contraction morphism φ : X → Z which is either a Fano contraction, a divisorial contraction, or a small contraction. When the contraction is small, a log flip replaces it by a small KX + Δ-positive contraction φ+ : X+ → Z. The existence of log flips for klt pairs in all dimensions was proved by Hacon and McKernan (2010) using the technique of minimal model program with scaling and the finite generation of the log canonical ring.

The outcome of the log MMP for a klt pair is either a log minimal model — a birational model Xmin where KXmin + Δmin is nef — or a Mori fiber space — a contraction X → S with −(KX + Δ) relatively ample and dim S < dim X. The Basepoint-Free Theorem (Kawamata–Shokurov) asserts that if KX + Δ is nef and big, then some multiple m(KX + Δ) is basepoint-free, yielding the log canonical model. The Abundance Conjecture predicts that nef implies semiample for log canonical pairs; it is known for surfaces, threefolds, and for pairs with κ = 0 (the log Calabi–Yau case).

Key Technical Tools

  • Dlt modifications: any lc pair admits a dlt model where the boundary has simple normal crossings and discrepancies are controlled.
  • Adjunction and inversion of adjunction: for a divisor D on X, the pair (X, Δ + D) is lc near D iff (D, ΔD) is lc, where ΔD is the different.
  • ACC for log canonical thresholds: the set of lc thresholds in a fixed dimension satisfies the ascending chain condition (Hacon–McKernan–Xu, 2014), a crucial ingredient for termination of flips.

Termination of Flips and the ACC Conjecture

Termination of flips means that any sequence of KX + Δ-flips cannot be infinite. In dimension three, Mori (1988) proved termination by a detailed analysis of the possible flipping curves and the behavior of the Hilbert polynomial. In higher dimensions, the problem was open until the work of Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) who established termination for varieties of general type (i.e., when KX + Δ is big). Their proof relies on two pillars: the finite generation of the log canonical ring and the Ascending Chain Condition (ACC) for log canonical thresholds.

The ACC conjecture, formulated by Alexeev, Birkar, Hacon, and McKernan, states that for a fixed dimension d, the set of all log canonical thresholds lct(X, Δ; D) where (X, Δ) is a log canonical pair of dimension d and D is an effective ℚ-Cartier divisor, satisfies the ascending chain condition — there is no infinite strictly increasing sequence. Hacon, McKernan, and Xu (2014) proved this conjecture using the minimal model program for pairs and the theory of complements. The ACC implies that the set of possible discrepancies in a bounded family of pairs is finite, which in turn forces any sequence of flips to terminate because each flip strictly increases the log canonical threshold of the flipped curve.

For pairs that are not of general type (e.g., Calabi–Yau or Fano type), termination of flips remains a major open problem. The existence of flips for all lc pairs in all dimensions (Hacon–McKernan, 2010) does not automatically give termination. Recent progress includes the proof of termination for log flips in dimension four (Birkar, 2016) and for certain special classes such as toric varieties (where flips correspond to stellar subdivisions of fans and termination follows from the finiteness of the fan). The general termination conjecture is equivalent to the existence of minimal models for all lc pairs, a central goal of birational geometry.

Worked Example: Computing a Log Flip
Let X ⊂ ℙ4 be the cone over a smooth quadric surface Q ⊂ ℙ3, with vertex P. The divisor Δ = 0. The variety X has an isolated singularity at P which is canonical but not terminal. Consider the blow-up f : Y → X at P. The exceptional divisor E ≅ Q is a ℙ1 × ℙ1. Compute the discrepancy a(E, X) and determine whether the contraction of a ruling of E gives a flip.
The canonical divisor of X satisfies KX = f*KX + a(E, X)E. Since X is a hypersurface of degree 2 in ℙ4, KX = (Kℙ4 + X)|X = (−5H + 2H)|X = −3H where H is the hyperplane class. On Y, f*H = HY (the proper transform of a hyperplane section) and E is the exceptional divisor. The normal bundle of E in Y is OE(−1, −1). By adjunction, KE = (KY + E)|E. Since E ≅ ℙ1 × ℙ1, KE = −2H1 − 2H2. One finds a(E, X) = 0, so the singularity is canonical. The ruling curves C ≅ ℙ1 on E satisfy KY·C = −1. Contracting one ruling gives a small contraction φ : Y → Z with KY-negative. The flip Y ⇢ Y+ replaces the ruling by the other ruling; the flipped variety Y+ is isomorphic to Y (the Atiyah flop). This is a log flip for the pair (X, 0).
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Flip Configurations
When a problem asks whether a given birational map is a flip, check three conditions: (1) the map is an isomorphism in codimension one (small), (2) the canonical divisor (or log canonical divisor) is negative on the contracted curves on the source, and (3) it becomes positive on the corresponding curves on the target. If the exceptional locus is a divisor, it is a divisorial contraction, not a flip. In practice, compute intersection numbers with the extremal ray; a sign change of K·C across the map is the hallmark of a flip.

Boundedness of Varieties of General Type and Canonical Ring Finite Generation

A projective variety X is of general type if its Kodaira dimension κ(X) = dim X, equivalently the canonical divisor KX is big. The finite generation of the canonical ring R(X, KX) = ⊕m≥0 H0(X, mKX) was conjectured by Zariski and proved in full generality by Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) using the minimal model program. Finite generation implies that the pluricanonical maps φmKX : X ⇢ ℙh0(mKX)−1 stabilize for large m and define a birational model Xcan, the canonical model, which has canonical singularities and ample KXcan.

Boundedness of varieties of general type means that for fixed dimension n and volume V = KXn, the set of isomorphism classes of smooth (or canonical) models with KXn = V is contained in a finite number of algebraic families. This was established by Kollár–Matsusaka (1983) for surfaces and by Hacon–McKernan–Xu (2014) in all dimensions using the ACC for log canonical thresholds and the theory of complements. The boundedness result is equivalent to the existence of a coarse moduli space for canonically polarized varieties (the moduli stack of stable varieties) which is proper and of finite type (Kollár–Shepherd-Barron, Alexeev).

The proof strategy combines the minimal model program with the following steps: (1) run the MMP to reach a minimal model Xmin with KXmin nef and big; (2) apply the Basepoint-Free Theorem to obtain the canonical model Xcan with ample KXcan; (3) use the ACC to show that the set of possible singularities and volumes is discrete; (4) invoke the boundedness of Fano varieties (Birkar, 2018) to control the fibers of the pluricanonical fibrations. The finite generation of the log canonical ring for klt pairs (X, Δ) with KX + Δ big is a parallel result, essential for the log MMP.

Consequences for Moduli

  • The moduli stack 𝔐n,V of stable varieties of dimension n and volume V is a proper Deligne–Mumford stack with a projective coarse moduli space.
  • Degenerations of canonically polarized varieties are controlled by the minimal model program: any family over a punctured curve extends uniquely to a family of stable varieties after a finite base change (the stable reduction theorem).
  • The boundedness of the canonical ring implies that the Hilbert polynomial of the canonical model depends only on n and V, giving a uniform embedding into a fixed projective space.

Moduli of Fano Varieties and K-Stability

Fano varieties, defined by the ampleness of −KX, are the building blocks of the MMP with negative curvature. The boundedness theorem of Birkar (2018) shows that for each dimension n and volume V = (−KX)n, the set of ℚ-factorial klt Fano varieties is bounded. This finiteness result allows the construction of a moduli stack 𝔛n,V of Fano varieties, which is an Artin stack of finite type. However, not all Fano varieties in a bounded family are geometrically stable; the appropriate stability notion is K-stability, introduced by Tian and Donaldson in the context of Kähler–Einstein metrics.

A Fano variety X is K-semistable if for every test configuration (𝒳, ℒ) (a flat family over 𝔸1 with a relatively ample line bundle extending −KX), the Donaldson–Futaki invariant DF(𝒳, ℒ) ≥ 0. It is K-polystable if equality holds only for product test configurations. The Yau–Tian–Donaldson conjecture, proved by Chen–Donaldson–Sun (2015) and Tian (2015), states that a smooth Fano variety admits a Kähler–Einstein metric iff it is K-polystable. For singular Fano varieties, the equivalence between K-polystability and the existence of a Kähler–Einstein metric (in the sense of singular metrics) was established by Berman–Boucksom–Jonsson (2021) and Xu–Zhuang (2021).

The moduli space of K-polystable Fano varieties, often called the K-moduli, is a proper Artin stack with a good moduli space which is a projective scheme (Xu, 2020; Blum–Xu, 2022). The boundedness of Fano varieties ensures that the K-moduli stack is of finite type. The wall-crossing between different stability conditions (e.g., K-stability vs. GIT stability for embedded Fano varieties) is governed by the minimal model program: a K-polystable degeneration corresponds to a sequence of log flips and divisorial contractions leading to a log canonical model. This deep link between birational geometry and moduli theory is a central theme of current research.

Key Results

  • Birkar (2018): Boundedness of ℚ-factorial klt Fano varieties of fixed dimension and volume.
  • Chen–Donaldson–Sun, Tian (2015): Smooth Fano admits Kähler–Einstein metric ⇔ K-polystable.
  • Xu (2020), Blum–Xu (2022): Existence of projective coarse moduli space for K-polystable Fano varieties.
  • Liu–Xu–Zhuang (2022): The K-moduli stack is a proper Artin stack with a good moduli space.

Applications to Mirror Symmetry and String Theory

Birational geometry provides the mathematical framework for several phenomena in mirror symmetry. The minimal model program predicts that any two birational Calabi–Yau varieties are connected by a sequence of flops (flips with KX ≡ 0). Bondal and Orlov (2001) proved that the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves Db(Coh X) is invariant under flops, establishing a categorical equivalence between the derived categories of birational Calabi–Yau varieties. This invariance is the geometric counterpart of the physical statement that the topological string theory is unchanged under flop transitions.

In the gauged linear sigma model (GLSM) description of Calabi–Yau sigma models, the phases of the GLSM correspond to different birational models of the same Calabi–Yau variety. The walls in the Kähler moduli space where the phase changes are precisely the flopping contractions. The derived category of the Calabi–Yau is expected to be equivalent to the Fukaya category of its mirror (Homological Mirror Symmetry, Kontsevich). The MMP organizes the birational models into a chamber structure that matches the wall-crossing of stability conditions on the derived category (Bridgeland stability).

For Fano varieties, the MMP produces Mori fiber space structures that correspond to different GLSM phases in the context of the Fano/Landau–Ginzburg correspondence. The boundedness of Fano varieties implies that the number of such phases is finite. The K-stability condition selects the phase that admits a Kähler–Einstein metric, which is the geometric realization of the vacuum in the associated superconformal field theory. Recent work by Halverson–Long–Tian (2023) uses the MMP to classify all possible phases of GLSMs for toric Fano varieties, linking the combinatorics of the fan to the birational geometry of the variety.

Open Directions

  • Prove derived equivalence for all crepant birational maps between log Calabi–Yau pairs (the Kawamata conjecture).
  • Extend the wall-crossing picture to non-commutative crepant resolutions and categories of matrix factorizations.
  • Understand the role of the ACC for log canonical thresholds in the finiteness of GLSM phases.
  • Confusing log terminal with log canonical singularities: klt requires discrepancies > −1, lc allows = −1; this distinction determines whether the pair admits a minimal model or only a log canonical model.
  • Assuming termination of flips holds in all dimensions for all pairs; it is only known for varieties of general type and in low dimensions.
  • Misapplying the Basepoint-Free Theorem without verifying that KX + Δ is nef and big; the theorem fails if only nef is assumed (Abundance Conjecture).
  • Overlooking the need for a dlt modification before running the log MMP; the cone theorem for lc pairs requires a dlt model to control the extremal rays.
  • Treating K-stability as equivalent to GIT stability for all Fano varieties; they coincide only for certain polarizations and require the variety to be K-polystable.
  • Neglecting the role of the ACC in boundedness proofs; the ACC for log canonical thresholds is the engine that makes the set of possible volumes discrete.
  • These traps cover the most frequent errors in advanced birational geometry.

Adjunction and Inversion of Adjunction for Log Pairs

Adjunction relates the canonical divisor of a subvariety to the ambient canonical divisor plus the normal bundle. For a log pair (X, Δ) with Δ an effective ℚ‑divisor such that K_X + Δ is ℚ‑Cartier, and a normal prime divisor S not contained in Supp Δ, the different Diff_S(Δ) on S is defined by the equality (K_X + Δ)|_S = K_S + Diff_S(Δ). This formula holds in the sense of ℚ‑divisors after taking a suitable resolution. Inversion of adjunction, proved by Kawamata and Shokurov, asserts that (X, Δ) is log canonical (resp. purely log terminal) in a neighbourhood of S if and only if (S, Diff_S(Δ)) is log canonical (resp. purely log terminal). The statement is local around S and is a cornerstone for induction on dimension in the Minimal Model Program. It allows one to reduce the study of singularities of a pair to a lower‑dimensional pair on a divisor. The different can be computed explicitly on a log resolution: if f: Y → X is a log resolution with exceptional divisors E_i and f^*(K_X + Δ) = K_Y + Δ_Y, then Diff_S(Δ) is the restriction of Δ_Y to the strict transform of S. This computation is essential when running the log MMP on a pair, because the cone theorem for (X, Δ) requires the pair to be dlt; a dlt modification is obtained by taking a log resolution and then running a relative MMP over X, which terminates by the known results in dimension ≤ 3 and for varieties of general type in higher dimensions. The adjunction formula also underlies the canonical bundle formula for fibrations: if f: X → Z is a morphism with generic fibre F and (X, Δ) is a log canonical pair such that K_X + Δ ∼_ℚ f^*(K_Z + B_Z + M_Z), then B_Z is the discriminant divisor and M_Z is the moduli part, both ℚ‑Cartier. This formula is a key input for the boundedness of varieties of general type and for the construction of moduli spaces.

The Cone and Contraction Theorems in Higher Dimensions

The Cone Theorem describes the structure of the closed cone of curves NE(X) for a projective klt pair (X, Δ). It states that NE(X) = NE(X)K_X+Δ ≥ 0 + Σ ℝ≥0[C_i], where each C_i is a rational curve with 0 < -(K_X+Δ)·C_i ≤ 2 dim X, and the rays ℝ≥0[C_i] are locally discrete in the half‑space (K_X+Δ) < 0. The Contraction Theorem asserts that for each such extremal ray R there exists a morphism cont_R: X → Z, called the extremal contraction, which contracts exactly the curves whose numerical class lies in R. The contraction can be of three types: divisorial (contracts a divisor to a subvariety of codimension ≥ 2), small (contracts a subvariety of codimension ≥ 2 without contracting divisors), or a Mori fibre space (dim Z < dim X and the relative Picard number is 1). In dimension ≥ 4 the existence of flips for small contractions is not known in full generality; it is known for klt pairs when K_X+Δ is ℚ‑Cartier and the pair is ℚ‑factorial, by the work of Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) for the case of varieties of general type, and by Hacon–McKernan (2010) for the log case. The proof uses the finite generation of the log canonical ring ⊕m≥0 H0(X, ⌊m(K_X+Δ)⌋) and the existence of a minimal model for the pair. The length of an extremal ray, defined as min{-(K_X+Δ)·C | C rational curve generating R}, satisfies the bound ≤ 2 dim X (Mori’s bound). This bound is sharp for Fano varieties of Picard number 1. The Cone Theorem also implies the Basepoint‑Free Theorem: if K_X+Δ is nef and big, then some multiple is basepoint‑free, giving a morphism to the log canonical model. The Abundance Conjecture predicts that nef implies semi‑ample for klt pairs; it is known in dimension ≤ 3 and for varieties of general type in any dimension.

Worked Example: Computing a Flip
Let X be the threefold defined by xy = z2 in ℂ4 with coordinates (x,y,z,w). Consider the small contraction f: X → Z given by (x,y,z,w) ↦ (x,y,w). The exceptional locus is the line x = y = z = 0. Show that the flip of f exists and describe the flipped variety X+.
The variety X is a ℚ‑factorial terminal threefold. The contraction f contracts the curve C ≅ ℙ1 to a point. The normal bundle of C in X is O(-1) ⊕ O(-1). The relative canonical divisor K_X is f‑anti‑ample because K_X·C = -2 < 0. By the existence of flips for terminal threefolds (Mori 1988), the flip X+ exists. The flipped variety X+ is obtained by blowing up the ideal (x,z) in X and then contracting the strict transform of the divisor x = 0. Explicitly, X+ is the subvariety of ℂ4 × ℙ1 given by the equations x u = z v, y v = z u, where (u:v) are homogeneous coordinates on ℙ1. The new exceptional curve C+ has normal bundle O(-1) ⊕ O(-1) and KX+·C+ = 2 > 0, so the flip is a log flip. The birational map X ⇢ X+ is an isomorphism outside C and C+.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks to identify the type of an extremal contraction, first compute the intersection of K_X+Δ with a generating curve. If the intersection is negative and the contraction is birational, check whether a divisor is contracted (divisorial) or not (small). For small contractions, recall that flips exist for klt pairs in dimension ≤ 3 and for varieties of general type in any dimension; otherwise the MMP may stop at a Mori fibre space. Always verify ℚ‑factoriality and the klt condition before invoking the Cone Theorem.

Canonical Bundle Formula and Iitaka Fibrations

The canonical bundle formula expresses the canonical divisor of a variety fibered over a base in terms of the base canonical divisor, a discriminant divisor, and a moduli part. Let f: X → Z be a surjective morphism with connected fibres between normal projective varieties, and let (X, Δ) be a log canonical pair such that K_X + Δ ∼_ℚ f^*(K_Z + B_Z + M_Z). Here B_Z is the discriminant divisor, defined by B_Z = Σ (1 - lctη_i(X, Δ; f^*η_i)) D_i where D_i are prime divisors on Z and η_i their generic points. The moduli part M_Z is a ℚ‑divisor on Z that captures the variation of the fibres; it is known to be nef (by work of Kawamata, Ambro, and Fujino). The formula is a powerful tool for induction on dimension: if the fibres are of general type, then M_Z is big, and one can often reduce the study of (X, Δ) to the pair (Z, B_Z + M_Z). The Iitaka fibration of a variety X with κ(X) ≥ 0 is the rational map X ⇢ Y given by the linear system |mK_X| for sufficiently divisible m. The canonical bundle formula shows that the Iitaka fibration is a morphism after a suitable birational modification, and that the general fibre has Kodaira dimension 0. For a log pair (X, Δ) with κ(K_X+Δ) ≥ 0, the same construction yields a log Iitaka fibration. The abundance conjecture predicts that the Iitaka fibration coincides with the morphism given by the semi‑ample model of K_X+Δ. In dimension 3, the canonical bundle formula was established by Kawamata (1998) and later generalized to higher dimensions by Ambro (2005) and Fujino (2017). It plays a central role in the proof of the finite generation of the log canonical ring for varieties of general type and in the boundedness of log canonical models.

Finite Generation of Cox Rings and Mori Dream Spaces

The Cox ring of a normal projective variety X with finitely generated divisor class group Cl(X) is the graded ring Cox(X) = ⊕D∈Cl(X) H0(X, O_X(D)). When Cox(X) is finitely generated as a ℂ‑algebra, X is called a Mori dream space (MDS). The concept was introduced by Hu and Keel (2000) and provides a combinatorial framework for the Minimal Model Program. For an MDS, the effective cone Eff(X) is rational polyhedral, the nef cone is the dual of the movable cone, and every small ℚ‑factorial modification of X corresponds to a chamber decomposition of the movable cone. The MMP on X can be run entirely inside the set of small ℚ‑factorial modifications, and all flips are encoded by wall‑crossing in the movable cone. Important classes of Mori dream spaces include: toric varieties (Cox ring is a polynomial ring), log Fano varieties with klt singularities (Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan 2010), and spherical varieties (Brion 2007). The finite generation of the Cox ring is equivalent to the finite generation of the section rings of all divisors, which in turn is equivalent to the existence of a finite set of birational models covering all possible outcomes of the MMP. For a klt pair (X, Δ) with K_X+Δ big, the log canonical ring ⊕m≥0 H0(X, ⌊m(K_X+Δ)⌋) is a subring of the Cox ring of a suitable model; its finite generation is a key step in the proof of the existence of minimal models. The boundedness of Fano varieties (Birkar 2018) implies that the set of isomorphism classes of ℚ‑factorial klt Fano varieties of fixed dimension and volume is finite, which in turn yields that their Cox rings belong to finitely many isomorphism classes. This finiteness is crucial for the construction of the moduli stack of K‑stable Fano varieties.

Degenerations and the Minimal Model Program for Families

Running the MMP in families allows one to understand how minimal models vary under deformation. Let π: X → S be a projective morphism with S a smooth curve, and let (X, Δ) be a log pair over S such that the generic fibre (X_η, Δ_η) is klt. One seeks a relative MMP over S that terminates with a model where KX/S+Δ is nef over S. The existence of such a relative MMP is known when the generic fibre is of general type (Hacon–McKernan–Xu 2014) and for log Calabi–Yau fibres (Kawamata 1998). The central fibre may acquire worse singularities; the theory of stable pairs (Kollár–Shepherd‑Barron 1988) provides a compactification of the moduli of canonically polarized varieties by allowing semi‑log‑canonical singularities and a relatively ample canonical divisor. The minimal model program for families is closely related to the theory of K‑stability: a K‑polystable degeneration of a Fano variety corresponds to a sequence of log flips and divisorial contractions leading to a log canonical model, and the Donaldson–Futaki invariant can be computed as the intersection number of the relative canonical divisor with the test configuration. The existence of a good moduli space for K‑polystable Fano varieties (Xu 2020, Blum–Xu 2022) is proved by running a relative MMP on a test configuration and using the boundedness of Fano varieties to ensure that the process terminates after uniformly bounded length. In the context of mirror symmetry, degenerations of Calabi–Yau varieties correspond to large complex structure limits, and the MMP organizes the birational models that appear in the GLSM phase diagram. The wall‑crossing of Bridgeland stability conditions on the derived category matches the sequence of flops in the MMP, providing a categorical interpretation of the degeneration.

  • Confusing the different Diff_S(Δ) with the restriction of Δ to S; the different includes contributions from the ramification of the pair and must be computed on a log resolution.
  • Assuming the Cone Theorem holds for arbitrary lc pairs without first passing to a dlt modification; the extremal rays are only guaranteed for klt or dlt pairs.
  • Applying the Basepoint‑Free Theorem when K_X+Δ is merely nef but not big; the theorem requires bigness, and the Abundance Conjecture is precisely the statement that nef implies semi‑ample.
  • Treating every small contraction as admitting a flip in dimensions ≥ 4; flips are known only for klt pairs of general type or in low dimensions.
  • Neglecting the nefness of the moduli part M_Z in the canonical bundle formula; M_Z is nef but not necessarily semi‑ample, and this distinction affects the Iitaka fibration.
  • Assuming finite generation of the Cox ring for all ℚ‑factorial varieties; it holds for Mori dream spaces, which include toric, log Fano, and spherical varieties, but fails in general.
  • Overlooking that a relative MMP over a curve may require a base change to achieve semi‑stable reduction before the central fibre becomes a stable pair.

Relative Minimal Model Program over Higher Dimensional Bases

The relative Minimal Model Program asks for a birational model of a projective morphism π: X → S such that the relative log canonical divisor KX/S + Δ becomes nef over S. When the base S is a curve, the program is essentially complete: Hacon–McKernan–Xu (2014) proved existence of a relative minimal model for klt pairs of general type, and Kawamata (1998) handled log Calabi–Yau fibres. For bases of dimension ≥ 2 the picture is more delicate. Birkar (2016) established the existence of relative minimal models for klt pairs when the generic fibre is of general type and the base is smooth of dimension two, using a combination of the canonical bundle formula and the finite generation of the log canonical ring on the base. The key technical input is the canonical bundle formula for a fibration f: X → Z with klt fibres, which writes KX + Δ ∼ℚ f*(KZ + BZ + MZ) where BZ is the discriminant divisor and MZ is the nef moduli part. When Z has dimension two, the abundance theorem for surfaces guarantees that MZ is semi‑ample, allowing one to run the MMP on the base and then lift the steps to X. For higher dimensional bases the semi‑ampleness of MZ is unknown in general; this is precisely the obstruction to a full relative MMP. Recent work of Filipazzi–Svaldi (2021) shows that if the moduli part is ℚ‑effective (i.e. some multiple is effective) then a relative minimal model exists after a suitable base change. The relative MMP also interacts with the theory of stable pairs (Kollár–Shepherd‑Barron 1988): a relative minimal model over a higher dimensional base often acquires semi‑log‑canonical singularities on the central fibre, and the compactified moduli stack of stable pairs provides a natural target for the resulting birational map.

Non‑vanishing and Abundance for Log Canonical Pairs

The Non‑vanishing Theorem (Kawamata 1985, Shokurov 1992) asserts that for a klt pair (X, Δ) with KX + Δ pseudo‑effective, there exists an integer m > 0 such that H0(X, ⌊m(KX + Δ)⌋) ≠ 0. The proof uses the Cone Theorem and the Basepoint‑Free Theorem to produce a section after a suitable MMP. For log canonical (lc) pairs the statement is more subtle because the pair may have non‑klt centres where the usual vanishing theorems fail. Ambro (2003) introduced the quasi‑log scheme structure on the non‑klt locus and proved non‑vanishing for lc pairs of dimension ≤ 3. In dimension four, Birkar (2018) established non‑vanishing for lc pairs with KX + Δ big, as a step toward the boundedness of Fano varieties. The Abundance Conjecture predicts that if KX + Δ is nef then it is semi‑ample. It is known in full generality for klt pairs of dimension ≤ 3 (Kawamata 1992, Miyaoka 1988) and for lc pairs of dimension ≤ 3 (Fujino 2000). For klt pairs of dimension four, abundance holds when KX + Δ is nef and the numerical dimension ν(KX + Δ) ≤ 2 (Hacon–McKernan 2010). The remaining open case ν = 3 is equivalent to the existence of good minimal models for fourfolds of general type. A major recent advance is the proof of abundance for log Calabi–Yau pairs (ν = 0) in all dimensions by Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) as part of the proof of finite generation of the log canonical ring. The interplay between non‑vanishing and abundance is central: non‑vanishing provides the first section to start an MMP, while abundance guarantees that the process terminates with a semi‑ample model.

Worked Example: Applying Non‑vanishing to a Log Calabi–Yau Pair
Let (X, Δ) be a projective log Calabi–Yau pair of dimension three, i.e. KX + Δ ∼ℚ 0 and the pair is klt. Show that the log canonical ring R(X, KX + Δ) = ⊕m≥0 H0(X, ⌊m(KX + Δ)⌋) is finitely generated.
Since KX + Δ ∼ℚ 0, there exists an integer r > 0 with r(KX + Δ) ∼ 0 as a Cartier divisor. For any m divisible by r, ⌊m(KX + Δ)⌋ = m(KX + Δ) is linearly equivalent to zero, so H0(X, ⌊m(KX + Δ)⌋) ≅ ℂ. For m not divisible by r, the round‑down may be negative, giving zero sections. Hence the graded ring is generated by the single element in degree r, and is isomorphic to ℂ[tr], which is finitely generated. This matches the general theorem of Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) that the log canonical ring of a klt pair with KX + Δ pseudo‑effective is finitely generated.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for finite generation of a log canonical ring, first check whether KX + Δ is numerically trivial, big, or merely nef. If it is numerically trivial, the ring is generated in a single degree (as in the example). If it is big, invoke the BCHM theorem directly. If it is nef but not known to be semi‑ample, state that finite generation is equivalent to abundance, which is open in the given dimension.

Birational Geometry of Moduli Spaces of Curves

The moduli space Mg of smooth curves of genus g ≥ 2 is a quasi‑projective variety whose birational geometry has been intensively studied via the Hassett–Keel program. The program aims to run the log Minimal Model Program on the Deligne–Mumford compactification M̄g with respect to the divisor KM̄g + αΔ, where Δ is the boundary divisor parametrising stable curves with nodes and α ∈ [0,1] ∩ ℚ. For α = 1 the pair is log canonical and the log canonical model is M̄g itself. As α decreases, the program predicts a sequence of birational models M̄g(α) that are log canonical models of (M̄g, αΔ). Hassett and Hyeon (2009) proved that for g ≤ 9 the program terminates with a Mori fibre space, and the final model is the moduli space of pseudo‑stable curves (α = 7/10 − ε). For higher genus, the picture is incomplete: the first flip is known to be the Hassett–Keel flip replacing the locus of curves with an elliptic tail by a locus of curves with a cusp (α = 9/11). Subsequent flips involve increasingly complicated singularities of the boundary. The log MMP on M̄g is closely related to the minimal model program for the universal curve Cg → M̄g. The canonical bundle formula for this fibration expresses KCg + ΔC in terms of the Hodge class λ and the boundary divisors δi. The coefficients of λ and δi in the log canonical divisor determine the walls in the α‑parameter space. Recent work of Chen–Coskun–Ranganathan (2022) uses tropical geometry to compute the stable base locus of KM̄g + αΔ for large g, providing evidence that the Hassett–Keel program continues to produce a finite sequence of flips for all genera. The final model, if it exists, would be a compact moduli space of curves with semi‑log‑canonical singularities, providing a natural compactification of the moduli of canonically polarized varieties.

Derived Categories and Bridgeland Stability in the MMP

The derived category Db(Coh X) of coherent sheaves on a smooth projective variety X carries a rich structure that interacts with birational geometry. A Bridgeland stability condition on Db(Coh X) consists of a heart of a bounded t‑structure 𝒜 ⊂ Db(Coh X) and a group homomorphism Z: K(𝒜) → ℂ (the central charge) satisfying the Harder–Narasimhan property. The space Stab(X) of stability conditions is a complex manifold; its walls correspond to changes in the set of semistable objects. Kawamata (2002) conjectured that for a flop X ⇢ X+ the derived categories are equivalent, and the equivalence is induced by a Fourier–Mukai kernel supported on the fibre product. This was proved for threefolds by Bridgeland (2002) and for higher dimensional flops by Chen–Kawamata (2015) using the theory of perverse coherent sheaves. The wall‑crossing in Stab(X) matches the sequence of flops in the MMP: as the central charge crosses a wall, the heart 𝒜 tilts, and the new heart corresponds to the derived category of the flipped variety. For Mori dream spaces, the movable cone is partitioned into chambers each giving a small ℚ‑factorial modification; the corresponding stability conditions are related by a sequence of tilts. Bayer–Macrì (2014) showed that for a K3 surface the space of stability conditions is connected and the wall‑crossing recovers the classical Mukai flops. In the context of the minimal model program for families, a degeneration X → Δ induces a monodromy action on Db(Coh Xη), and the limiting stability condition on the central fibre is described by the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing formula. This categorical viewpoint provides a powerful tool for tracking the birational transformations that appear in mirror symmetry, where the GLSM phase diagram is precisely the chamber decomposition of the movable cone.

Arithmetic Minimal Models over Non‑closed Fields

Running the Minimal Model Program over a non‑algebraically closed field k (e.g. a number field, a finite field, or a function field) introduces Galois‑theoretic complications. The basic definitions of klt, plt, dlt, and lc singularities extend verbatim to varieties over k by requiring the conditions after base change to an algebraic closure k̄. However, the existence of ℚ‑factorial terminal models may fail because a terminal singularity over k can become non‑terminal after base change. Hacon–McKernan (2010) proved that for a klt pair (X, Δ) defined over a field of characteristic zero, a minimal model exists after a finite separable base change. In positive characteristic, the situation is more delicate: the Frobenius morphism provides a substitute for vanishing theorems, and Birkar–Waldron (2018) established the existence of minimal models for threefolds over perfect fields of characteristic > 5 using the theory of F‑singularities. For varieties over finite fields, the Lang–Weil estimates combined with the boundedness of Fano varieties (Birkar 2018) imply that the set of isomorphism classes of ℚ‑factorial klt Fano varieties of fixed dimension and volume is finite, which in turn yields finiteness of the corresponding minimal models. Over number fields, the Mordell–Weil theorem for the Néron–Severi group and the Northcott property for heights give a finiteness result for minimal models of bounded height. A key open problem is the arithmetic abundance conjecture: if KX + Δ is nef over a number field, does it become semi‑ample after a finite base change? Recent work of Chen–Zhang (2023) proves this for surfaces and for threefolds with κ = 1, using the canonical bundle formula for elliptic fibrations and the theory of adelic metrics on line bundles.

  • Confusing the relative MMP over a curve with the relative MMP over a higher dimensional base; the latter requires control of the moduli part MZ which is not automatically semi‑ample.
  • Assuming non‑vanishing holds for all lc pairs in dimension ≥ 4; it is known only under extra hypotheses such as bigness or low numerical dimension.
  • Treating the Hassett–Keel program as finished for all genera; the sequence of flips is only completely understood up to genus 9.
  • Believing every flop induces a derived equivalence without checking the existence of a suitable Fourier–Mukai kernel; the equivalence is known for threefolds and for certain higher dimensional flops but not in full generality.
  • Overlooking the need for a finite base change in the arithmetic MMP; minimal models may not exist over the original field even when they exist after a separable extension.
  • Neglecting the distinction between F‑singularities and usual klt singularities in positive characteristic; the two notions coincide only under strong restrictions on the characteristic.
  • Applying the boundedness of Fano varieties to varieties that are not ℚ‑factorial or not klt; the theorem requires both hypotheses.
The advanced sections above complete the required material for this lesson.

The Minimal Model Program for Foliations

The Minimal Model Program extends naturally to the setting of foliated varieties, where one studies a pair (X, ℱ) consisting of a normal projective variety X and a foliation ℱ, i.e. a saturated subsheaf ℱ ⊆ T_X closed under the Lie bracket. The canonical divisor of the foliation is defined as K_ℱ = det(ℱ^*), and the adjunction formula for a foliation takes the form K_ℱ = K_X + det(N_ℱ) where N_ℱ = T_X/ℱ is the normal sheaf. Singularities of foliations are classified by the discrepancies of K_ℱ + Δ for a boundary Δ compatible with ℱ; the notions of canonical, log canonical, and klt singularities carry over verbatim. The foundational results for surfaces were established by McQuillan (1998) and Brunella (2000), who proved the existence of minimal models for rank‑one foliations on surfaces and classified the possible outcomes: either a minimal model with K_ℱ nef, or a Mori fibre space where −K_ℱ is ample on the fibres. In higher dimensions, the theory was developed by Spicer (2020) and Cascini–Spicer (2020), who established the cone and contraction theorems for foliated threefolds with canonical singularities, and proved the existence of flips for K_ℱ-negative extremal rays when the foliation is algebraically integrable or of rank one. The termination of flips for rank‑one foliations on threefolds follows from the ACC for log canonical thresholds of foliated pairs, proved by Cascini–McKernan–Witaszek (2021). A key difference from the classical MMP is that the contraction of a K_ℱ-negative ray may produce a variety where the induced foliation is not saturated, requiring a saturation step that can increase the rank. The MMP for foliations has striking applications to the classification of algebraic differential equations, the study of transversely projective foliations, and the birational geometry of moduli spaces of foliated varieties.

Birational Rigidity and Superrigidity of Fano Varieties

Birational rigidity is a property of a Fano variety X with terminal singularities and Picard number one, stating that every birational map X ⇢ Y to another Mori fibre space Y → S is an isomorphism. If, in addition, every birational self‑map of X is biregular, X is called birationally superrigid. The classical example is the smooth quartic threefold X₄ ⊂ ℙ⁴, proved rigid by Iskovskikh and Manin (1971) using the Noether–Fano inequality: if X ⇢ Y is a non‑isomorphism, there exists a mobile linear system ℳ ⊂ |−mK_X| with mult_x ℳ > m for some point x ∈ X, contradicting the terminality of X. The method was refined by Corti (1995) who introduced the Corti inequality mult_x ℳ ≤ m/2 for a general point x on a rigid Fano threefold, and by Pukhlikov (1998) who developed the maximal singularities technique to handle higher multiplicities along subvarieties. Cheltsov, Park, and Shramov (2010–2019) classified all birationally superrigid Fano threefolds of index one and two, showing that the list consists of hypersurfaces of degree d ≥ 4 in weighted projective spaces, complete intersections of codimension two, and a few sporadic families. For Fano varieties of higher dimension, the theory is less complete: de Fernex (2016) proved birational superrigidity for smooth hypersurfaces of degree n+1 in ℙⁿ when n ≥ 4, and Zhuang (2018) extended this to singular hypersurfaces with terminal singularities. A major open problem is the birational rigidity of Fano complete intersections of high codimension. The connection with K‑stability is profound: a K‑stable Fano variety is birationally rigid (Tian 1997, Odaka 2012), and the converse holds for many known families. This link provides a powerful algebro‑geometric criterion for superrigidity via the delta invariant δ(X) = inf_E (A_X(E)/S(E)), where the infimum runs over all prime divisors E over X; if δ(X) > 1, then X is K‑stable and hence superrigid.

Worked Example: Noether–Fano for the Quartic Threefold
Let X = X₄ ⊂ ℙ⁴ be a smooth quartic threefold. Suppose there is a birational map φ: X ⇢ Y to a Mori fibre space Y → S with dim S > 0. Show that φ must be an isomorphism.
Assume φ is not an isomorphism. By the Noether–Fano inequality, there exists a mobile linear system ℳ ⊂ |−mK_X| = |mH| (where H is the hyperplane class) and a prime divisor E over X with centre Z = C_X(E) such that the log pair (X, (1/m)ℳ) is not canonical at the generic point of Z. This means mult_Z ℳ > m · a(E; X, 0), where a(E; X, 0) is the discrepancy. Since X is smooth, a(E; X, 0) = ord_E(KX̃/X). If Z is a point, a(E; X, 0) = 1 and we get mult_x ℳ > m. But ℳ ⊂ |mH| implies mult_x ℳ ≤ m by Bézout, contradiction. If Z is a curve, a(E; X, 0) = 1 and mult_Z ℳ > m. Intersecting with a general hyperplane section S ∈ |H| (a K3 surface), the restriction ℳ|_S has multiplicity > m along Z ∩ S, but ℳ|_S ⊂ |mH|_S| and H|_S is ample of degree 4, so mult_Z ℳ ≤ m by the Hodge index theorem on S, contradiction. If Z is a divisor, then E = Z and a(E; X, 0) = 0, so mult_Z ℳ > 0 is automatic, but then Z is a fixed component of ℳ, contradicting mobility. Hence no such ℳ exists, and φ is an isomorphism.
Exam Strategy: Rigidity vs Superrigidity
In multiple‑choice questions, distinguish carefully: birational rigidity means no non‑isomorphic Mori fibre space models; superrigidity adds that Bir(X) = Aut(X). The quartic threefold is superrigid; a generic cubic threefold is rigid but not superrigid (it has a birational involution from the projection from a line). Remember the Corti inequality mult_x ℳ ≤ m/2 for a general point on a rigid Fano threefold of index one — it is the key estimate that rules out maximal singularities at general points.

The Minimal Model Program in Positive Characteristic

Running the Minimal Model Program over fields of positive characteristic p > 0 requires replacing the Kodaira vanishing theorem and Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing — which fail in general — with techniques based on the Frobenius morphism. The central objects are F‑singularities: a pair (X, Δ) is F‑pure if the map O_X → F_* O_X(⌈(p−1)Δ⌉) splits, strongly F‑regular if this holds locally for all effective divisors, and sharply F‑pure if the splitting is compatible with all subvarieties. Hara (1998) and Takagi (2004) showed that for ℚ‑Gorenstein pairs, klt ⇒ strongly F‑regular ⇒ F‑pure ⇒ lc, with the reverse implications holding under mild conditions (e.g. F‑finite and Δ = 0). The cone theorem for threefolds over perfect fields of characteristic p > 5 was proved by Hacon–Witaszek (2014) using the Frobenius trace map and the theory of test ideals; they established the existence of K_X + Δ-negative extremal rays and their contractions. Birkar–Waldron (2018) completed the threefold MMP for klt pairs over perfect fields of characteristic p > 5, proving the existence of flips by reducing to the characteristic zero case via ultraproducts and the Lefschetz principle for F‑singularities. In characteristic p ≤ 5, the situation is more delicate: the classification of terminal singularities is incomplete, and the existence of flips for threefolds remains open. For higher dimensions, the only general results are the cone theorem for F‑regular pairs (Schwede–Smith 2010) and the basepoint‑free theorem for globally F‑regular varieties (Hara 2005). A major obstacle is the failure of Kodaira vanishing even for smooth varieties (Raynaud 1978), which prevents the standard proof of the basepoint‑free theorem. Recent work of Das–McKernan (2022) uses Frobenius‑stable maps to prove a version of the non‑vanishing theorem for F‑pure pairs in all dimensions, assuming the ACC for F‑pure thresholds. The abundance conjecture in positive characteristic is wide open beyond dimension three.

Log Canonical Thresholds and the ACC Conjecture

The log canonical threshold (lct) of an ideal 𝔞 ⊂ O_X on a klt pair (X, Δ) is defined as lct(X, Δ; 𝔞) = sup{c ≥ 0 | (X, Δ + c·𝔞) is log canonical}. For a single divisor D, this reduces to the classical lct(X, Δ; D). The set of all log canonical thresholds on smooth varieties of fixed dimension satisfies the Ascending Chain Condition (ACC), proved by de Fernex–Ein–Mustaţă (2004) using the theory of multiplier ideals and the subadditivity theorem. Kollár (2008) extended this to the set of lct's of log canonical pairs with coefficients in a fixed DCC set, proving the ACC for log canonical thresholds in full generality. The ACC for lct is equivalent to the ACC for minimal log discrepancies (mld) and implies the termination of flips for klt pairs via the Shokurov–Kollár termination argument: each flip increases the minimal log discrepancy of the pair, and the ACC prevents an infinite strictly increasing sequence. The global log canonical threshold lct(X, Δ) = inf_D lct(X, Δ; D) where D runs over all effective ℚ‑divisors with D ∼_ℚ −(K_X + Δ), is a key invariant in K‑stability: Tian's α‑invariant α(X) = lct(X, 0) for a Fano variety satisfies α(X) > dim X/(dim X + 1) ⇒ X is K‑stable (Tian 1987). The ACC for lct also implies the boundedness of complements (Shokurov 2000, Birkar 2019): for a fixed dimension d and a DCC set of coefficients Φ, there exists a positive integer N depending only on d and Φ such that every lc pair (X, Δ) with coefficients in Φ has an N‑complement. This finiteness result is a cornerstone of the boundedness of Fano varieties (Birkar 2018) and the finite generation of the Cox ring for Mori dream spaces.

The Minimal Model Program for Calabi–Yau and Hyperkähler Varieties

For varieties with K_X ∼_ℚ 0, the Minimal Model Program takes a special form: every minimal model is a good minimal model (i.e. K_X is semi‑ample), and the MMP reduces to a sequence of flops — isomorphisms in codimension one that preserve the canonical class. A Calabi–Yau variety is a normal projective variety with K_X ∼ 0 and h^i(X, O_X) = 0 for 0 < i < dim X; a hyperkähler variety (or irreducible holomorphic symplectic variety) is a simply connected Calabi–Yau variety carrying a non‑degenerate holomorphic two‑form σ ∈ H0(X, Ω²_X). The birational geometry of hyperkähler varieties is governed by the Beauville–Bogomolov–Fujiki quadratic form q_X on H²(X, ℤ), which has signature (3, b₂−3). The movable cone Mov(X) is the convex hull of the classes of movable divisors, and its walls correspond to Mukai flops along Lagrangian subvarieties. Huybrechts (1999) proved that the birational Kähler cone BK(X) — the union of the Kähler cones of all hyperkähler varieties birational to X — is a fundamental domain for the action of the monodromy group Mon²(X) on the positive cone. Kawamata (2002) conjectured that every flop X ⇢ X⁺ of hyperkähler varieties induces an equivalence D^b(Coh X) ≅ D^b(Coh X⁺) via a Fourier–Mukai kernel supported on the fibre product; this was proved for threefolds by Bridgeland (2002) and for higher dimensional flops by Chen–Kawamata (2015) using perverse coherent sheaves. For Calabi–Yau threefolds, the MMP is equivalent to the wall‑crossing in the space of Bridgeland stability conditions on D^b(Coh X) (Toda 2007, Bayer–Macrì 2014). The Kawamata–Morrison cone conjecture predicts that the action of the automorphism group Aut(X) on the nef cone has a rational polyhedral fundamental domain; this is known for hyperkähler varieties (Sterk 1985, Amerik–Verbistky 2015) and for Calabi–Yau threefolds with ρ(X) = 2 (Wilson 1992). The derived category viewpoint provides a powerful framework for tracking the sequence of flops in the MMP: as the central charge crosses a wall in Stab(X), the heart of the stability condition tilts, and the new heart corresponds to the derived category of the flopped variety.

  • Confusing the MMP for foliations with the classical MMP; the saturation step after contraction can change the rank of the foliation, which has no analogue in the classical setting.
  • Assuming birational rigidity implies superrigidity; the generic cubic threefold is rigid but admits a birational involution (projection from a line), so it is not superrigid.
  • Applying Kodaira vanishing in positive characteristic; it fails even for smooth varieties (Raynaud's counterexample), and one must use Frobenius‑based techniques (F‑singularities, test ideals) instead.
  • Treating the ACC for log canonical thresholds as equivalent to the ACC for minimal log discrepancies without the klt hypothesis; the equivalence holds for klt pairs but not for general lc pairs.
  • Believing every flop of Calabi–Yau varieties induces a derived equivalence; the Fourier–Mukai kernel existence is proved for threefolds and certain higher dimensional flops, but not in full generality.
  • Overlooking the distinction between F‑pure and strongly F‑regular singularities in positive characteristic; the latter implies the former, but the converse requires the pair to be ℚ‑Gorenstein and F‑finite.
  • Assuming the Kawamata–Morrison cone conjecture is proved for all Calabi–Yau threefolds; it is known only for ρ = 2 and for hyperkähler varieties.
The advanced sections above complete the required material for this lesson.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein Varieties

When the canonical divisor KX fails to be ℚ‑Cartier, the classical Minimal Model Program cannot be applied directly because the adjunction formula and the definition of discrepancies break down. The modern theory of generalized pairs (Birkar–Zhang 2016) replaces a single variety by a pair (X, B + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a morphism to a variety with ℚ‑Cartier canonical class. In this setting one can define generalized log discrepancies and run an MMP whose steps are generalized flips. Birkar (2016) proved the existence of such flips for generalized klt pairs in all dimensions, using a reduction to the ℚ‑Gorenstein case via a suitable dlt modification and the finite generation of the associated adjoint rings. The key technical tool is the canonical bundle formula for a generalized lc trivial fibration f : (X, B + M) → Z, which expresses KX + B + M as the pullback of a generalized divisor on Z plus a boundary coming from the discriminant. This formula allows one to control the behavior of the generalized canonical class under birational maps and to prove termination of generalized flips by adapting the Shokurov–Kollár ACC argument to the generalized minimal log discrepancy. Applications include the construction of minimal models for varieties with non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein singularities that appear naturally in the compactification of moduli spaces of polarized varieties (e.g. the moduli of stable surfaces with KX not ℚ‑Cartier).

Equivariant Minimal Model Program and Group Actions

Let G be a finite group acting regularly on a normal projective variety X. The G‑equivariant MMP seeks a G‑minimal model or a G‑Mori fibre space through a sequence of G‑equivariant divisorial contractions and G‑equivariant flips. Kawamata (1997) established the equivariant cone theorem: the G‑invariant part of the Mori cone NE(X)G is generated by G‑invariant extremal rays that can be contracted by a G‑equivariant morphism. The contraction theorem extends verbatim because the contraction morphism can be chosen G‑equivariant by averaging a relatively ample line bundle over the group. The existence of G‑equivariant flips was proved by Birkar (2010) using the equivariant finite generation of the associated section rings: one replaces the usual flip construction by a G‑linearized Proj of the G‑invariant part of the graded ring. A crucial point is that the G‑action on the flipped variety is induced by the natural action on the section ring, guaranteeing that the flip is G‑equivariant. The program terminates for G‑klt pairs because each G‑equivariant flip increases the G‑invariant minimal log discrepancy, and the ACC for G‑invariant log discrepancies holds (Birkar 2012). Equivariant minimal models are essential for constructing G‑equivariant compactifications of moduli spaces, for instance the G‑Hilbert scheme of points on a surface, and for studying the birational geometry of quotients X/G via the Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing for G‑linearized line bundles.

Equivariant Flip Construction
Let G = ℤ/2 act on a terminal ℚ‑factorial threefold X by an involution that fixes a curve C pointwise. Suppose an extremal ray R ⊂ NE(X) is G‑invariant and its contraction f : X → Y contracts a divisor E to a point. Explain why the flip of f exists as a G‑equivariant morphism and describe the induced G‑action on the flipped variety X⁺.
The contraction f is G‑equivariant because the ray R is G‑invariant and the relatively ample line bundle used to define f can be averaged over G. The flip is constructed as X⁺ = ProjY ⊕m≥0 f* OX(mKX). Since KX is G‑linearized (the action lifts to the canonical sheaf), each graded piece f* OX(mKX) carries a natural G‑action, and the Proj construction inherits a G‑action making the morphism X⁺ → Y G‑equivariant. The birational map X ⇢ X⁺ is then G‑equivariant because it is induced by the inclusion of the G‑invariant section rings. The fixed curve C maps to a curve C⁺ ⊂ X⁺ which is also pointwise fixed, and the exceptional divisor of the flip is a G‑invariant divisor exchanged with its counterpart on X.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for an equivariant MMP step, first verify that the extremal ray is G‑invariant. Then recall that the contraction theorem and flip existence both have G‑equivariant versions proved by averaging line bundles and using G‑invariant section rings. Mention the ACC for G‑invariant log discrepancies to justify termination.

The Minimal Model Program for Kähler Manifolds and Non‑Projective Varieties

The analytic category admits a parallel MMP for compact Kähler manifolds, initiated by Campana and Peternell (1991). The Kähler cone K(X) replaces the ample cone, and the movable cone Mov(X) is defined using divisors that move in families covering X. The Kähler cone theorem states that the Kähler Mori cone NEK(X) is generated by countably many Kähler extremal rays with KX-negative intersection, each of which can be contracted by a Kähler morphism (a proper surjective holomorphic map with connected fibres that is a submersion on a Zariski open set). For threefolds, the existence of Kähler flips was proved by Höring and Peternell (2016) using the algebraic approximation of Kähler threefolds by projective models and the algebraic flip theorem. The abundance conjecture for Kähler Calabi–Yau manifolds (KX ≡ 0) asserts that KX is torsion; this is known for Kähler threefolds by the work of Campana–Peternell and for higher dimensions under the additional assumption of a Kähler Ricci‑flat metric (Yau’s theorem). A major open problem is the termination of Kähler flips in dimension ≥ 4; the algebraic proof via ACC for log discrepancies does not directly transfer because the analytic minimal log discrepancy is not known to satisfy ACC. Nevertheless, the Kähler MMP provides a framework for studying the birational geometry of non‑projective hyperkähler manifolds and of compactifications of moduli spaces that are naturally Kähler but not projective.

Valuative Criteria for K‑Stability and the Minimal Model Program

K‑stability of a Fano variety X can be tested by a valuative criterion due to Fujita (2019) and Li (2020). For a prime divisor E over X (i.e. a valuation with centre on X), define the beta invariant β(E) = AX(E) − SX(E), where AX(E) is the log discrepancy and SX(E) is the expected vanishing order. The criterion states that X is K‑semistable iff β(E) ≥ 0 for all divisors E over X, and K‑stable iff β(E) > 0 for all non‑trivial E. This bridges the MMP and K‑stability: a K‑polystable degeneration of X corresponds to a log Fano pair (X₀, Δ₀) that is a minimal model in the sense of the log MMP. The delta invariant δ(X) = infE AX(E)/SX(E) (Fujita–Odaka 2018) satisfies δ(X) > 1 ⇒ X is K‑stable, and δ(X) = 1 characterises the existence of a K‑semistable but not K‑stable degeneration. The MMP for log Fano pairs produces a sequence of K‑equivariant flips that terminates at a K‑polystable model, providing a geometric construction of the K‑moduli space of Fano varieties. Recent work by Blum–Liu–Xu (2021) shows that the minimizer of the normalized volume function on the space of valuations coincides with the unique K‑polystable degeneration, giving a variational interpretation of the MMP steps as a gradient flow on the space of valuations.

The Minimal Model Program in Dimension Four: Abundance and Termination

Dimension four is the frontier where many foundational results of the MMP are known but several conjectures remain open. The existence of minimal models for varieties of general type in dimension four was proved by Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) as part of the general inductive proof. For klt pairs with KX + Δ nef, the abundance conjecture (semi‑ampleness of KX + Δ) is a theorem in dimension four (Hacon–McKernan 2010, using the non‑vanishing theorem for fourfolds and the canonical bundle formula for Iitaka fibrations). The termination of log flips for fourfolds was established by Kawamata (1992) for terminal flips and extended to klt flips by Shokurov (2003) via the ACC for log canonical thresholds in dimension four. A subtle point is that the minimal log discrepancy in dimension four can accumulate at 1, so the ACC argument requires the DCC for log canonical thresholds which holds by the work of de Fernex–Ein–Mustaţă (2004). The finite generation of the Cox ring for Mori dream spaces of dimension four follows from the existence of a MMP with scaling and the polyhedrality of the movable cone (Hu–Keel 2000). Open problems include the abundance for lc pairs in dimension four, the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial fourfolds, and the boundedness of ε‑log canonical Fano fourfolds which is a stepping stone toward the full boundedness of Fano varieties in dimension four (Birkar 2018 proved boundedness for all dimensions, but effective bounds in dimension four are still under investigation).

  • Assuming the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein varieties follows automatically from the ℚ‑Gorenstein case; the generalized pair formalism is essential and the finite generation of adjoint rings is a deep separate theorem.
  • Confusing G‑equivariant flips with ordinary flips; the G‑action must lift to the section ring, and the flip is constructed as a G‑invariant Proj, not merely by taking the ordinary flip and then averaging.
  • Believing the Kähler MMP is a direct translation of the projective MMP; the lack of an algebraic approximation in dimension ≥ 4 and the failure of Kodaira vanishing on non‑projective Kähler manifolds create genuine analytic obstacles.
  • Treating the beta invariant β(E) as the same as the log discrepancy AX(E); β(E) subtracts the expected vanishing order SX(E), and the valuative criterion requires β(E) ≥ 0, not AX(E) ≥ 0.
  • Thinking abundance for fourfolds is proved for all lc pairs; it is known only for klt pairs, and the lc case remains a major open problem.
  • Assuming termination of flips in dimension four implies termination in all higher dimensions; the inductive proofs use dimension‑specific ACC statements that do not automatically lift.
  • Overlooking the role of the canonical bundle formula for generalized pairs when running the MMP for non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein varieties; without it one cannot control the discriminant and the moduli part.

These sections provide the depth required for advanced examinations in birational geometry.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein Varieties

The classical Minimal Model Program assumes that the variety is ℚ‑factorial and that the canonical divisor is ℚ‑Cartier. When either condition fails, the standard machinery of extremal rays and contractions must be replaced by the theory of generalized pairs. A generalized pair consists of a normal variety X, an effective ℚ‑divisor Δ on X, and a ℚ‑Cartier divisor M on a birational model X′ that pushes forward to a pseudo‑effective divisor on X. The log canonical divisor of the pair is K_X + Δ + M_X, where M_X is the trace of M on X. This formalism, developed in the work of Birkar and Zhang, allows one to run the MMP for varieties whose canonical class is not ℚ‑Cartier and which may have non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities.

The key steps are: (1) replace the usual cone theorem by a cone theorem for generalized pairs, where the extremal rays are generated by curves C with (K_X + Δ + M_X)·C < 0; (2) construct contractions of such rays using the basepoint‑free theorem for the adjoint divisor K_X + Δ + M_X; (3) when a contraction is small, perform a flip in the category of generalized pairs. The existence of flips for generalized pairs follows from the finite generation of the adjoint ring ⊕m≥0 H0(X, ⌊m(K_X + Δ + M_X)⌋), a result proved by Birkar using the inductive method of Shokurov. The termination of flips in this setting is reduced to the ACC for log canonical thresholds of generalized pairs, which holds by the work of de Fernex, Ein, and Mustaţă.

An important subtlety is that the moduli part M_X may acquire non‑trivial horizontal components after a flip. The canonical bundle formula for generalized pairs, established by Birkar and Chen, controls the behavior of M_X under birational maps and ensures that the moduli part remains nef on the base of the Iitaka fibration. This control is essential for proving the abundance conjecture for generalized pairs of non‑negative Kodaira dimension. In practice, one often reduces to the case where M is the pullback of a nef divisor from a lower‑dimensional variety, and then applies the relative MMP over that base.

Applications include the construction of minimal models for non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein Fano varieties, the study of degenerations of Calabi–Yau varieties with non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities, and the extension of the boundedness theorem for Fano varieties to the non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein setting. The theory also provides a framework for the MMP of varieties with non‑ℚ‑factorial terminal singularities, which appear naturally in the classification of threefold singularities.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Terminal Singularities

Terminal singularities are the mildest singularities that appear in the minimal model program for varieties of general type. In the ℚ‑factorial case, the MMP proceeds by contracting K_X‑negative extremal rays and flipping the resulting small contractions. When X is not ℚ‑factorial, the cone of curves may contain extremal rays that are not generated by curves with negative intersection against K_X, because K_X is not ℚ‑Cartier. The solution is to work with a ℚ‑factorialization: a small birational morphism f: Y → X where Y is ℚ‑factorial and f is an isomorphism in codimension one. The terminal singularities of X pull back to terminal singularities on Y, and the MMP can be run on Y. The challenge is to descend the resulting minimal model or Mori fibre space back to X.

Descent is possible when the MMP on Y is f‑equivariant, i.e. each step is a morphism over X. This requires that the extremal rays contracted on Y are f‑negative. The existence of such an f‑equivariant MMP for terminal threefolds was proved by Mori and Kollár using the theory of flops. In higher dimensions, the existence of ℚ‑factorializations and the termination of f‑equivariant flips are known for varieties with terminal singularities under the assumption of the general termination conjecture. The finite generation of the Cox ring of a terminal variety, which is equivalent to the Mori dream space property, has been established for ℚ‑factorial terminal varieties by Hu and Keel; the non‑ℚ‑factorial case follows by passing to a ℚ‑factorialization and using the fact that the Cox ring is invariant under small birational maps.

A concrete example is the MMP for a terminal threefold X with a single cDV singularity. A ℚ‑factorialization Y is obtained by a small resolution of the singularity. The MMP on Y consists of a sequence of flops and divisorial contractions that are all isomorphisms over the smooth locus of X. The final model Y_min is either a minimal model with KY_min nef or a Mori fibre space. Because the flops are isomorphisms in codimension one, they descend to a sequence of flops on X, yielding a minimal model X_min with KX_min nef. This process shows that the minimal model of a terminal variety is unique up to flops, even in the non‑ℚ‑factorial setting.

The main open problem is the termination of f‑equivariant flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial terminal varieties in dimension ≥ 4. The difficulty lies in controlling the discrepancies of the exceptional divisors that appear during the MMP on Y. Recent progress uses the theory of b‑divisors and the ACC for minimal log discrepancies to bound the number of flips.

Technique: Descending an f‑equivariant MMP
Let X be a terminal threefold with a single cDV singularity, and let f: Y → X be a ℚ‑factorialization obtained by a small resolution. Suppose the MMP on Y consists of a flop φ: Y ⇢ Y₁ followed by a divisorial contraction g: Y₁ → Z. Explain why φ descends to a flop on X and why the composition g ∘ f⁻¹ is a morphism on X.
Because f is an isomorphism in codimension one, the flop φ contracts a curve C ⊂ Y that is not contained in the exceptional locus of f. Its image f(C) is a curve in X, and the strict transform of f(C) on Y₁ is the flopped curve C⁺. The map φ is an isomorphism outside C ∪ C⁺, hence it induces an isomorphism X ⇢ X₁ outside f(C) ∪ f(C⁺). Since X has terminal singularities, any small birational map between terminal varieties is a flop. Therefore φ descends to a flop on X. The divisorial contraction g contracts a divisor E ⊂ Y₁ that is f₁‑negative. Because f₁ is an isomorphism in codimension one, the image f₁(E) is a divisor on X₁, and g descends to a divisorial contraction on X₁. The composition g ∘ f⁻¹ is therefore a morphism X₁ → Z that contracts the divisor f₁(E).
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks about the MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial varieties, first identify whether the singularities are terminal, canonical, or log canonical. For terminal singularities, the standard approach is to take a ℚ‑factorialization, run an equivariant MMP, and then descend. Remember that flops are isomorphisms in codimension one and therefore always descend. Divisorial contractions descend only if the contracted divisor is not exceptional for the ℚ‑factorialization. Practice drawing the commutative diagram of the ℚ‑factorialization, the MMP steps, and the descended maps.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Canonical Singularities

Canonical singularities are more general than terminal singularities and appear naturally as the singularities of minimal models of varieties of general type. In the ℚ‑factorial case, the MMP for canonical pairs (X, Δ) is well‑established: one runs the (K_X + Δ)‑MMP, which terminates with a minimal model where K_X + Δ is nef. For non‑ℚ‑factorial canonical singularities, the same strategy of ℚ‑factorialization works, but the descent of divisorial contractions is more delicate because the exceptional divisor of the ℚ‑factorialization may have discrepancy zero. This means that the pullback of K_X + Δ to the ℚ‑factorialization Y is K_Y + Δ_Y + E, where E is an effective f‑exceptional divisor with coefficient zero in the discrepancy formula. The presence of E can create new extremal rays that are not f‑negative, obstructing an f‑equivariant MMP.

To overcome this, one uses the theory of generalized pairs. The pair (Y, Δ_Y + E) together with the moduli part M = 0 forms a generalized pair whose log canonical divisor is K_Y + Δ_Y + E. The cone theorem for generalized pairs guarantees that any (K_Y + Δ_Y + E)‑negative extremal ray is generated by a curve C with (K_Y + Δ_Y + E)·C < 0. Since E is f‑exceptional, such a ray is automatically f‑negative, allowing an f‑equivariant MMP. The flips and divisorial contractions in this MMP are performed in the category of generalized pairs, and the finite generation of the adjoint ring for generalized pairs ensures the existence of flips. After the MMP terminates, the resulting model Y_min has KY_min + ΔY_min + E_min nef. Because E_min is f_min‑exceptional and has coefficient zero, the pushforward to X_min yields a minimal model with KX_min + ΔX_min nef.

An important application is the proof of the existence of minimal models for canonical pairs in dimension four, which was a key step in the inductive proof of the general existence theorem by Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, and McKernan. The argument uses the fact that the non‑ℚ‑factorial case can be reduced to the ℚ‑factorial case by taking a ℚ‑factorialization and running the generalized pair MMP. The termination of flips for canonical pairs in dimension four follows from the ACC for log canonical thresholds of canonical pairs, which is a consequence of the work of Kawamata and Shokurov.

Open questions include the termination of the generalized pair MMP for canonical pairs in dimension ≥ 5, and the boundedness of the set of minimal models for a fixed canonical pair. The latter is related to the finite generation of the Cox ring for canonical Mori dream spaces, which is known in the ℚ‑factorial case but remains open for non‑ℚ‑factorial canonical varieties.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities

Log canonical singularities are the most general class of singularities for which the MMP is expected to work. The log minimal model program for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs (X, Δ) was developed by Ambro, Fujino, and others, using the theory of dlt modifications and the adjunction formula for log canonical pairs. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, the situation is more complicated because a ℚ‑factorialization may not exist in the category of log canonical pairs: a small resolution of a log canonical singularity can introduce divisors with discrepancy < −1, destroying the log canonical property. Instead, one works with a dlt blow‑up, which is a birational morphism f: Y → X where Y is ℚ‑factorial and the pair (Y, Δ_Y) is dlt (divisorially log terminal). The dlt blow‑up exists for any log canonical pair and is unique up to isomorphism in codimension one.

The MMP for the dlt pair (Y, Δ_Y) is then run with scaling of an ample divisor. The steps of this MMP are (K_Y + Δ_Y)‑negative extremal contractions. Because (Y, Δ_Y) is dlt, the cone theorem and contraction theorem hold, and flips exist by the work of Birkar on the existence of flips for dlt pairs. The termination of flips for dlt pairs in dimension ≤ 4 is known; in higher dimensions it is a major open problem. The MMP on Y is not automatically f‑equivariant, but one can choose the scaling divisor carefully to ensure that the contracted rays are f‑negative. This is achieved by using the fact that the relative Picard number ρ(Y/X) is finite and that the MMP with scaling terminates after finitely many steps.

After the MMP on Y terminates, one obtains a minimal model Y_min or a Mori fibre space. The final step is to descend this model to X. If the MMP produced a minimal model Y_min with KY_min + ΔY_min nef, then the pushforward X_min is a log canonical model of (X, Δ). If the MMP produced a Mori fibre space, the descent yields a log canonical Mori fibre space structure on X. The uniqueness of the log canonical model up to flops follows from the fact that any two dlt blow‑ups are connected by a sequence of flops that are isomorphisms in codimension one.
