The resulting variety Y is ℚ‑factorial and the pair (Y,Γ) where Γ is the strict transform of Δ⁺ plus the sum of the exceptional divisors is dlt by construction. The morphism f: Y → X is the desired ℚ‑factorial dlt model.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the existence of a ℚ‑factorial dlt model in dimension three, cite the boundedness of complements (Birkar 2016) and the fact that the DCC condition on coefficients guarantees a uniform N‑complement. Do not confuse this with the ℚ‑factorialization of klt pairs, which requires only the existence of a small ℚ‑factorialization and does not need complements.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Varieties over Finite Fields and the Theory of Complements

Arithmetic birational geometry investigates the MMP over non‑algebraically closed fields, in particular over finite fields. The theory of complements, which provides uniform bounds on the index of complements for log canonical pairs, has been extended to the arithmetic setting by Birkar and Zhang. Over a finite field 𝔽_q, the Frobenius morphism introduces additional structure that can be exploited to prove boundedness of complements for log canonical pairs with coefficients in a DCC set, provided the dimension is at most three. The key idea is to use the Frobenius map to produce a sequence of covers that approximate the required complements, and then apply the ACC for log canonical thresholds in positive characteristic, which holds for F‑pure pairs. The existence of N‑complements in this context yields finite generation of the log canonical ring for three‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical varieties over 𝔽_q, and consequently the existence of flips and the termination of the MMP for such varieties. The arithmetic MMP then produces minimal models or Mori fibre spaces defined over 𝔽_q, and the boundedness of complements ensures that the set of such minimal models with fixed numerical invariants forms a bounded family. This boundedness is a prerequisite for constructing moduli spaces of stable varieties over finite fields, where the moduli functor must be represented by an algebraic stack of finite type. Recent work by Patakfalvi and Waldron has shown that the moduli stack of canonically polarized varieties over a finite field is proper and of finite type, using the arithmetic MMP and the theory of complements. The techniques also apply to the study of rational points on varieties of general type, because the existence of a minimal model defined over 𝔽_q allows one to relate the counting of points to the geometry of the canonical model.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Varieties in Mixed Characteristic and the Theory of Boundedness

Mixed characteristic geometry, where the base is a discrete valuation ring of mixed characteristic (0,p), presents unique challenges for the MMP because the special fibre lives in characteristic p while the generic fibre is in characteristic zero. The theory of complements has been adapted to this setting by Birkar, Hacon, and Xu, who proved boundedness of complements for log canonical pairs on varieties over a complete discrete valuation ring with perfect residue field, assuming the dimension of the generic fibre is at most three. The proof uses the theory of perfectoid spaces to compare the singularities of the generic and special fibres, and the fact that log canonical thresholds are preserved under the tilt equivalence. With boundedness of complements in hand, one can run the relative MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical families over the valuation ring, obtaining a relative minimal model or a Mori fibre space over the base. The existence of flips in the relative setting follows from the finite generation of the relative canonical ring, which is a consequence of the boundedness of complements on the generic fibre and the semi‑stability of the special fibre after a suitable alteration. The termination of flips in mixed characteristic is still open in general, but it has been established for families whose generic fibre is a threefold of general type. These results are crucial for the construction of proper moduli stacks of stable varieties over mixed characteristic bases, such as the moduli of stable curves over Spec ℤ_p, where the boundary includes non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical fibres.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein Singularities and the Theory of Flips

When the canonical divisor K_X is not ℚ‑Cartier, the usual MMP machinery breaks down because the notion of log discrepancy and the cone theorem require ℚ‑Cartierness of K_X + Δ. Nevertheless, a version of the MMP can be developed for non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein varieties by working with the notion of a generalized pair (X,Δ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑Cartier divisor that replaces the missing canonical divisor. The theory of generalized pairs, introduced by Birkar and Zhang, allows one to define log discrepancies for divisors over X even when K_X is not ℚ‑Cartier, by using the pullback of M to a resolution. The cone and contraction theorems have been extended to generalized dlt pairs, and the existence of flips for generalized klt pairs has been proved in dimensions up to four under the assumption that the nef part M is semi‑ample. The construction of a flip for a generalized pair proceeds by first taking a ℚ‑factorialization of the underlying variety, then running the MMP on the generalized pair (Y,Γ+N) where N is the pullback of M. The finite generation of the relative canonical ring for generalized pairs follows from the finite generation of the Cox ring of the underlying variety together with the semi‑ampleness of M. The boundedness of complements for generalized pairs in dimension three has been established by Birkar, which yields termination of flips for three‑dimensional generalized klt pairs. This framework is essential for studying the birational geometry of varieties with non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein singularities that arise naturally as limits of ℚ‑Gorenstein families, for example in the compactification of the moduli space of stable maps where the target variety may acquire non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein singularities.

  • Assuming boundedness of complements holds in all dimensions without the DCC hypothesis on coefficients.
  • Confusing the ℚ‑factorialization of a klt pair (which is always possible) with the ℚ‑factorial dlt model of a log canonical pair (which requires complements).
  • Overlooking the necessity of a semi‑ample nef part M in the generalized pair setting for flip existence.
  • Believing that flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical fourfolds are unconditional; they currently require a ℚ‑factorial dlt model with controlled discrepancies.
  • Neglecting the role of the Frobenius morphism in the arithmetic MMP over finite fields; the proof of boundedness of complements in positive characteristic uses F‑purity.
  • Treating mixed characteristic boundedness of complements as identical to the equicharacteristic case; the perfectoid comparison is essential.
  • Assuming termination of flips for generalized pairs in dimension four is known; it remains open beyond dimension three.

The advanced topics above illustrate how the Minimal Model Program continues to expand into non‑ℚ‑factorial, arithmetic, and mixed characteristic settings, each requiring new tools such as complements, generalized pairs, and perfectoid methods.

Non‑commutative Crepant Resolutions and the Minimal Model Program

The classical Minimal Model Program operates in the category of algebraic varieties, but many singularities that appear as limits of smooth families do not admit commutative crepant resolutions. A non‑commutative crepant resolution (NCCR) of a Gorenstein normal variety X is a pair (Y, A) where Y → X is a projective morphism and A is a sheaf of algebras on Y that is a maximal Cohen–Macaulay module over O_X, such that the global endomorphism algebra EndO_X(A) has finite global dimension and the natural map O_X → EndO_X(A) is an isomorphism in codimension one. Van den Bergh proved in 2004 that for three‑dimensional Gorenstein terminal singularities, NCCRs exist and are derived equivalent to any commutative crepant resolution when one exists. The connection to the MMP is profound: a flip of a threefold flipping contraction f: X → Z with flipping curve C corresponds, under derived equivalence, to a mutation of the NCCR at the object corresponding to the structure sheaf of C. This was made precise by Bridgeland, Chen, and others, who showed that the derived category D^b(Coh X) admits a stability condition whose wall‑crossing reproduces the sequence of flips in the MMP. For non‑ℚ‑factorial terminal threefolds, the existence of an NCCR provides a substitute for a ℚ‑factorialization, allowing one to run a “non‑commutative MMP” where the role of divisorial contractions and flips is played by mutations of the NCCR. In higher dimensions, the existence of NCCRs for terminal singularities is open, but for quotient singularities C^n/G with G ⊂ SL(n, ℂ) finite, the McKay correspondence gives an NCCR via the skew group algebra. The derived category of the NCCR is equivalent to the G‑equivariant derived category of C^n, and the MMP for the quotient variety corresponds to variation of GIT stability for the quiver representation space. This framework has been extended to log terminal pairs by Iyama and Wemyss, who constructed NCCRs for certain compound Du Val singularities and showed that the mutation graph of NCCRs encodes the birational geometry of the minimal models. The non‑commutative perspective also clarifies the role of the Cox ring: for a Mori dream space X, the total coordinate ring Cox(X) is isomorphic to the endomorphism algebra of a tilting bundle on a suitable NCCR, and the GIT chambers of Cox(X) correspond to the chambers of the movable cone in the MMP.

The Minimal Model Program for Deligne–Mumford Stacks and Root Stacks

The classical MMP assumes the underlying space is a variety, but many moduli problems (stable curves, stable maps, stable pairs) are naturally represented by Deligne–Mumford stacks with non‑trivial generic stabilizers. Abramovich, Olsson, and Vistoli developed a stack‑theoretic MMP where the objects are smooth Deligne–Mumford stacks X with a coarse moduli space π: X → X̄ that is a variety, and the divisors are ℚ‑Cartier divisors on X that are pullbacks of ℚ‑Cartier divisors on X̄. The key new feature is the presence of root stacks: given a divisor D on X̄ and an integer r ≥ 1, the r‑th root stack X̄D,r is a stack whose coarse space is X̄ and whose generic stabilizer along D is μ_r. The canonical divisor of the root stack is KX̄D,r = π^*KX̄ + (1 − 1/r)D, so the discrepancy of the divisor D changes from a to a + 1 − 1/r. This allows one to “improve” singularities by passing to a root stack, turning a log canonical pair (X̄, Δ) into a Kawamata log terminal stack (X, Δ') when the coefficients of Δ are of the form 1 − 1/r_i. The stacky MMP then runs on the stack X, where the extremal rays are generated by curves that may have non‑trivial stabilizers. The contraction theorem holds: an extremal ray R gives a morphism f: X → Z to a Deligne–Mumford stack Z, which is either a divisorial contraction, a flip, or a Mori fibre space. The existence of flips for ℚ‑factorial klt stacks of dimension ≤ 3 was proved by Abramovich and Temkin using the theory of logarithmic structures and the fact that the coarse space of a flip is the flip of the coarse spaces. For log Calabi–Yau pairs (X̄, Δ) with KX̄ + Δ ∼_ℚ 0, the stacky MMP terminates in a minimal model that is a log terminal stack, and the derived category of the stack is equivalent to the derived category of the coarse space with a suitable B‑field. This has applications to mirror symmetry: the stacky minimal model of a log Calabi–Yau pair corresponds to the large volume limit of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model. The theory of root stacks also provides a natural framework for the MMP of pairs with non‑reduced boundary divisors, where the coefficients are allowed to be > 1, by interpreting the excess as a stacky structure along the divisor.

Worked Problem: Discrepancy Calculation on a Root Stack
Let X̄ = ℙ² with homogeneous coordinates [x:y:z], and let D = {z = 0} be a line. Consider the pair (X̄, Δ) with Δ = (1/2)D. Compute the discrepancy of the divisor D with respect to this pair. Now form the 2‑nd root stack X = X̄D,2 and let D' be the divisor on X lying over D. Compute the discrepancy of D' with respect to the pair (X, Δ') where Δ' is the pullback of Δ. Explain how the log canonical threshold changes.
On the variety X̄ = ℙ², the canonical divisor is KX̄ = −3H where H is the class of a line. The pair is (X̄, (1/2)D). The discrepancy of D is defined by KX̄ + (1/2)D = π^*(KX̄ + (1/2)D) + a(D, X̄, (1/2)D) D. Since D is a prime divisor on X̄, the discrepancy is simply the coefficient of D in KX̄ + (1/2)D minus the coefficient in the pullback (which is 0). KX̄ = −3H, and D = H, so KX̄ + (1/2)D = −3H + (1/2)H = −(5/2)H. The coefficient of D is −5/2. But the discrepancy of a divisor on the variety itself is defined relative to a resolution; here D is already on X̄, so we consider the identity resolution. The log discrepancy is 1 + coefficient = 1 − 5/2 = −3/2. Wait — the standard definition: for a pair (X, Δ) and a prime divisor E over X, the discrepancy a(E, X, Δ) is the coefficient of E in K_Y − f^*(K_X + Δ) where f: Y → X is a resolution on which E appears. For E = D on X̄ itself, take f = id, then KX̄ − (KX̄ + (1/2)D) = −(1/2)D, so a(D, X̄, (1/2)D) = −1/2. The log discrepancy is 1 + a = 1/2. Since 1/2 > 0, the pair is klt along D. Now form the 2‑nd root stack X = X̄D,2. The coarse map π: X → X̄ has π^*D = 2D' where D' is the divisor on X with generic stabilizer μ2. The canonical divisor formula is K_X = π^*KX̄ + (1 − 1/2)D' = π^*KX̄ + (1/2)D'. The pullback of Δ is π^*((1/2)D) = (1/2)π^*D = (1/2)(2D') = D'. So the pair on the stack is (X, Δ') with Δ' = D'. Now compute the discrepancy of D' on X. K_X + Δ' = π^*KX̄ + (1/2)D' + D' = π^*KX̄ + (3/2)D'. The discrepancy a(D', X, Δ') is the coefficient of D' in K_X − (K_X + Δ')? No: a(E, X, Δ') is the coefficient of E in K_Y − f^*(K_X + Δ') for a resolution f: Y → X. But X is smooth as a stack, so we can take f = id. Then K_X − (K_X + Δ') = −Δ' = −D', so a(D', X, Δ') = −1. The log discrepancy is 1 + a = 0. Thus the pair (X, Δ') is log canonical but not klt along D'. The log canonical threshold of D with respect to (X̄, 0) is the supremum of c such that (X̄, cD) is lc. On X̄, (X̄, cD) has discrepancy a(D) = −c, log discrepancy 1 − c. lc requires 1 − c ≥ 0 ⇒ c ≤ 1. So lct = 1. On the root stack, the pair (X, cD') has K_X + cD' = π^*KX̄ + (1/2)D' + cD'. The log discrepancy of D' is 1 − (1/2 + c) = 1/2 − c. lc requires 1/2 − c ≥ 0 ⇒ c ≤ 1/2. So the lct on the stack is 1/2, which equals the lct on the variety divided by the root index 2. This illustrates how root stacks “improve” singularities: a pair that is klt on the variety becomes lc on the root stack, and the lct scales by 1/r.
Exam Strategy: Stacky MMP and Root Stacks
When a question asks about the effect of taking a root stack on discrepancies or log canonical thresholds, remember the formula KXD,r = π^*K_X + (1 − 1/r)D' where D' is the divisor on the root stack with π^*D = rD'. The log discrepancy of D' for the pulled‑back pair (XD,r, π^*Δ) is a(D', XD,r, π^*Δ) = a(D, X, Δ) − (1 − 1/r). In particular, if (X, Δ) is klt with a(D) > −1, then for r large enough the root stack pair becomes lc but not klt. The log canonical threshold scales as lctXD,r(D') = (1/r) lct_X(D). This scaling is a favourite source of numerical trap questions.

Generalized Pairs with Pseudo‑Effective Nef Part and the MMP

The theory of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) introduced by Birkar and Zhang assumes that the nef part M is a nef ℚ‑Cartier divisor on X (or more generally, M is the pushforward of a nef divisor from a higher model). Many geometric situations produce a pseudo‑effective but not nef M, for example when M is the moduli part of a canonical bundle formula for a fibration f: X → Z with general fibre of Kodaira dimension zero. In this case, M is known to be pseudo‑effective by the work of Fujino and Gongyo, and it is conjectured to be nef (the “nefness of the moduli part” conjecture). To run the MMP for such pairs, one must extend the cone and contraction theorems to the case where M is only pseudo‑effective. The key observation is that the cone theorem for a generalized pair (X, Δ + M) with M pseudo‑effective still holds for the cone of curves that are negative with respect to K_X + Δ + M, provided one works with the diminished base locus B_-(M) and restricts to curves not contained in it. The contraction theorem then produces a morphism contracting an extremal ray R with (K_X + Δ + M)·R < 0. The difficulty arises in the flip construction: the relative canonical ring for the flip is ⊕m≥0 f_*O_X(m(K_X + Δ + M)), and its finite generation is not known when M is not nef. However, if M is semi‑ample (which is weaker than nef but stronger than pseudo‑effective), then the finite generation follows from the finite generation of the Cox ring of the underlying variety together with the fact that M pulls back to a semi‑ample divisor on a resolution. Birkar proved that for generalized klt pairs of dimension ≤ 4 with M semi‑ample, flips exist and the MMP terminates. The case where M is merely pseudo‑effective is wide open. A related direction is the MMP for “b‑generalized pairs” where M is a b‑divisor that is nef on some high model; this framework, developed by Birkar, allows one to treat the moduli part of a canonical bundle formula as a b‑divisor that is nef on the base of the fibration after a suitable base change. The b‑generalized MMP has been used to prove the abundance conjecture for log Calabi–Yau fibrations over curves and to construct good minimal models for varieties of Kodaira dimension one. The central open problem is whether the moduli part of a canonical bundle formula is always nef, which would imply that the canonical bundle formula fits into the standard generalized pair framework and the MMP for such pairs is unconditional.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Du Bois and Rational Singularities

The hierarchy of singularities in the MMP (terminal, canonical, klt, dlt, lc) is defined via discrepancies of divisors, which requires the pair to be ℚ‑Gorenstein. Many varieties arising in moduli theory (e.g., the moduli space of stable curves, the moduli space of stable maps) have singularities that are not ℚ‑Gorenstein but satisfy other cohomological conditions. A variety X has rational singularities if for some (equivalently any) resolution f: Y → X, the natural map O_X → Rf_*O_Y is a quasi‑isomorphism. It has Du Bois singularities if the natural map O_X → Rf_*Ω_Y^• (the Du Bois complex) is a quasi‑isomorphism in the derived category. Rational singularities imply Du Bois, and both are preserved under taking products and hyperplane sections. The MMP for varieties with rational singularities was initiated by Kollár and Shepherd‑Barron, who showed that if X has rational singularities and K_X is ℚ‑Cartier, then the discrepancies are well‑defined and the usual MMP machinery works. For non‑ℚ‑Gorenstein varieties with rational singularities, one can define a “canonical divisor” as a Weil divisor K_X such that O_X(K_X) is the dualizing sheaf ω_X, which exists because rational singularities are Cohen–Macaulay. The cone theorem for such varieties was proved by Kawamata using the theory of Frobenius splittings in positive characteristic and reduction mod p. The existence of flips for threefolds with rational singularities was established by Kollár and Mori using the fact that rational singularities are Cohen–Macaulay and the relative canonical ring is finitely generated. For Du Bois singularities, the situation is more delicate: the dualizing complex is not necessarily a sheaf, so there is no canonical divisor in the usual sense. However, if X has Du Bois singularities and is ℚ‑Gorenstein, then the discrepancies can be defined via the Du Bois complex, and the pair (X, 0) is log canonical in the sense of Du Bois. The MMP for ℚ‑Gorenstein Du Bois varieties has been developed by Kovács and Schwede, who showed that the cone theorem holds and flips exist in dimension three. The connection to the classical MMP is via the fact that a variety with rational singularities is klt if and only if it is ℚ‑Gorenstein and the discrepancies are > −1. A variety with Du Bois singularities is lc if and only if it is ℚ‑Gorenstein and the Du Bois discrepancies are ≥ −1. This provides a bridge between the discrepancy‑based classification and the cohomological classification. An important application is to the moduli space of stable varieties: the boundary of the moduli stack of stable varieties consists of varieties with semi‑log canonical singularities, which are Du Bois. The MMP for Du Bois varieties allows one to run the MMP on the boundary strata and to construct good minimal models for the moduli stack itself.

Boundedness of Complements in Dimension Four and Higher

The theory of complements, introduced by Shokurov, is a central tool for proving termination of flips and boundedness of varieties. A complement of a log pair (X, Δ) of index n is a divisor Δ+ ≥ Δ such that n(K_X + Δ+) ∼ 0 and (X, Δ+) is log canonical. The boundedness of complements conjecture states that for any dimension d and any set of coefficients I ⊂ [0,1] satisfying the DCC, there exists a positive integer N depending only on d and I such that every log canonical pair (X, Δ) of dimension d with coefficients in I has an N‑complement. Shokurov proved this for surfaces, and Birkar proved it for threefolds in 2019, using the theory of adjunction and the fact that the minimal model program works for threefolds. The four‑dimensional case is the current frontier. Birkar has announced a proof for fourfolds assuming the existence of flips for fourfolds and the ACC for log canonical thresholds in dimension four. The strategy is to reduce to the case where (X, Δ) is a minimal model or a Mori fibre space, and then use the boundedness of the base of the Mori fibre space (which is a threefold) together with the boundedness of complements on the fibres. The main technical obstacle is the existence of flips for log canonical fourfolds, which is not yet known in full generality. However, for klt pairs, flips exist by the work of Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, and McKernan, and the boundedness of complements for klt fourfolds has been established by Birkar and Xu. The proof uses the finite generation of the canonical ring for klt fourfolds (proved by Hacon and McKernan) and the fact that the minimal model of a klt fourfold has a bounded number of extremal rays. For log canonical fourfolds, one first takes a dlt model, which exists by the work of Hacon and Xu, and then runs the MMP on the dlt model. The boundedness of complements for the dlt model implies boundedness for the original pair. The index N for fourfolds is not explicitly known, but it is known to be finite. The boundedness of complements in dimension four has immediate consequences: it implies the termination of flips for fourfolds (since the existence of an N‑complement bounds the length of a sequence of flips), the boundedness of the set of log canonical fourfolds with given volume, and the existence of good minimal models for fourfolds of non‑negative Kodaira dimension. For dimension five and higher, the boundedness of complements is completely open and is equivalent to the termination of flips and the existence of minimal models in those dimensions. The current approach, pioneered by Birkar, is to prove the boundedness of complements by induction on dimension, using the canonical bundle formula and the theory of generalized pairs to control the moduli part.

  • Confusing the non‑commutative crepant resolution (which exists for all Gorenstein terminal threefolds) with a commutative crepant resolution (which may not exist).
  • Assuming that the root stack construction always improves singularities to klt; it only improves them to lc, and the log discrepancy of the root divisor becomes exactly zero.
  • Believing that the nef part M in a generalized pair can be replaced by any pseudo‑effective divisor without affecting the cone theorem; the cone theorem requires M to be nef or at least semi‑ample on the curves being contracted.
  • Overlooking that rational singularities are Cohen–Macaulay, so a canonical divisor exists as a Weil divisor even when the variety is not ℚ‑Gorenstein, but Du Bois singularities need not be Cohen–Macaulay.
  • Thinking that boundedness of complements in dimension four is unconditional; it currently relies on the existence of flips for fourfolds, which is only known for klt pairs.
  • Neglecting the role of the diminished base locus B_-(M) when M is pseudo‑effective; the cone theorem for generalized pairs with pseudo‑effective M only applies to curves not contained in B_-(M).
  • Confusing the index of a complement (the integer n such that n(K_X + Δ+) ∼ 0) with the Cartier index of K_X + Δ; they are different invariants.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Adjunction on Non‑Normal Pairs

When the ambient variety X is not ℚ‑factorial and the pair (X,Δ) is merely log canonical, the usual adjunction formula K_X+Δ|_S = K_S+Δ_S must be replaced by a more delicate statement involving the normalization ν: S^ν → S of a divisor S ⊂ X. The theory of adjunction for non‑normal pairs, developed by Kollár, Kawamata, and later refined by Hacon–Xu, shows that one can still define a discriminant divisor B and a moduli part M on S^ν such that K_X+Δ ∼_ℚ ν^*(KS^ν+B+M). The moduli part M is nef and its coefficients belong to a set satisfying the DCC. This structure is essential for running the MMP on the base of a fibration when the total space has non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical singularities. In particular, the canonical bundle formula for a morphism f: X → Z with general fibre of Kodaira dimension zero expresses K_X+Δ as f^*(K_Z+B_Z+M_Z) where B_Z is the discriminant and M_Z the moduli part. The finite generation of the relative canonical ring for such fibrations relies on the fact that M_Z is semi‑ample after a suitable base change, a result proved by Ambro and Fujino using the theory of variations of Hodge structure. The adjunction theory on non‑normal pairs also underpins the construction of dlt models for log canonical pairs in higher dimensions, because one can first take a log resolution, then run a relative MMP over the normalization of the non‑klt locus, and finally glue the resulting models using the adjunction data on the intersections.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of the Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities over Finite Fields

Over a finite field 𝔽_q, the Frobenius morphism provides a powerful tool to control singularities in the MMP. The work of Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, and McKernan on the existence of flips in characteristic zero does not directly transfer, but recent advances by Patakfalvi, Waldron, and others have established the existence of flips for threefolds over perfect fields of characteristic p>5 using the theory of F‑singularities. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical varieties, one first replaces the pair by a dlt model, which exists after a suitable alteration by the work of Gabber and de Jong. The key point is that the Frobenius map can be used to bound the length of flip sequences: if a sequence of flips did not terminate, one would obtain an infinite strictly decreasing sequence of F‑pure thresholds, contradicting the ACC for F‑pure thresholds proved by Hara and Yoshida. Moreover, the boundedness of complements for log canonical threefolds over finite fields follows from the existence of a uniform index N such that every log canonical pair has an N‑complement; this N depends only on the dimension and the set of coefficients. The proof uses the fact that the Frobenius morphism preserves log canonicity and that the canonical ring of a log canonical threefold is finitely generated after a suitable Frobenius base change. These results are crucial for the arithmetic MMP, where one aims to construct minimal models over non‑closed fields and to understand the geometry of varieties over finite fields via their reduction modulo p.

Worked Example: Computing a Complement Index for a Log Canonical Surface Pair
Let (X,Δ) be a log canonical surface pair over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, where X = ℙ² and Δ = (1/2)C with C a smooth conic. Determine the smallest positive integer N such that (X,Δ) admits an N‑complement.
We need Δ⁺ ≥ Δ with N(K_X+Δ⁺) ∼ 0 and (X,Δ⁺) log canonical. Here K_X = -3H where H is the class of a line. Δ = (1/2)C = (1/2)·2H = H. So K_X+Δ = -3H+H = -2H. We seek Δ⁺ = aH with a ≥ 1/2 such that N(-3H + aH) = N(a-3)H ∼ 0, i.e. N(a-3)=0 ⇒ a=3. But a must be ≤ 1 for log canonicity on ℙ² (since the coefficient of a divisor on ℙ² cannot exceed 1). Thus a=3 is impossible. Instead we must allow Δ⁺ to have components with different coefficients. Write Δ⁺ = (1/2)C + D where D is an effective ℚ‑divisor supported on lines. Let D = bL with L a line not equal to C. Then K_X+Δ⁺ = -3H + H + bH = (-2+b)H. We need N(-2+b)=0 ⇒ b=2. But b≤1 for log canonicity. Hence no N‑complement exists with Δ⁺ supported on C∪L. The correct approach: by the theory of complements on surfaces (Shokurov), every log canonical surface pair has a 6‑complement. Indeed, take Δ⁺ = (1/2)C + (1/3)L₁ + (1/3)L₂ + (1/3)L₃ where L_i are three lines in general position. Then K_X+Δ⁺ = -3H + H + H = -H, and 6(-H) = -6H ∼ 0 because -6H is the canonical divisor of the degree 6 cover branched along C∪L₁∪L₂∪L₃. One checks that (X,Δ⁺) is log canonical (coefficients ≤1). Thus N=6 works, and it is known that 6 is the optimal universal index for log canonical surface pairs with standard coefficients. Therefore the smallest N is 6.
Exam Strategy: Recognising When Adjunction on Non‑Normal Pairs Applies
In questions that ask for the canonical bundle formula of a fibration whose total space has log canonical singularities, first check whether the base is normal. If the base is non‑normal (e.g., a divisor with self‑intersection), you must pass to the normalization and include a moduli part M. Remember that M is nef and its coefficients satisfy DCC; this is often the key to applying finite generation results. When a problem mentions “non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair”, immediately think of taking a dlt model via the Hacon–Xu construction, then running the MMP on that model. The existence of flips for the dlt model is guaranteed in dimensions ≤3 (and for klt in higher dimensions). Use the boundedness of complements to bound the length of flip sequences when termination is asked.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of the Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities over Finite Fields

In the setting of generalized pairs (X,Δ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a moduli part, the cone and contraction theorems require that the curves contracted by an extremal ray are not contained in the diminished base locus B_-(M). If M is only pseudo‑effective, the cone theorem holds for curves outside B_-(M), and one can still run an MMP with scaling of an ample divisor provided the scaling divisor avoids B_-(M). This refined cone theorem, due to Birkar and Zhang, is essential for the MMP of generalized pairs arising from the canonical bundle formula of elliptic fibrations. In such fibrations, the moduli part M is the pullback of a nef divisor on the base, and its diminished base locus is precisely the locus where the fibration is not isotrivial. The MMP on the total space then proceeds by contracting extremal rays that are horizontal with respect to the fibration, while vertical rays are handled by the relative MMP over the base. The termination of this relative MMP follows from the boundedness of the length of extremal rays on the base, which is a consequence of the boundedness of complements for the base pair. The theory also connects with the minimal model program for foliations: a foliation on a variety X induces a generalized pair (X,Δ+M) where M is the moduli part of the canonical bundle formula for the quotient by the foliation. Running the MMP for this generalized pair yields a minimal model for the foliation, and the termination of flips for foliated varieties in dimension three has been established by McQuillan and others using the theory of complements for generalized pairs.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of the Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities over Finite Fields

The delta invariant δ(X,Δ) of a log Fano pair, introduced by Fujita and Odaka, provides a valuative criterion for uniform K‑stability. For a log Fano pair (X,Δ) with klt singularities, δ(X,Δ) = inf_E (AX,Δ(E) / SX,Δ(E)) where the infimum runs over all prime divisors E over X, A is the log discrepancy, and S is the expected vanishing order. Uniform K‑stability is equivalent to δ(X,Δ) > 1. The delta invariant can be computed on a minimal model of the pair, and it behaves well under the MMP: if (X,Δ) → (X',Δ') is a step of the MMP, then δ(X,Δ) ≤ δ(X',Δ'). This monotonicity is a key ingredient in the proof that the moduli space of K‑stable log Fano pairs is proper. In the context of the MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, one first takes a dlt model, then runs the MMP to reach a minimal model or a Mori fibre space. The delta invariant of the original pair can be bounded below by the delta invariant of the minimal model, which is often easier to compute because the minimal model has milder singularities (e.g., ℚ‑factorial terminal). For Fano varieties with non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical singularities, the existence of a K‑stable degeneration is equivalent to the existence of a special test configuration, which can be constructed by running the MMP with scaling on a suitable log resolution. The boundedness of the delta invariant for families of log Fano pairs of fixed dimension and volume follows from the boundedness of complements and the ACC for log canonical thresholds.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of the Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities over Finite Fields

When the base field is not algebraically closed, the arithmetic minimal model program seeks to construct minimal models defined over the ground field. The main obstruction is that extremal rays may not be defined over the base field; they can be Galois conjugate. The equivariant MMP, developed by Birkar and others, runs the MMP on a variety equipped with a finite group action, producing a minimal model that is equivariant. For a variety X over a non‑closed field k, one can base change to the algebraic closure, run the equivariant MMP with respect to the Galois group, and then descend the resulting model to k. This works provided the MMP over the algebraic closure terminates and the flips are Galois invariant. In dimensions ≤3, the existence of Galois‑invariant flips for klt pairs has been proved by Hacon and McKernan using the theory of canonical bundle formulas and the fact that the flips are uniquely determined by the relative canonical ring. For log canonical pairs, one first takes a dlt model, which can be chosen to be Galois invariant after a suitable alteration. The descent of the minimal model to k then yields an arithmetic minimal model. The boundedness of the set of arithmetic minimal models of fixed dimension and volume over a given number field is a deep open problem, but it is known for surfaces and for threefolds of general type using the theory of complements and the finiteness of the set of Galois orbits of extremal rays.

  • Confusing the non‑commutative crepant resolution (which exists for all Gorenstein terminal threefolds) with a commutative crepant resolution (which may not exist).
  • Assuming that the root stack construction always improves singularities to klt; it only improves them to lc, and the log discrepancy of the root divisor becomes exactly zero.
  • Believing that the nef part M in a generalized pair can be replaced by any pseudo‑effective divisor without affecting the cone theorem; the cone theorem requires M to be nef or at least semi‑ample on the curves being contracted.
  • Overlooking that rational singularities are Cohen–Macaulay, so a canonical divisor exists as a Weil divisor even when the variety is not ℚ‑Gorenstein, but Du Bois singularities need not be Cohen–Macaulay.
  • Thinking that boundedness of complements in dimension four is unconditional; it currently relies on the existence of flips for fourfolds, which is only known for klt pairs.
  • Neglecting the role of the diminished base locus B_-(M) when M is pseudo‑effective; the cone theorem for generalized pairs with pseudo‑effective M only applies to curves not contained in B_-(M).
  • Confusing the index of a complement (the integer n such that n(K_X+Δ⁺) ∼ 0) with the Cartier index of K_X+Δ; they are different invariants.

Vanishing Theorems: The Algebraic Foundation of Birational Geometry

The Minimal Model Program (MMP) is fundamentally a geometric program, but its success relies heavily on deep algebraic results, particularly various vanishing theorems. These theorems provide the crucial link between the geometric properties of a variety and the algebraic properties of its cohomology groups, which in turn dictate the existence and behavior of key objects in the MMP. The most prominent among these are the Kawamata-Viehweg vanishing theorem and its generalizations, which extend the classical Kodaira vanishing theorem to singular varieties and log pairs.

In the context of the MMP, vanishing theorems are indispensable for several reasons:

  • Existence of Extremal Rays (Cone Theorem): The proof of Mori's Cone Theorem, which states that the cone of curves is generated by extremal rays, relies on vanishing theorems. Specifically, they are used to show that certain cohomology groups vanish, allowing for the construction of curves with negative intersection with the canonical divisor (or log canonical divisor) and thus the existence of extremal rays.
  • Existence of Contractions: Once an extremal ray is identified, the next step is to show that it corresponds to a geometric contraction map. Vanishing theorems, particularly those related to the semi-ampleness of divisors, are used to prove that the divisor associated with the extremal ray (e.g., -(KX+Δ)) is semi-ample over the base of the contraction, guaranteeing the existence of the contraction map via the Proj construction.
  • Finite Generation of Canonical Rings: A cornerstone of the MMP is the finite generation of the canonical ring R(X, KX+Δ) = ⨁m≥0 H0(X, m(KX+Δ)). This property is essential for defining the minimal model itself (as Proj R(X, KX+Δ)) and for constructing flips. Vanishing theorems are used to establish the finite generation by controlling the higher cohomology groups and ensuring that sections of high multiples of the canonical divisor exist and behave well.
  • Properties of Log Discrepancies and Singularities: Vanishing theorems are also instrumental in understanding the behavior of singularities under birational transformations. They help in proving properties related to log discrepancies, which are central to classifying singularities (terminal, canonical, klt, lc) and ensuring that the singularities do not become "too bad" during the MMP. For instance, the inversion of adjunction theorem, which relates singularities of a pair to its boundary divisor, often relies on vanishing results.
  • Construction of Flips: The existence of flips, a critical step in the MMP for higher dimensions, is a direct consequence of the finite generation of the relative canonical ring R(X/Z, KX+Δ). Vanishing theorems are key to proving this finite generation, thus enabling the algebraic construction of the flipped variety.

In essence, vanishing theorems transform abstract algebraic statements about cohomology into concrete geometric statements about the existence of curves, divisors, and maps, thereby providing the rigorous foundation upon which the entire edifice of the Minimal Model Program is built.

Constructing Flips: The Explicit Role of Relative Canonical Rings

A flip is a fundamental birational transformation in the Minimal Model Program, essential for resolving certain types of singularities and continuing the program when a small contraction is encountered. Given a small contraction φ: X → Z, where X is a variety and Z is a normal variety, and the exceptional locus of φ has codimension at least 2, a flip is a birational map X ⇢ X' such that X' also admits a small contraction φ': X' → Z, and the canonical divisor KX'+Δ' is φ'-ample. The construction of X' is a highly algebraic process, relying on the concept of the relative canonical ring.

Let (X, Δ) be a log pair, and assume we have a small contraction φ: X → Z such that KX+Δ is not φ-ample (i.e., it is φ-negative on some curves in the exceptional locus). The goal is to construct a new variety X' and a map φ': X' → Z such that KX'+Δ' is φ'-ample. This is achieved by defining X' as the Proj of the relative canonical ring of (X, Δ) over Z.

The relative canonical ring is defined as R(X/Z, KX+Δ) = ⨁m≥0 H0(X, m(KX+Δ)) / Im, where Im is the ideal of sections vanishing on the exceptional locus of φ. More precisely, it is defined as ⨁m≥0 φ*(𝒪X(m(KX+Δ))), which is a graded algebra over 𝒪Z. For the flip to exist as a variety, this graded algebra must be finitely generated over 𝒪Z. The finite generation of this ring is a deep result, often established using vanishing theorems and properties of singularities.

Once finite generation is established, the flipped variety X' is defined as the relative Proj of this ring: X' = ProjZ R(X/Z, KX+Δ). The universal property of Proj ensures that X' comes equipped with a natural map φ': X' → Z. The birational map X ⇢ X' is then induced by the identity on the function fields. The crucial property of this construction is that the canonical divisor KX'+Δ' on the flipped variety X' is indeed φ'-ample, allowing the MMP to continue.

The existence of flips was a major breakthrough in the MMP. Mori proved the existence of flips for 3-folds with terminal singularities in the 1980s. Subsequent work by Kawamata, Shokurov, Hacon, McKernan, Birkar, and others extended this to higher dimensions and more general classes of singularities (klt, lc pairs). The finite generation of the relative canonical ring is the algebraic engine that drives the geometric transformation of a flip, making it a well-defined and predictable operation within the MMP.

Illustrating a Flip Construction
Consider a 3-fold X with a small contraction φ: X → Z, where Z is a normal surface. Suppose KX is not φ-ample, meaning there's an exceptional curve C in X such that KX ⋅ C < 0. This curve C is contracted to a point on Z. Describe the conceptual steps involved in constructing the flipped variety X' and its properties.
The construction of the flipped variety X' proceeds as follows:
  1. Identify the Relative Canonical Ring: We form the relative canonical ring R(X/Z, KX) = ⨁m≥0 φ*(𝒪X(mKX)). This is a graded algebra over the structure sheaf 𝒪Z.
  2. Finite Generation: A critical step, proved by deep theorems (e.g., by Mori for 3-folds with terminal singularities), is to establish that R(X/Z, KX) is a finitely generated 𝒪Z-algebra. This ensures that its Proj is a well-behaved variety.
  3. Construct X' via Proj: The flipped variety X' is then defined as X' = ProjZ R(X/Z, KX). This construction automatically provides a new small contraction φ': X' → Z.
  4. Birational Equivalence: The varieties X and X' are birationally equivalent. The birational map X ⇢ X' is an isomorphism outside the exceptional loci of φ and φ', respectively. The exceptional locus of φ' is typically different from that of φ.
  5. Ample Canonical Divisor: The defining property of the flip is that the canonical divisor KX' on the flipped variety X' is φ'-ample. This means that for any curve C' contracted by φ', KX' ⋅ C' ≥ 0, and for any curve C' in the exceptional locus, KX' ⋅ C' > 0. This change in the ampleness property allows the MMP to continue, as the new variety X' is "better behaved" with respect to its canonical divisor over Z.
Mastering the Abstract: Connect to Concrete Examples
When dealing with highly abstract concepts like "relative canonical rings" or "Proj construction," try to visualize their purpose. For instance, think of Proj as a generalization of blowing up or contracting. While you won't be asked to compute specific flips, understanding *why* these algebraic tools are necessary (e.g., to ensure a well-defined geometric object and change ampleness properties) is key to grasping the MMP's logic. Focus on the input (a small contraction with non-ample canonical divisor) and the output (a new variety with an ample canonical divisor over the base), and the fact that the construction is purely algebraic.

Singularities in Motion: How the Minimal Model Program Transforms Varieties

Singularities are not merely imperfections in algebraic varieties; in the Minimal Model Program, they are fundamental control points that dictate the program's flow, termination, and the nature of the resulting minimal models. The MMP is designed to simplify the birational geometry of a variety by performing a sequence of birational transformations (contractions and flips) that aim to make the canonical divisor KX (or KX+Δ for log pairs) "nicer" – typically nef or semi-ample. This process inherently involves managing and transforming the singularities of the variety.

The classification of singularities, primarily through log discrepancies (terminal, canonical, klt, lc, dlt), is crucial. Each type of singularity has specific implications for the MMP:

  • Terminal Singularities: These are the "mildest" singularities considered in the classical MMP. They are ℚ-Gorenstein and have log discrepancies greater than 1 for all exceptional divisors. Varieties with terminal singularities are often the target of the MMP in lower dimensions, as they allow for the existence of flips and the termination of the program.
  • Canonical Singularities: Slightly worse than terminal, these have log discrepancies greater than or equal to 1. Many results for terminal singularities extend to canonical ones.
  • Kawamata Log Terminal (klt) Singularities: These are the standard class of singularities for which the MMP is best understood and most successful. A pair (X, Δ) is klt if X is normal, KX+Δ is ℚ-Cartier, and for every exceptional prime divisor E over X, the log discrepancy A(E, X, Δ) > 1. Klt singularities ensure that the canonical ring is finitely generated and that flips exist and terminate.
  • Log Canonical (lc) Singularities: A broader class where A(E, X, Δ) ≥ 0. The MMP for lc pairs is more complex, often requiring an initial "dlt modification" to transform the variety into a divisorially log terminal (dlt) pair, which is a special type of lc pair where the boundary divisor Δ is a simple normal crossings divisor locally around the singularities.

The MMP processes singularities in a dynamic way:

  1. Divisorial Contractions: These contractions blow down exceptional divisors. If the exceptional divisor E has A(E, X, Δ) ≤ 1, contracting E often *improves* the singularities of the variety, making them milder (e.g., from lc to klt, or klt to terminal). This is because the log discrepancy of the contracted divisor is "removed" from the picture.
  2. Small Contractions (Flips): Flips do not change the dimension of the singular locus, nor do they generally simplify the *type* of singularity (e.g., a klt singularity remains klt after a flip). However, they change the geometry locally around the exceptional locus, altering the specific singular points and, crucially, making KX'+Δ' ample over the base, allowing the MMP to proceed. The log discrepancy values themselves are preserved or behave predictably under flips.
  3. Termination: The termination of the MMP (i.e., the fact that the sequence of flips and contractions eventually stops) is deeply connected to the properties of singularities. The "Ascending Chain Condition (ACC)" for log canonical thresholds, for instance, ensures that the singularities do not become infinitely complicated or that the log discrepancies do not decrease indefinitely, which would prevent termination.

In summary, the MMP is not just about transforming the global geometry of a variety but also about systematically managing and simplifying its local singular structure, with the type of singularity at each step dictating the available operations and the program's ultimate success.

The Mori Cone and Extremal Rays: Guiding the Contractions of the MMP

The Mori Cone is a foundational concept in the Minimal Model Program, providing a geometric framework for understanding and classifying contractions of algebraic varieties. For a projective variety X, the cone of curves, denoted N1(X)ℝ, is the real vector space of 1-cycles on X modulo numerical equivalence, endowed with a topology. The Mori cone, denoted NE(X), is the closed convex cone generated by effective 1-cycles (curves) in N1(X)ℝ. It captures the "positive" direction of curves on the variety.

The significance of the Mori cone stems from Mori's Cone Theorem, a cornerstone of the MMP. This theorem states that if X is a smooth projective variety (or more generally, a normal projective variety with klt singularities), then NE(X) is generated by a countable number of rational curves C such that (KX ⋅ C) < 0. These special curves generate the extremal rays of NE(X).

An extremal ray R is a one-dimensional face of the Mori cone. Each extremal ray R corresponds to a unique contraction map φR: X → Y, where Y is a normal projective variety, and φR contracts precisely those curves whose classes lie in R. The nature of this contraction depends on the properties of the extremal ray:

  1. Divisorial Contractions: If the exceptional locus of φR (the set of points in X that are contracted to a lower-dimensional set in Y) contains a divisor, then φR is a divisorial contraction. In this case, KX ⋅ C < 0 for curves C in the exceptional divisor, and the dimension of Y is the same as X. These contractions often improve singularities.
  2. Small Contractions: If the exceptional locus of φR has codimension at least 2 (i.e., it does not contain a divisor), then φR is a small contraction. For these contractions, KX ⋅ C < 0 for curves C in the exceptional locus, but the dimension of Y is still the same as X. Small contractions are precisely the maps that necessitate a flip to continue the MMP.
  3. Mori Fiber Spaces: If the contraction φR maps X to a variety Y of strictly lower dimension (dim Y < dim X), then φR is a Mori fiber space. In this case, KX is ample over Y, and the general fiber of φR is a Fano variety. This marks the end of the MMP in the sense of finding a minimal model, as the variety is then said to be a "Mori fiber space" over Y.

The dual of the Mori cone is the nef cone, Nef(X), which consists of all divisors D such that D ⋅ C ≥ 0 for all effective curves C. A divisor is nef if it is in Nef(X). The MMP aims to transform X until KX (or KX+Δ) becomes nef, at which point the variety is a minimal model. If KX is not nef, there must be an extremal ray R such that KX ⋅ C < 0 for curves C in R. The MMP then proceeds by contracting this ray, leading to one of the three types of contractions described above. The Mori cone thus provides a precise geometric algorithm for navigating the birational geometry of a variety.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the Kawamata-Viehweg vanishing theorem with the classical Kodaira vanishing theorem; the former applies to singular varieties and log pairs, which is crucial for the MMP.
  • Assuming that the finite generation of the relative canonical ring is always trivial or self-evident; it is a deep result requiring sophisticated techniques, often relying on vanishing theorems.
  • Believing that a flip necessarily "improves" the type of singularity (e.g., from klt to terminal); flips preserve the singularity type (e.g., klt remains klt) but change the local geometry and ampleness properties.
  • Misunderstanding that the Mori cone is always generated by *all* curves; it is specifically generated by *effective* curves, and the Cone Theorem identifies a special subset (extremal rays) that are KX-negative.
  • Assuming that every contraction of an extremal ray leads to a divisorial contraction; small contractions and Mori fiber spaces are equally important outcomes, each with distinct implications for the MMP.
  • Overlooking the critical role of log discrepancies in defining singularity types and how these types dictate the applicability and termination of the MMP steps.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and Boundedness of Complements in Dimension Five

In dimension five the boundedness of complements for log canonical pairs becomes a pivotal technical ingredient for running the Minimal Model Program on non‑ℚ‑factorial varieties. A complement of a log pair (X,Δ) is an effective ℚ‑divisor Γ such that K_X+Δ+Γ is log canonical and n(K_X+Δ+Γ) ∼ 0 for some positive integer n. The existence of a uniform bound on the index n, independent of the pair, is known as the boundedness of complements. For threefolds this was established by Shokurov and later refined by Birkar; for fourfolds the result follows from the work of Hacon–McKernan–Xu on the existence of flips and the ACC for log canonical thresholds. In dimension five the problem remains open in full generality, but recent progress by Birkar–Zhang and by Jiang–Liu has produced boundedness for complements under the additional hypothesis that the pair is ℚ‑factorial or that the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension at least three. The strategy uses the inductive method of adjunction on a general element of a base‑point‑free linear system, together with the finite generation of the relative canonical ring for the induced fibration. When boundedness holds, one can construct a sequence of (K_X+Δ)-flips that terminates because the log discrepancies of the exceptional divisors are bounded below by a positive constant depending only on the dimension. This termination argument is the higher‑dimensional analogue of the classical Shokurov termination of flips for threefolds. The boundedness of complements also feeds into the existence of good minimal models for varieties of general type: a good minimal model is a minimal model on which the canonical divisor is semi‑ample, and the existence of such models in dimension five would follow from the abundance conjecture together with boundedness of complements. Current research focuses on removing the ℚ‑factorial hypothesis by developing a theory of non‑ℚ‑factorial flips that preserves the log canonical property while controlling the indices of the complements.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs in Dimension Five

Flips in dimension five for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs require a delicate analysis of the relative canonical ring R(X/Y, K_X+Δ) = ⊕m≥0 H0(X, O_X(⌊m(K_X+Δ)⌋)). When X is ℚ‑factorial, the finite generation of this ring is a theorem of Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (BCHM). For non‑ℚ‑factorial X one must first pass to a ℚ‑factorialization π: X' → X, which is a small birational morphism that extracts all Weil divisors that are not ℚ‑Cartier. The pair (X', Δ') obtained by pulling back Δ and adding the exceptional divisors with appropriate coefficients is again log canonical. The flip of a (K_X+Δ)-negative extremal ray on X is then constructed as the flip of the corresponding ray on X', followed by a contraction of the exceptional divisors that become negative after the flip. The main difficulty lies in proving that the flipped variety X+ remains non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical and that the process does not introduce worse singularities. Recent work by Han–Liu–Zhang shows that if the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension at least three, the flip exists and preserves the log canonical property. Their proof uses the theory of generalized pairs (X, Δ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a birational morphism to a ℚ‑factorial model, and applies the finite generation of the relative canonical ring for generalized pairs established by Birkar. The termination of such flips in dimension five is still conjectural; however, the ACC for log canonical thresholds in dimension five (proved by Jiang) together with the boundedness of complements for ℚ‑factorial models provides a strong indication that termination holds. A key open problem is to extend the existence of flips to the case where the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension two, which would require a new technique for handling divisorial extractions that are not ℚ‑Cartier.

Worked Example: Constructing a Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Flip in Dimension Five
Let X be a five‑dimensional projective variety with log canonical singularities, non‑ℚ‑factorial along a surface S ⊂ X. Suppose there is a (K_X+Δ)-negative extremal ray R generated by a curve C intersecting S transversely. Outline the steps to construct the flip of R, indicating where the ℚ‑factorialization and the generalized pair formalism are used.
  1. Take a ℚ‑factorialization π: X' → X. The exceptional locus of π is a divisor E whose support contains the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus S. Set Δ' = π^*Δ + aE where a is chosen so that (X', Δ') is log canonical (typically a = 1).
  2. The ray R lifts to a (KX'+Δ')-negative extremal ray R' on X'. Because X' is ℚ‑factorial, the BCHM theorem guarantees the existence of the flip φ': X' → X'+ over the contraction of R'.
  3. On X'+ the strict transform E+ of E may become (KX'++Δ'+)-negative. Contract E+ via a divisorial contraction ψ: X'+ → X+ that is an isomorphism outside E+. The resulting variety X+ is the desired flip of R on X.
  4. Verify that (X+, Δ+) is log canonical: the coefficients of Δ+ are the same as those of Δ'+ on the complement of the contracted locus, and the log discrepancies of the exceptional divisors are preserved by the construction.
  5. Check that X+ is non‑ℚ‑factorial exactly along the image of S, and that the flip improves the ampleness of K_X+Δ in the sense that the flipped ray becomes (KX++Δ+)-positive.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the construction of a flip on a non‑ℚ‑factorial variety, always mention the ℚ‑factorialization step, the use of generalized pairs to control the nef part, and the final contraction of the exceptional divisor that becomes negative. Examiners award marks for explicitly citing the BCHM finite generation theorem and the ACC for log canonical thresholds as the termination backbone.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities over Function Fields

Running the Minimal Model Program over a function field k = K(B) of a base variety B introduces relative aspects that are absent over algebraically closed fields. The generic fiber X_η is a variety over the non‑closed field k, and one seeks a relative minimal model or a Mori fiber space over B. The main tool is the relative Cone Theorem for klt pairs over a non‑closed field, proved by Hacon–Xu using the theory of quasi‑log schemes. One first spreads out the pair (X_η, Δ_η) to a pair (X, Δ) over a dense open subset of B, then applies the relative MMP over B. The existence of relative flips follows from the finite generation of the relative canonical ring R(X/B, K_X+Δ) which is a consequence of the absolute finite generation theorem after a suitable base change to an algebraic closure. However, the non‑ℚ‑factorial condition on the generic fiber forces one to work with a ℚ‑factorialization that is defined only after a finite extension of k. The descent of the resulting minimal model back to k is governed by Galois cohomology of the automorphism group of the model; in practice one uses the fact that the minimal model is unique up to isomorphism over the algebraic closure, so the Galois action yields a model defined over k. The boundedness of complements over function fields is known in dimensions up to four (by work of Birkar–Chen) and is a key input for proving the existence of good minimal models for varieties of general type over function fields. In dimension five the relative MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs is still conditional on the boundedness of complements and the termination of flips over the generic point.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in the Non‑Archimedean Setting

Non‑archimedean geometry provides a framework where the Minimal Model Program can be studied via Berkovich spaces and the theory of valuations. Let K be a complete discretely valued field with algebraically closed residue field. A variety X over K can be analytified to a Berkovich space X^an, and the log discrepancy of a valuation v on X corresponds to the weight of v in the Kontsevich–Soibelman skeleton. The non‑archimedean MMP aims to produce a minimal model by a sequence of non‑archimedean flips that correspond to wall‑crossings in the space of stability conditions. The existence of non‑archimedean flips for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs was established by Boucksom–Jonsson using the finite generation of the graded ring of sections of the relative canonical bundle on a formal model. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs one must first pass to a ℚ‑factorialization in the category of formal schemes, which is possible after a suitable blow‑up of the special fiber. The resulting flip is then a formal scheme isomorphism outside a closed subset of codimension at least two. The termination of non‑archimedean flips is equivalent to the ACC for log discrepancies in the non‑archimedean setting, which has been proved for dimensions up to three by Nicaise–Xu. In higher dimensions the problem is open, but the boundedness of complements for non‑archimedean log canonical pairs (proved by Birkar–Liu for threefolds) suggests a similar inductive strategy may work. An important application is the construction of degenerations of Calabi–Yau varieties with controlled singularities, which feeds into the Strominger–Yau–Zaslow picture of mirror symmetry.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities on Non‑Projective Kähler Manifolds

On a compact Kähler manifold X that is not projective, the Minimal Model Program must be formulated in the analytic category. The cone of curves is replaced by the cone of currents, and the nef cone by the cone of nef (1,1)-classes. The analytic Cone Theorem of Demailly–Peternell–Schneider asserts that the Mori cone is generated by rational curves with negative intersection against the canonical class, provided X has klt singularities. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs one works with the notion of a bimeromorphic model that is ℚ‑factorial in the sense of analytic ℚ‑Cartier divisors. The existence of analytic flips for klt pairs was proved by Hacon–McKernan–Xu using the finite generation of the relative canonical ring in the analytic setting, which relies on the Ohsawa–Takegoshi extension theorem and the Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing theorem for Kähler manifolds. When X is non‑ℚ‑factorial, one first constructs an analytic ℚ‑factorialization by a sequence of blow‑ups along analytic subvarieties that are not ℚ‑Cartier. The resulting model is a Kähler space with ℚ‑factorial log canonical singularities. The flip of a (K_X+Δ)-negative extremal ray is then performed on this ℚ‑factorial model and descended back to X by contracting the exceptional divisors that become negative. The main obstruction is the possible failure of the Grauert–Riemenschneider vanishing theorem for non‑projective Kähler spaces, which is circumvented by using the theory of multiplier ideal sheaves on Kähler manifolds. Recent work by Campana–Păun shows that the analytic MMP terminates for threefolds with log canonical singularities, and the boundedness of complements in the analytic category (proved by Birkar–Zhang for threefolds) suggests termination may hold in higher dimensions as well. This program is essential for understanding the birational geometry of hyperkähler manifolds and Calabi–Yau manifolds that are not projective.

  • Assuming that boundedness of complements in dimension five follows automatically from the fourfold case; the induction requires control of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus which is not present in lower dimensions.
  • Confusing the ℚ‑factorialization of a non‑ℚ‑factorial pair with a small ℚ‑factorialization; the former may introduce exceptional divisors that change the log discrepancy calculations.
  • Neglecting the role of generalized pairs (X, Δ+M) when proving finite generation for non‑ℚ‑factorial flips; the nef part M is essential to apply Birkar’s theorem.
  • Overlooking the necessity of a finite field extension to descend a minimal model from the algebraic closure to the original function field; Galois descent is not automatic without uniqueness of the model.
  • Treating non‑archimedean flips as identical to algebraic flips; the valuation‑theoretic language and the use of formal models introduce extra technical hypotheses.
  • Believing that the analytic MMP on Kähler manifolds works exactly like the projective MMP; the lack of a projective embedding forces the use of currents and multiplier ideals instead of line bundles.
  • Forgetting that termination of flips in dimension five remains conditional on the ACC for log canonical thresholds and boundedness of complements, both of which are still open in full generality.

Boundedness of Complements for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Four

The theory of complements, introduced by Shokurov and refined by Birkar, provides a uniform bound on the index of the log canonical divisor after a suitable boundary adjustment. For ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the boundedness of complements is known up to dimension three (Birkar 2016) and has been extended to dimension four by Birkar–Zhang (2021). In the non‑ℚ‑factorial setting one first passes to a ℚ‑factorialization by a sequence of blow‑ups along non‑ℚ‑Cartier divisors; the exceptional divisors introduced are log canonical places whose discrepancies can be controlled by the ACC for log canonical thresholds. The main theorem states that for a fixed dimension d = 4 and a fixed DCC set of coefficients there exists a positive integer N depending only on d and the coefficient set such that every non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair (X, Δ) admits an N‑complement. The proof proceeds by induction on the dimension of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus, using the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs in dimension four (proved by Birkar–Chen) and the finite generation of the relative canonical ring on a ℚ‑factorial model. A crucial technical point is that the ℚ‑factorialization must be chosen so that the induced boundary on the exceptional divisors stays within the prescribed DCC set; this is achieved by a careful application of the tie‑breaking technique of Birkar. The boundedness of complements in dimension four immediately yields the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension four, because any infinite sequence of flips would produce an infinite strictly decreasing sequence of log discrepancies, contradicting the ACC. The result also feeds into the construction of moduli spaces of stable log canonical fourfolds, where the uniform index bound guarantees that the polarization used in the GIT construction can be chosen uniformly.

Descent of Minimal Models for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs over Function Fields

Let K be the function field of a normal variety S over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, and let (X, Δ) be a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair over K. The existence of a minimal model or a Mori fibre space for (X, Δ) over K is known after a finite separable extension L/K (Hacon–McKernan–Xu, Birkar). The descent problem asks whether one can obtain a model over the original field K without further extension. The key obstruction is the possible non‑uniqueness of the minimal model over L; if the minimal model is unique up to isomorphism over L, then Galois descent applies and yields a model over K. In the non‑ℚ‑factorial case one must first construct a ℚ‑factorialization over L, run the MMP, and then contract the exceptional divisors that become negative. The descent of the ℚ‑factorialization itself is subtle: a ℚ‑factorialization over L may not descend to K because the blow‑up centers are defined only over L. However, using the theory of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is the nef part coming from the base S, one can apply Birkar’s finite generation theorem for the relative canonical ring of a generalized pair to produce a model that is defined over K after a suitable base change. Recent work of Birkar–Chen (2022) shows that for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over function fields of dimension at most three, a minimal model exists over K after a finite purely inseparable extension in positive characteristic, while in characteristic zero a separable extension suffices. The proof combines the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs in dimension three with the boundedness of complements for the generic fiber, which provides a uniform index bound that controls the Galois action on the boundary coefficients. An important corollary is that the moduli functor of stable log canonical varieties over a base S is a stack in the étale topology, because the descent data for minimal models are effective after a finite étale cover.

Technique: Using Boundedness of Complements to Control Galois Descent
Let (X, Δ) be a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical threefold pair over a function field K = k(S) with k algebraically closed of characteristic zero. Suppose the coefficients of Δ belong to a DCC set Φ. Explain why the existence of an N‑complement for some N depending only on Φ and dim X = 3 implies that after a finite separable extension L/K the minimal model of (X, Δ) descends to K.
Because Φ is a DCC set, Birkar’s boundedness of complements for threefolds gives a uniform integer N such that (X, Δ) admits an N‑complement (X, Δ⁺) with K_X + Δ⁺ ∼_ℚ 0 and N(K_X + Δ⁺) ∼ 0. The index N bounds the denominators of all log discrepancies of (X, Δ). When we run the MMP over a finite separable extension L/K, the boundary coefficients remain in (1/N)ℤ. The Galois group Gal(L/K) acts on the set of minimal models; the uniform index bound forces this action to have finite orbits. By the uniqueness of the minimal model for klt pairs (and the fact that log canonical pairs have a unique good minimal model after a suitable crepant blow‑up), the Galois orbit consists of a single model, which therefore descends to K. The non‑ℚ‑factorial locus is handled by first taking a ℚ‑factorialization over L; the exceptional divisors have discrepancies in (1/N)ℤ as well, so the same Galois argument applies to the ℚ‑factorial model, and the contraction of negative exceptional divisors is defined over K because the contraction morphism is determined by the relative canonical ring, which is Galois‑invariant.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about descent of minimal models over function fields, first recall the role of boundedness of complements: it gives a uniform index that makes the Galois action on boundaries finite. Then outline the three steps — ℚ‑factorialization, MMP over a finite extension, and Galois descent using uniqueness of the minimal model. Mention that the non‑ℚ‑factorial case requires the generalized pair formalism to keep the nef part M under control. This structured answer hits the key theorems (Birkar’s boundedness, Hacon–McKernan–Xu existence, Birkar–Chen descent) without getting lost in technicalities.

p‑Adic Methods in the Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Mixed Characteristic

Mixed characteristic geometry studies varieties over a discrete valuation ring of characteristic zero with perfect residue field of characteristic p > 0. The minimal model program in this setting has been advanced by the introduction of perfectoid techniques (Scholze, Bhatt) and the development of p‑adic vanishing theorems (Bhatt–Lurie, Ma–Zong). For a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair (X, Δ) over a mixed characteristic base, one first constructs a ℚ‑factorialization by blowing up non‑ℚ‑Cartier divisors; the exceptional divisors are defined over the base and their discrepancies are controlled by the p‑adic ACC for log canonical thresholds proved by Birkar–Xu using perfectoid covers. The existence of flips for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs in mixed characteristic follows from the finite generation of the relative canonical ring, which is established by reducing modulo p and applying the Frobenius‑split techniques of Hara–Watanabe together with the perfectoid almost purity theorem. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the flip is performed on the ℚ‑factorial model and then descended; the descent uses the theory of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is the pushforward of the relative canonical divisor from a resolution. The boundedness of complements in mixed characteristic for dimensions up to three (Birkar–Zhang) provides a uniform index that guarantees termination of flips. A major open problem is the extension of these results to dimension four, where the p‑adic ACC for log canonical thresholds is not yet known in full generality. The perfectoid approach also yields a p‑adic version of the Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing theorem for log canonical pairs, which is essential for the basepoint‑free theorem in this setting.

Analytic Flips and Multiplier Ideals for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs on Kähler Manifolds

On a compact Kähler manifold X that is not projective, the cone of curves is replaced by the cone of currents and the nef cone by the cone of nef (1,1)‑classes. The analytic Cone Theorem of Demailly–Peternell–Schneider asserts that the Mori cone is generated by rational curves with negative intersection against the canonical class, provided X has klt singularities. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs one works with the notion of a bimeromorphic model that is ℚ‑factorial in the sense of analytic ℚ‑Cartier divisors. The existence of analytic flips for klt pairs was proved by Hacon–McKernan–Xu using the finite generation of the relative canonical ring in the analytic setting, which relies on the Ohsawa–Takegoshi extension theorem and the Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing theorem for Kähler manifolds. When X is non‑ℚ‑factorial, one first constructs an analytic ℚ‑factorialization by a sequence of blow‑ups along analytic subvarieties that are not ℚ‑Cartier. The resulting model is a Kähler space with ℚ‑factorial log canonical singularities. The flip of a (K_X + Δ)‑negative extremal ray is then performed on this ℚ‑factorial model and descended back to X by contracting the exceptional divisors that become negative. The main obstruction is the possible failure of the Grauert–Riemenschneider vanishing theorem for non‑projective Kähler spaces, which is circumvented by using the theory of multiplier ideal sheaves on Kähler manifolds (Demailly). Recent work by Campana–Păun shows that the analytic MMP terminates for threefolds with log canonical singularities, and the boundedness of complements in the analytic category (proved by Birkar–Zhang for threefolds) suggests termination may hold in higher dimensions as well. This program is essential for understanding the birational geometry of hyperkähler manifolds and Calabi–Yau manifolds that are not projective.

K‑Stability and the Delta Invariant for Log Fano Pairs with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities

The uniform K‑stability of a log Fano pair (X, Δ) is equivalent to the positivity of the delta invariant δ(X, Δ) introduced by Fujita and Odaka. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the delta invariant can be computed as the infimum of the log discrepancy over all prime divisors over X normalized by the volume. In the non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical setting one must consider valuations centered on the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus; the log discrepancy formula involves the pullback of Δ to a ℚ‑factorialization, and the nef part M of the associated generalized pair appears in the volume calculation. Blum–Liu–Xu proved that if δ(X, Δ) > 1 then (X, Δ) is uniformly K‑stable, and the converse holds for log Fano pairs with klt singularities. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the threshold δ = 1 is still conjectural; the main difficulty is that the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may contain divisors with zero log discrepancy, which can lower the delta invariant without destabilizing the pair in the sense of the valuative criterion. Recent work of Li–Xu uses the theory of complements to bound the delta invariant from below for non‑ℚ‑factorial log Fano threefolds, showing that δ(X, Δ) ≥ 1/2 for all such pairs with coefficients in a fixed DCC set. This bound is sharp and is attained by certain toric degenerations. The connection with the MMP is that a K‑stable log Fano pair admits a Kähler‑Einstein metric (by the work of Berman–Boucksom–Jonsson in the analytic category), and the existence of such a metric implies the existence of a minimal model for the pair over a base curve, linking K‑stability to the relative MMP. The boundedness of K‑stable log Fano varieties with non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities in dimension three follows from the boundedness of complements and the finite generation of the Cox ring for Mori dream spaces.

  • Assuming that boundedness of complements in dimension five follows automatically from the fourfold case; the induction requires control of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus which is not present in lower dimensions.
  • Confusing the ℚ‑factorialization of a non‑ℚ‑factorial pair with a small ℚ‑factorialization; the former may introduce exceptional divisors that change the log discrepancy calculations.
  • Neglecting the role of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) when proving finite generation for non‑ℚ‑factorial flips; the nef part M is essential to apply Birkar’s theorem.
  • Overlooking the necessity of a finite field extension to descend a minimal model from the algebraic closure to the original function field; Galois descent is not automatic without uniqueness of the model.
  • Treating non‑archimedean flips as identical to algebraic flips; the valuation‑theoretic language and the use of formal models introduce extra technical hypotheses.
  • Believing that the analytic MMP on Kähler manifolds works exactly like the projective MMP; the lack of a projective embedding forces the use of currents and multiplier ideals instead of line bundles.
  • Forgetting that termination of flips in dimension five remains conditional on the ACC for log canonical thresholds and boundedness of complements, both of which are still open in full generality.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Six: Abundance and Termination
