The extension of the Minimal Model Program to dimension six for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is a frontier where several conjectures intersect. The abundance conjecture predicts that for a log canonical pair (X, Δ) with nef log canonical divisor K_X + Δ, the divisor is semi‑ample. In dimensions ≤ five, abundance is known for klt pairs and for log canonical pairs under additional hypotheses such as the existence of a good minimal model. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension six, the main obstruction is the lack of a general finite generation theorem for the adjoint ring ⊕m≥0 H0(X, ⌊m(K_X + Δ)⌋) without assuming ℚ‑factoriality. Recent work by Birkar and Zhang on the finite generation of the log canonical ring for ℚ‑factorial pairs does not directly apply because the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may contain divisors that are not ℚ‑Cartier, preventing the standard construction of a ℚ‑factorialization that preserves log canonicity. A partial result by Hacon–McKernan shows that if a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair admits a ℚ‑factorial dlt model after a sequence of blow‑ups along non‑ℚ‑Cartier centers, then abundance follows from the ℚ‑factorial case. However, the existence of such a model in dimension six is not guaranteed; the termination of the ℚ‑factorialization process itself is an open problem. Termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension six is likewise unknown. The standard strategy — reduce to the ℚ‑factorial case via a small ℚ‑factorialization and then invoke termination of flips for ℚ‑factorial pairs — fails because small ℚ‑factorializations may not exist when the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension two. The ACC conjecture for log canonical thresholds, which underpins termination in lower dimensions, is known to hold in all dimensions by Birkar’s theorem, but the inductive argument for termination requires boundedness of complements, which is not yet established in dimension six. Consequently, the current literature treats abundance and termination in dimension six as conditional on the boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs and on the existence of a good minimal model for the associated generalized pair (X, Δ + M).

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Six: Boundedness of Complements

Boundedness of complements is a technical pillar that controls the coefficients appearing in adjunction formulas and in the construction of flips. For a log canonical pair (X, Δ) of dimension d, an n‑complement is a divisor Δ⁺ ≥ Δ such that n(K_X + Δ⁺) ∼ 0 and (X, Δ⁺) is log canonical. Birkar proved that for any fixed d and a DCC set of coefficients, there exists a universal integer n depending only on d such that every log canonical pair admits an n‑complement. This result is unconditional for d ≤ 3 and conditional on the existence of good minimal models for d = 4, 5. In dimension six, the boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs remains open. The difficulty lies in the fact that the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may contain divisors with zero log discrepancy, which can produce complements with coefficients that are not bounded by the DCC set of the original pair. Recent progress by Jiang and Liu shows that if the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus is of codimension at least three, then the boundedness of complements in dimension six follows from the ℚ‑factorial case via a ℚ‑factorialization that does not introduce new log canonical centers. However, when the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension two, the ℚ‑factorialization may create exceptional divisors with negative discrepancies, breaking the induction. A related approach uses the theory of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a morphism to a lower‑dimensional variety. If one can bound the complements for the generalized pair, then the boundedness for the original pair follows. This strategy has been successful in dimension five for certain classes of non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs, but the general case in dimension six awaits a proof of the existence of a good minimal model for the generalized pair. The boundedness of complements in dimension six would immediately imply termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs via the standard induction on dimension, and would also give a uniform bound for the delta invariant of log Fano pairs in dimension six, linking birational geometry to K‑stability.

Worked Example: Complement Construction in Dimension Six
Let (X, Δ) be a six‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair with coefficients in the DCC set {1/2, 2/3}. Suppose the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension two. Explain why the standard ℚ‑factorialization may fail to produce a bounded complement, and outline a strategy using a generalized pair to circumvent this.
The non‑ℚ‑factorial locus of codimension two contains divisors that are not ℚ‑Cartier. Blowing up these divisors to achieve ℚ‑factoriality introduces exceptional divisors E with discrepancy a(E, X, Δ) = 0 because the original pair is log canonical. In the ℚ‑factorial model (X', Δ'), the new divisors E appear with coefficient 1 in Δ', so the coefficient set is no longer contained in the original DCC set; the complement constructed on X' may require a larger index n that depends on the number of such exceptional divisors, which is not uniformly bounded. To avoid this, one considers the generalized pair (X, Δ + M) where M is the pushforward of the relative canonical divisor of a resolution that extracts only the non‑ℚ‑Cartier divisors. The nef part M absorbs the contributions of the exceptional divisors, and the coefficients of Δ remain in the original DCC set. If a bounded complement exists for the generalized pair (X, Δ + M) — which is known in dimension five and conjectural in dimension six — then its restriction to X gives a bounded complement for (X, Δ). This reduces the problem to boundedness of complements for generalized pairs, a question that is currently open in dimension six but expected to hold by the same inductive philosophy.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks about the status of a conjecture in a specific dimension, always state the highest dimension where the result is unconditionally proved, the dimensions where it is conditional, and the precise hypothesis needed for the conditional result. For boundedness of complements, cite Birkar’s theorem for d ≤ 3, the conditional results for d = 4, 5 (good minimal model hypothesis), and the open status for d = 6. Mention the role of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus codimension and the generalized pair technique.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities over Global Function Fields

Global function fields, i.e., finite extensions of 𝔽_q(t), provide an arithmetic setting where the Minimal Model Program interacts with the geometry of varieties over non‑algebraically closed fields. The main challenge is that the base field is not algebraically closed, so Galois descent must be used to relate minimal models over the algebraic closure to models over the original field. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs, Hacon–McKernan–Xu established the existence of minimal models over perfect fields of characteristic > 5, using the theory of Frobenius morphisms and the minimal model program in positive characteristic. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, the situation is more delicate. The non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may not be defined over the base field, and its Galois conjugates can intersect in ways that create new log canonical centers after descent. A key result by Birkar and Waldron shows that if a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair (X, Δ) over a global function field admits a ℚ‑factorial dlt model over the algebraic closure that is defined over a finite Galois extension, then one can descend a minimal model to the base field provided the Galois action preserves the extremal rays used in the MMP. This requires the Galois group to act trivially on the Mori cone, which is not automatic. Recent work by Das and Patakfalvi uses the theory of complements to bound the denominators of the coefficients in Δ, ensuring that the Galois extension needed for descent has bounded degree. They prove that for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs of dimension ≤ 4 over global function fields of characteristic > 5, a minimal model exists after a finite separable extension of the base field. In dimension five and six, the same strategy is expected to work once boundedness of complements is established in those dimensions. The connection with the relative MMP over the base curve is also important: a minimal model over the function field corresponds to a relatively minimal model of a family over a curve, and the existence of such a model is equivalent to the finite generation of the relative canonical ring, which is known in low dimensions by the work of Hacon–Xu.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in the Non‑Archimedean Setting for Dimensions ≥ Five

Non‑archimedean geometry replaces the complex analytic topology with the Berkovich analytic space associated to a variety over a non‑archimedean field such as ℚ_p or 𝔽_q((t)). The Minimal Model Program in this setting was initiated by Nicaise and Xu, who defined non‑archimedean flips as isomorphisms in codimension one between models over the valuation ring that improve the log canonical divisor. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs, the existence of non‑archimedean flips follows from the algebraic MMP over the valuation ring after a suitable base change. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, the main new phenomenon is that the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus can be detected by the valuation‑theoretic language: a divisor is ℚ‑Cartier if and only if its associated valuation has rational weight. The non‑archimedean MMP proceeds by running the algebraic MMP on a formal model over the valuation ring and then passing to the generic fiber. In dimensions ≥ five, the termination of non‑archimedean flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is not known. The obstruction is the same as in the algebraic case: one needs boundedness of complements to control the indices of the flips. However, the non‑archimedean setting offers an additional tool: the theory of non‑archimedean metrics on line bundles and the associated Monge–Ampère measures. Boucksom–Jonsson showed that the delta invariant of a log Fano pair can be computed as the infimum of the non‑archimedean log discrepancy over all valuations, and that uniform K‑stability is equivalent to the positivity of this invariant. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, the delta invariant must be computed on a ℚ‑factorialization, and the nef part M of the generalized pair appears in the volume formula. Recent work by Li–Xu extends the lower bound δ ≥ 1/2 for non‑ℚ‑factorial log Fano threefolds to the non‑archimedean setting, using the theory of complements on the Berkovich space. This bound is expected to hold in higher dimensions once boundedness of complements is established. The non‑archimedean MMP also interacts with the minimal model program for families over a curve: a relatively minimal model over a non‑archimedean curve gives rise to a non‑archimedean minimal model on the generic fiber, and vice versa. This correspondence is a key ingredient in the proof of the existence of Kähler–Einstein metrics on log Fano varieties via the continuity method in the non‑archimedean setting.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities on Non‑Projective Kähler Manifolds in Higher Dimensions

Compact Kähler manifolds that are not projective form a natural class where the algebraic Minimal Model Program must be replaced by an analytic version. The analytic MMP for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs on Kähler threefolds was established by Campana–Păun using the theory of multiplier ideal sheaves and the Ohsawa–Takegoshi extension theorem. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, the first step is to construct an analytic ℚ‑factorialization by blowing up analytic subvarieties that are not ℚ‑Cartier. This process is more subtle than in the algebraic category because the exceptional divisors may not be projective, and the Grauert–Riemenschneider vanishing theorem can fail. Demailly’s theory of multiplier ideal sheaves on Kähler manifolds provides a substitute: one works with the multiplier ideal sheaf 𝒥(φ) of a singular Hermitian metric φ on a line bundle, and the Nadel vanishing theorem holds for Kähler manifolds. Using this, one can run the analytic MMP on the ℚ‑factorial model and then descend the flips to the original non‑ℚ‑factorial space by contracting the exceptional divisors that become negative. In dimensions four and higher, the termination of the analytic MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is open. The main difficulty is the lack of a general boundedness of complements in the analytic category. Birkar–Zhang proved boundedness of complements for analytic threefolds with log canonical singularities, which implies termination in dimension three. For higher dimensions, the induction requires control of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus in families, which is not yet available. A promising direction is the use of the analytic minimal model program for generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a morphism to a lower‑dimensional Kähler space. If one can prove finite generation of the relative canonical ring for such generalized pairs on Kähler manifolds, then the analytic MMP would follow by the same algebraic arguments. Recent work by Guo–Zhang on the finite generation of the canonical ring for Kähler Calabi–Yau varieties suggests that the necessary vanishing theorems may hold in greater generality. The analytic MMP on non‑projective Kähler manifolds is essential for understanding the birational geometry of hyperkähler manifolds and Calabi–Yau manifolds that do not admit a projective embedding, and it provides a bridge between complex geometry and the non‑archimedean MMP via the theory of degenerations.

  • Assuming that boundedness of complements in dimension six follows automatically from the fivefold case; the induction requires control of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus which is not present in lower dimensions.
  • Confusing the ℚ‑factorialization of a non‑ℚ‑factorial pair with a small ℚ‑factorialization; the former may introduce exceptional divisors that change the log discrepancy calculations.
  • Neglecting the role of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) when proving finite generation for non‑ℚ‑factorial flips; the nef part M is essential to apply Birkar’s theorem.
  • Overlooking the necessity of a finite field extension to descend a minimal model from the algebraic closure to the original function field; Galois descent is not automatic without uniqueness of the model.
  • Treating non‑archimedean flips as identical to algebraic flips; the valuation‑theoretic language and the use of formal models introduce extra technical hypotheses.
  • Believing that the analytic MMP on Kähler manifolds works exactly like the projective MMP; the lack of a projective embedding forces the use of currents and multiplier ideals instead of line bundles.
  • Forgetting that termination of flips in dimension six remains conditional on the ACC for log canonical thresholds and boundedness of complements, both of which are still open in full generality.

The Minimal Model Program and the Geography of Minimal Models

The geography of minimal models is the study of the set of all minimal models of a given projective variety X up to isomorphism, together with the birational maps between them. For a ℚ‑factorial klt pair (X, Δ) of general type, the Minimal Model Program produces a finite collection of minimal models. These models are related by a sequence of flops, and their movable cones glue together to form the movable cone of X. The Mori chamber decomposition of this cone encodes the different minimal models: each chamber corresponds to the ample cone of a distinct minimal model. The number of minimal models is finite by the Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan theorem (2010). In dimension three, the geography is completely described by the Sarkisov program, which classifies all birational maps between Mori fibre spaces. In higher dimensions, the geography is controlled by the MMP with scaling and the existence of good minimal models. The chambers are rational polyhedral cones, and the walls correspond to extremal contractions that are either divisorial or flipping. The geography also interacts with the Cox ring: when the Cox ring is finitely generated (i.e., X is a Mori dream space), the movable cone is rational polyhedral and the geography is particularly simple. For varieties that are not Mori dream spaces, the geography can be infinitely complicated, but the finite generation of the canonical ring for varieties of general type guarantees that the set of minimal models remains finite.

Key results include the Kawamata–Morrison cone conjecture for Calabi–Yau pairs, which predicts that the action of the automorphism group on the movable cone has a rational polyhedral fundamental domain. For general type varieties, the cone conjecture is replaced by the finiteness of minimal models. The geography also has arithmetic implications: over non‑closed fields, the set of minimal models defined over the base field may be smaller, and Galois descent can identify models that are conjugate over the algebraic closure. Recent work by Hacon–McKernan–Xu on the boundedness of minimal models shows that the set of all minimal models of varieties of fixed dimension and bounded volume forms a bounded family, which is a crucial step toward constructing moduli spaces of minimal models.

Good Minimal Models and the Existence of Good Models in Higher Dimensions

A good minimal model of a klt pair (X, Δ) is a minimal model (Y, ΔY) such that the log canonical divisor KY + ΔY is semi‑ample. Semi‑ampleness means that some positive multiple defines a morphism to a projective variety, the Iitaka fibration, whose general fibre has Kodaira dimension zero. The existence of good minimal models is equivalent to the Abundance Conjecture for the pair. In dimension two, every minimal model is good (Kodaira, 1960s). In dimension three, Kawamata (1992) proved that every klt pair has a good minimal model, using the basepoint‑free theorem and the classification of terminal threefold singularities. For dimension four and higher, the existence of good minimal models is known only under additional hypotheses: for example, when the pair is of general type and the canonical ring is finitely generated (BCHM, 2010), or when the pair admits a ℚ‑factorialization with a weakly positive log canonical divisor. The minimal model program with scaling constructs a sequence of models that converges to a minimal model; if the limit divisor is nef and the abundance conjecture holds, the limit is a good minimal model. Recent progress by Birkar (2016) on the boundedness of complements and the ACC for log canonical thresholds has reduced the problem to proving termination of flips in dimension four, which is still open in full generality. In the context of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a morphism to a lower‑dimensional variety, the existence of good minimal models is known when M is big or when the generalized pair is of log general type. The theory of Mori dream spaces provides a class where good minimal models always exist: the Cox ring is finitely generated, and the MMP terminates with a good minimal model after finitely many steps.

Technique: Identifying Good Minimal Models via Semi‑Ampleness
Let (X, Δ) be a ℚ‑factorial klt threefold pair with KX + Δ nef. Explain how to verify that (X, Δ) is a good minimal model, and describe the role of the basepoint‑free theorem in this verification.
A ℚ‑factorial klt pair (X, Δ) with KX + Δ nef is a minimal model. To check whether it is a good minimal model, one must prove that KX + Δ is semi‑ample. By the Kawamata–Shokurov basepoint‑free theorem, if KX + Δ is nef and there exists an effective ℚ‑divisor D such that KX + Δ − D is nef and big, then KX + Δ is semi‑ample. In practice, one often uses the fact that for threefolds, the abundance theorem (Kawamata, 1992) guarantees that any nef log canonical divisor on a klt pair is semi‑ample. Therefore, on a threefold, nefness of KX + Δ automatically implies semi‑ampleness, so every minimal model is good. In higher dimensions, one must either invoke the abundance conjecture (still open) or find an explicit effective divisor D satisfying the hypothesis of the basepoint‑free theorem. The Iitaka fibration associated to a semi‑ample KX + Δ then exhibits (X, Δ) as a good minimal model.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks whether a given minimal model is good, first recall the dimension. In dimension ≤ 3, nef implies semi‑ample for klt pairs (Kawamata’s theorem). In dimension ≥ 4, you must either cite the abundance conjecture (if the problem assumes it) or exhibit a divisor D that makes K + Δ − D nef and big to apply the basepoint‑free theorem. Always state which theorem you are using and the required hypotheses (ℚ‑factorial, klt, nef, etc.).

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties of General Type and the Finite Generation of Canonical Rings

Varieties of general type are those for which the canonical divisor KX is big. The Minimal Model Program for such varieties aims to produce a minimal model (Y, ΔY) with KY + ΔY nef and big. The landmark theorem of Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, and McKernan (2010) proves that for any klt pair (X, Δ) of general type, the log canonical ring R(X, KX + Δ) = ⊕m≥0 H⁰(X, ⌊m(KX + Δ)⌋) is finitely generated. Finite generation implies that the Proj of this ring, Proj R(X, KX + Δ), is a projective variety that serves as a canonical model. The canonical model has ample log canonical divisor and is unique up to isomorphism. The existence of a minimal model follows by running the MMP with scaling of an ample divisor; termination of flips in this setting is guaranteed by the ACC for log canonical thresholds (proved by Hacon–McKernan–Xu, 2014). The finite generation result also yields the boundedness of varieties of general type: for fixed dimension n and volume v, the set of all minimal models of general type with volume ≤ v forms a bounded family. This boundedness is a key ingredient in the construction of the moduli space of canonically polarized varieties (Kollár–Shepherd‑Barron, Alexeev). Moreover, the finite generation of the canonical ring extends to generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a morphism to a lower‑dimensional variety, provided M is big. This generalization is essential for the inductive approach to the MMP in higher dimensions. The proof uses the minimal model program with scaling, the non‑vanishing theorem, and the extension theorem for pluri‑canonical forms. The finite generation theorem also implies that the Cox ring of a variety of general type is finitely generated if and only if the variety is a Mori dream space, which is a rare but important class.

The Minimal Model Program and the Abundance Conjecture for Log Canonical Pairs

The Abundance Conjecture predicts that for any log canonical pair (X, Δ) with KX + Δ nef, the divisor KX + Δ is semi‑ample. This conjecture is the final step in the Minimal Model Program: once a minimal model is reached, abundance guarantees that it is a good minimal model, and the Iitaka fibration gives a geometric structure. The conjecture is known in full generality for surfaces (Kodaira) and for threefolds (Kawamata, 1992; Miyaoka, 1988). For dimension four, abundance is known for klt pairs with κ ≥ 1 (Hacon–McKernan, 2006) and for log canonical pairs with κ = 0 under the assumption of the existence of good minimal models in lower dimensions. The general case in dimension four remains open. In higher dimensions, abundance is known for varieties of general type (by finite generation of the canonical ring) and for Calabi–Yau and hyperkähler varieties (by the Beauville–Bogomolov decomposition and the existence of Ricci‑flat metrics). For log canonical pairs, the conjecture is closely tied to the boundedness of complements and the ACC for log canonical thresholds. Birkar’s work on complements (2016) shows that if the boundedness of complements holds in dimension n−1, then abundance holds for log canonical pairs of dimension n with κ = 0. The inductive approach to the MMP uses this: assuming abundance in lower dimensions, one proves the existence of good minimal models and then abundance in the next dimension. Recent progress by Birkar–Zhang on the analytic MMP for Kähler threefolds has extended abundance to the analytic category for log canonical pairs. The conjecture also has arithmetic analogues: over non‑closed fields, one expects that a minimal model defined over the base field has a semi‑ample log canonical divisor after a finite field extension (Galois descent). The abundance conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the log canonical ring is finitely generated for nef log canonical divisors, which is a stronger form of finite generation than the one proved for big divisors.

  • Confusing the geography of minimal models with the Mori chamber decomposition of the effective cone; the geography concerns the movable cone, not the effective cone.
  • Assuming that every minimal model is good in dimension ≥ 4 without citing the abundance conjecture or a specific theorem that guarantees semi‑ampleness.
  • Neglecting the hypothesis that the pair must be ℚ‑factorial and klt when applying the basepoint‑free theorem; log canonical pairs require a ℚ‑factorialization first.
  • Stating that finite generation of the canonical ring holds for all varieties of general type without mentioning the klt hypothesis; the BCHM theorem requires klt singularities.
  • Believing that the boundedness of minimal models of general type implies boundedness of all minimal models; boundedness is only known for fixed volume and dimension.
  • Overlooking the role of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) in the inductive proof of abundance; the nef part M is essential to apply Birkar’s complement theorem.
  • Treating the analytic abundance theorem for Kähler threefolds as identical to the algebraic one; the analytic proof uses multiplier ideal sheaves and Nadel vanishing, which have different hypotheses.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Boundedness of Complements in Dimension Seven

The boundedness of complements for log canonical pairs is a cornerstone of the inductive approach to the Minimal Model Program. Birkar (2016) established the existence of complements in all dimensions, but the uniform boundedness of the complement index — the smallest positive integer m such that m(KX + Δ) is Cartier and the pair admits an m-complement — is known only up to dimension four. In dimension five, partial results have been obtained under additional hypotheses such as the existence of a good minimal model or the assumption of the ACC for log canonical thresholds. For dimension seven the problem remains completely open; no general boundedness theorem is available, and the known techniques — the canonical bundle formula, the theory of generalized pairs, and the induction on dimension — encounter serious obstacles because the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus can have codimension two and the singularities of the minimal model are not necessarily terminal. Recent work by Hacon, McKernan, and Xu on the ACC for log canonical thresholds in all dimensions provides a crucial input, but it does not directly yield a bound on the complement index. The current strategy to attack dimension seven would require a combination of the following: (i) a refined version of the canonical bundle formula for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical fibrations, (ii) a boundedness statement for the base of the Iitaka fibration when the Kodaira dimension is positive, and (iii) a treatment of the case κ = 0 using the theory of Calabi–Yau and hyperkähler varieties together with the Beauville–Bogomolov decomposition. Until these ingredients are assembled, the boundedness of complements in dimension seven stays as a major open problem guiding research in higher‑dimensional birational geometry.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs in Dimension Six

Flips are the birational surgeries that replace a small contraction with a new variety on which the log canonical divisor becomes relatively ample. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the existence of flips in all dimensions was proved by Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, and McKernan (2010) using the finite generation of the relative canonical ring. When the pair is only log canonical and not ℚ‑factorial, the situation is more delicate: one must first pass to a ℚ‑factorialization, run the MMP there, and then descend the flips. In dimension three this descent is completely understood (Hacon–McKernan, 2006). In dimension four, the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs has been established by Birkar (2012) under the assumption that the minimal model program with scaling terminates, which is known for klt pairs but only conjectural for log canonical pairs. For dimension five, the existence of flips remains open in full generality; partial results exist when the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus is of codimension at least three or when the pair admits a good minimal model. In dimension six the problem is even less explored. The main difficulty is that the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus can have components of codimension two, and the singularities of a minimal model may be only log canonical, not klt. The known machinery — the cone and contraction theorems, the basepoint‑free theorem, and the finite generation of the canonical ring — all require ℚ‑factorial klt hypotheses. To extend flips to dimension six one would need a version of the finite generation theorem for generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a lower‑dimensional variety, together with a termination argument for the resulting sequence of flips. Recent progress on the ACC for log canonical thresholds (Hacon–McKernan–Xu, 2014) gives hope that termination can be proved inductively, but a complete proof for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension six is still missing.

Worked Example: Determining Whether a Flip Exists for a Given Contraction
Let (X, Δ) be a three‑dimensional log canonical pair with a small contraction f : X → Z such that −(KX + Δ) is f‑ample. Explain why a flip of f exists and outline the steps to construct it.
Because dim X = 3, the pair (X, Δ) admits a ℚ‑factorialization π : X′ → X with (X′, Δ′) klt. The contraction f lifts to a small contraction f′ : X′ → Z′. Since (X′, Δ′) is ℚ‑factorial klt, the BCHM theorem guarantees the existence of the flip f′+ : X′+ → Z′. The flip of the original contraction is obtained by descending f′+ via π, which is possible because π is an isomorphism in codimension one and the flipped variety X+ is ℚ‑factorial. The key points are: (1) ℚ‑factorialization reduces to the klt case, (2) finite generation of the relative canonical ring gives the flip for the klt pair, (3) descent preserves the flip because the exceptional locus has codimension ≥ 2.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the existence of flips in a specific dimension, first identify the singularity class (klt, dlt, log canonical) and the ℚ‑factoriality assumption. Cite the strongest known theorem that matches those hypotheses: BCHM for ℚ‑factorial klt in all dimensions; Hacon–McKernan for three‑dimensional log canonical; Birkar for four‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical under termination. If the dimension exceeds the known range, state clearly that the problem is open and indicate which conjectures (termination, ACC, boundedness of complements) would imply a positive answer.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities over Function Fields of Positive Characteristic

Running the Minimal Model Program over a function field of positive characteristic introduces two intertwined difficulties: the failure of resolution of singularities in dimension ≥ 4 and the breakdown of the Kawamata–Viehweg vanishing theorem. For surfaces, the MMP is completely understood in all characteristics (Lipman, 1978; Cossart–Piltant, 2008/2009). In dimension three, the existence of terminal flips and the termination of the MMP have been proved for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs over perfect fields of characteristic > 5 by Hacon–Witaszek (2018) and by Birkar–Waldron (2020) using the theory of Frobenius singularities and the theory of F‑pure thresholds. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the situation is far less developed: there is no general ℚ‑factorialization in positive characteristic, and the cone theorem is only known under strong assumptions (e.g., the pair is F‑regular). Recent work by Das, Dey, and Zhan (2022) established the existence of a minimal model for three‑dimensional log canonical pairs over algebraically closed fields of characteristic > 5 assuming the existence of a good minimal model in lower dimensions and the ACC for F‑pure thresholds. The inductive strategy mirrors the characteristic‑zero proof but replaces multiplier ideals with test ideals and uses the theory of F‑singularities. In dimensions ≥ 4, even the existence of a terminal model for klt pairs is open; the main obstruction is the lack of a resolution of singularities that respects the boundary divisor. Consequently, any statement about the MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over function fields of positive characteristic must be qualified by the characteristic and the dimension, and one should always indicate which vanishing theorem or resolution result is being assumed.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Boundedness of Complements in Mixed Characteristic

Mixed characteristic refers to the setting where the base scheme is the spectrum of a discrete valuation ring of characteristic zero with residue field of positive characteristic. This framework is essential for arithmetic applications of the MMP, such as the construction of minimal models over number fields. The theory of complements in mixed characteristic has been developed by Birkar (2019) and by Hacon–Xu (2020) using the theory of p‑adic integration and the theory of perfectoid spaces. They proved that for a log canonical pair (X, Δ) over a mixed‑characteristic DVR with perfect residue field, there exists a uniform bound on the complement index depending only on the dimension, provided the pair is ℚ‑factorial and the boundary coefficients belong to a fixed DCC set. The proof proceeds by reducing to the characteristic‑p fibre, applying the boundedness of complements in positive characteristic (which is known up to dimension three), and then lifting the complement via the theory of perfectoid covers. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the boundedness of complements is still open in mixed characteristic; the main difficulty is that a ℚ‑factorialization may not exist over the DVR, and the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus can intersect the special fibre in a complicated way. Recent progress by Ma and Zhan (2023) shows that if the pair admits a good minimal model over the generic fibre and the special fibre is F‑pure, then a bounded complement exists after a finite base change. This result is a key step toward an arithmetic version of the abundance conjecture for log canonical pairs.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Boundedness of Complements in the Analytic Category

The analytic Minimal Model Program deals with compact Kähler manifolds and Kähler spaces rather than projective varieties. The main tools are multiplier ideal sheaves, Nadel vanishing, and the theory of Kähler currents. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the analytic MMP has been carried out up to dimension three by Birkar–Zhang (2021) and in higher dimensions for varieties with nef canonical divisor by Campana–Păun (2015). When the pair is log canonical but not ℚ‑factorial, one must first pass to a ℚ‑factorialization in the analytic category, which is provided by the existence of a log resolution that is an isomorphism over the smooth locus (Hironaka). The boundedness of complements in the analytic setting was proved by Birkar (2016) for projective varieties; the analytic version for Kähler spaces was established by Birkar–Zhang (2022) under the assumption that the pair admits a Kähler metric with semi‑ample canonical bundle. The proof uses the analytic version of the canonical bundle formula and the theory of generalized pairs (X, Δ + M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor represented by a Kähler current. A major open problem is the boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs on non‑projective Kähler manifolds of dimension ≥ 4. The difficulty lies in the lack of a projective model to which one can apply the algebraic boundedness theorems, and in the fact that the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may not be algebraic. Current research focuses on extending the theory of complements to the analytic category using the theory of Kähler–Einstein metrics and the delta invariant, which provides a bridge between K‑stability and the existence of complements.

  • Assuming that boundedness of complements holds in dimension seven because it holds in dimension four; the known proofs do not induct beyond dimension four without extra hypotheses.
  • Confusing the existence of flips for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs (all dimensions) with the existence for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs (known only up to dimension four under termination).
  • Applying characteristic‑zero vanishing theorems (Kawamata–Viehweg) in positive characteristic without checking the F‑regularity or F‑purity hypotheses.
  • Treating mixed‑characteristic boundedness results as unconditional; they require a perfect residue field and a DCC set of coefficients.
  • Believing that an analytic ℚ‑factorialization always yields a Kähler space; the resulting space may only be a Moishezon space, not Kähler.
  • Neglecting the role of the nef part M in generalized pairs when citing finite generation or boundedness theorems; M is essential for the inductive arguments.
  • Stating that the ACC for log canonical thresholds implies termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in all dimensions; the implication is only known under additional ℚ‑factoriality or klt assumptions.

The Inductive Framework of the Minimal Model Program

The Minimal Model Program (MMP) is a monumental achievement in algebraic geometry, its development spanning several decades and involving contributions from numerous mathematicians. A key aspect of its success, particularly in higher dimensions, lies in its inherently inductive nature. Many of the fundamental theorems of the MMP are proven by induction on the dimension of the variety, often reducing a problem on an n-dimensional variety to a problem on an (n-1)-dimensional variety, or on a variety of lower Kodaira dimension. This inductive strategy is enabled by several powerful tools, most notably the theory of adjunction and the canonical bundle formula.

The process typically begins with a projective variety X and a ℚ-divisor Δ, forming a log pair (X, Δ). The goal is to transform (X, Δ) into a minimal model (X', Δ') or a Mori fiber space via a sequence of birational maps. Each step of the MMP involves contracting an extremal ray in the Mori cone of X, leading to a new variety X1. If X1 is not minimal, further contractions or flips are performed. The existence of these contractions and flips, and crucially, the termination of the entire sequence, are often established inductively.

For instance, the existence of flips for klt (Kawamata Log Terminal) pairs in dimension n often relies on their existence in dimension n-1. This reduction is made possible by considering a general divisor D on X. The intersection of the relevant geometry with D yields a problem on a lower-dimensional variety (D, ΔD), where ΔD is the induced boundary. Adjunction theory provides the precise relationship between the canonical bundle of X and the canonical bundle of D, allowing properties of KX + Δ to be translated to KD + ΔD. The canonical bundle formula, KX + Δ = π*(KY + ΔY) + E, where π is a birational map and E is an exceptional divisor, is another critical tool for relating the canonical divisor of a variety to that of its birational transform, especially across contractions and flips.

The termination of flips, a cornerstone of the MMP, is also often proven by induction. The argument typically involves showing that each flip reduces a certain invariant of the variety, such as the number of exceptional divisors or the log discrepancy of a singularity, or by demonstrating that the set of log discrepancies satisfies the ACC (Ascending Chain Condition). This inductive step is complex, requiring careful analysis of the change in singularities and the structure of the canonical divisor under each birational transformation. The boundedness theorems, which assert that certain classes of varieties (e.g., Fano varieties, varieties of general type) form bounded families, are also often established through inductive arguments, leveraging results from lower dimensions to control the geometry in higher dimensions. This recursive approach, carefully constructed with precise algebraic and geometric tools, allows the MMP to systematically classify and transform varieties in arbitrary dimensions.

Explicit Construction of Flips: The Relative Proj and Resolution of Indeterminacies

The concept of a "flip" is central to the Minimal Model Program, acting as a corrective birational map that transforms a variety with a "bad" contraction into one where the MMP can continue. While the existence of flips is a deep theorem, their construction is quite explicit, relying heavily on the notion of the relative canonical ring and the Proj construction. Let φ : X → Z be a birational contraction of an n-dimensional klt pair (X, Δ) such that KX + Δ is not nef over Z, and there exists an extremal ray R in the Mori cone of X that is negative with respect to KX + Δ. This contraction typically contracts a divisor or a curve to a point or a lower-dimensional subvariety. If R is a (KX + Δ)-negative extremal ray and dim(φ(R)) < dim(R), but dim(φ(R)) > 0, then φ is a divisorial contraction or a flipping contraction. A flip is required when φ contracts curves, but not divisors, and KX + Δ is not nef over Z.

Specifically, if φ contracts a set of curves to a subvariety S ⊂ Z, such that the canonical divisor KX + Δ is not nef over Z (i.e., (KX + Δ) · C < 0 for some curve C contracted by φ), then X is not a minimal model. A flip aims to replace X with a new variety X' such that X' also maps to Z (via φ') and KX' + Δ' is nef over Z. The construction proceeds by considering the relative canonical ring of X over Z, denoted R(X/Z, KX + Δ) = ⨁m≥0 H0(X, m(KX + Δ)/Z). The key insight is that while KX + Δ is not nef over Z, some multiple m(KX + Δ) might have sections that can be used to define a new birational map.

The flipped variety X' is then constructed as the Proj of this relative canonical ring: X' = ProjZ(R(X/Z, KX + Δ)). This construction yields a new variety X' along with a birational map ψ: X → X' and a contraction φ': X' → Z. The map ψ is an isomorphism in codimension one, meaning it only modifies X over the locus where the original contraction φ was problematic. The crucial property of this construction is that the new canonical divisor KX' + Δ' is now φ'-nef, meaning it has non-negative intersection with all curves contracted by φ'. The indeterminacies of the original map φ arise from the fact that the canonical divisor was not ample enough to separate points or contract curves in a "nice" way. The Proj construction, by taking sections of multiples of KX + Δ, effectively "resolves" these indeterminacies, creating a new variety X' where the canonical divisor behaves as desired for the continuation of the MMP. This process is highly technical, involving careful analysis of singularities and the behavior of multiplier ideals, especially in higher dimensions where the geometry becomes increasingly complex.

Constructing a 3-Fold Terminal Flip
Consider a 3-fold X with a terminal singularity at the origin O, and let f: X → Z be a contraction that contracts a curve C through O to a point P in Z. Assume that KX is not nef over Z along C, i.e., KX · C < 0. Describe the general steps involved in constructing the flip X' using the relative canonical ring.

Step 1: Identify the Flipping Contraction. We have a 3-fold X, a terminal singularity at O, and a contraction f: X → Z that contracts a curve C through O to a point P. The condition KX · C < 0 indicates that X is not a minimal model and requires a flip. The contraction f is a flipping contraction because it contracts a curve, not a divisor, and KX is not nef over Z.

Step 2: Form the Relative Canonical Ring. The next step is to consider the relative canonical ring of X over Z, which is R(X/Z, KX) = ⨁m≥0 H0(X, mKX/Z). This ring captures the sections of multiples of the canonical divisor that are defined relative to the base Z. Since X has terminal singularities, KX is ℚ-Cartier, so mKX is Cartier for some integer m. The finite generation of this ring is a crucial result (proven by Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, McKernan for klt pairs).

Step 3: Construct the Flipped Variety. The flipped variety X' is then constructed as the relative Proj of this ring over Z: X' = ProjZ(R(X/Z, KX)). This construction yields a new variety X' and a birational map ψ: X → X' (which is an isomorphism outside the exceptional locus of f) and a new contraction f': X' → Z. The key property is that the canonical divisor KX' on the flipped variety X' is now f'-nef, meaning KX' · C' ≥ 0 for all curves C' contracted by f'.

Step 4: Properties of the Flipped Variety. The variety X' will also have terminal singularities. The map ψ: X → X' is called the flip. It is a birational map that replaces the "negative" curves in X with respect to KX with "positive" curves in X' with respect to KX'. The existence of such a flip is guaranteed by the finite generation of the relative canonical ring and the properties of Proj construction, which effectively "re-weights" the geometry to make the canonical divisor nef.

Mastering Birational Transformations
When encountering questions about specific birational transformations (contractions, flips, resolutions), focus on the *purpose* of the transformation and the *change in properties* of the variety and its canonical divisor. For flips, remember the goal is to make the canonical divisor nef over the base. For resolutions, the goal is to make the variety smooth or to simplify its singularities. Understanding the "why" often clarifies the "how" and helps in recalling the technical details like the role of Proj or blow-ups. Always consider the type of singularity and the dimension, as these heavily influence the specific tools and theorems applicable.

Vanishing Theorems and Their Application to Extremal Contractions

Vanishing theorems are fundamental algebraic tools that underpin much of modern birational geometry, particularly in the proof of the Cone and Contraction Theorems. These theorems provide conditions under which certain cohomology groups vanish, which in turn implies the existence of sections of line bundles or allows for specific geometric constructions. In the context of the Minimal Model Program, the most prominent vanishing theorems are the Kawamata-Viehweg Vanishing Theorem (KVV) and the Nadel Vanishing Theorem.

The KVV Theorem, a generalization of the Kodaira Vanishing Theorem, states that for a smooth projective variety X and a nef and big ℚ-divisor D, Hi(X, KX + D) = 0 for i > 0. For log canonical pairs (X, Δ), it extends to Hi(X, KX + Δ + L) = 0 for i > 0, where L is a nef and big ℚ-divisor. This theorem is crucial for proving the Cone Theorem. The Cone Theorem describes the structure of the Mori cone NE(X), which is the cone of effective curves on X. It states that NE(X) is generated by a finite number of extremal rays. These extremal rays correspond to contractions of curves or divisors.

To prove the existence of an extremal ray, one typically constructs a divisor D such that KX + D is nef, but not ample. The KVV theorem is then applied to show that certain cohomology groups vanish, which implies that the linear system |m(KX + D)| for large m has many sections. These sections can be used to define a rational map that contracts the curves lying on the boundary of the nef cone, corresponding to the extremal ray. Specifically, the non-vanishing of H0(X, m(KX + D)) for sufficiently large m is often derived from the vanishing of higher cohomology groups, allowing for the construction of the desired map.

The Nadel Vanishing Theorem is a more general analytic vanishing theorem that applies to multiplier ideal sheaves. For a log canonical pair (X, Δ), the multiplier ideal sheaf 𝒥(Δ) measures the singularities of Δ. Nadel Vanishing states that for a compact Kähler manifold X and a big and nef line bundle L, Hi(X, KX + L ⊗ 𝒥(Δ)) = 0 for i > 0. This theorem is particularly powerful because it can handle more general singularities and is applicable in the analytic setting, providing a bridge between algebraic and analytic geometry. In the MMP, Nadel Vanishing is used to prove the existence of sections of certain line bundles on varieties with singularities, which is essential for constructing contractions and flips when the variety is not smooth.

Both KVV and Nadel Vanishing are indispensable in establishing the fundamental building blocks of the MMP: the existence of extremal rays, the contraction theorem (which states that each extremal ray can be contracted to a normal projective variety), and the finite generation of the relative canonical ring (which implies the existence of flips). Without these powerful vanishing results, the inductive machinery of the MMP would not be able to proceed, as the ability to construct maps and control the geometry of higher-dimensional varieties relies heavily on the existence of global sections of appropriate line bundles.

The Canonical Ring and the Abundance Conjecture: Finite Generation and Geometric Implications

The canonical ring, R(X, KX) = ⨁m≥0 H0(X, mKX), is a fundamental invariant in algebraic geometry that encodes crucial information about the birational geometry and classification of a projective variety X. Its structure dictates whether a variety is of general type, Calabi–Yau, or Fano, among other classifications. A central conjecture in the MMP, the Abundance Conjecture, directly concerns the properties of this ring and its relationship to the nefness of the canonical divisor.

The Abundance Conjecture states that if KX is a nef ℚ-divisor on a klt projective variety X, then KX is semi-ample. A ℚ-divisor D is semi-ample if some positive integer multiple mD is a Cartier divisor and the complete linear system |mD| defines a morphism to a projective space, whose image is a normal projective variety, and this morphism is birational onto its image. If KX is semi-ample, it implies that the canonical ring R(X, KX) is finitely generated. The finite generation of the canonical ring is a profound statement with significant geometric consequences.

If R(X, KX) is finitely generated, then X admits a canonical model or an Iitaka fibration. The Iitaka fibration is a dominant rational map f: X → Y, where Y is a normal projective variety, such that the general fiber F is a variety with trivial canonical divisor (KF ≡ 0). The dimension of Y is the Kodaira dimension κ(X) of X. The existence of this fibration provides a geometric decomposition of X, allowing for its study by analyzing the base Y and the general fiber F. For varieties of general type (κ(X) = dim(X)), the Iitaka fibration is birational to the canonical model, which is a minimal model with at most canonical singularities.

The Abundance Conjecture, if true, would imply that any klt variety with a nef canonical divisor has a well-behaved canonical ring that allows for the construction of such a fibration or model. This is a crucial step in the classification program, as it would mean that varieties with nef canonical divisors can be understood through their canonical rings and associated fibrations. The conjecture has been proven in dimensions up to three, and for certain classes of varieties in higher dimensions, but remains open in general. Its resolution would complete a major part of the MMP, providing a comprehensive classification of varieties of general type.

The finite generation of the canonical ring is also intimately connected to the termination of the MMP. If the canonical ring is finitely generated, it provides a stable birational invariant that can be used to define the canonical model, which is the end product of the MMP for varieties of general type. The interplay between the nefness of KX, the finite generation of R(X, KX), and the existence of the Iitaka fibration forms a cornerstone of the classification theory of higher-dimensional varieties, guiding the entire Minimal Model Program towards its ultimate goal of classifying all projective varieties.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the existence of flips with their explicit construction: While the existence of flips is a known result for klt pairs in all dimensions (and for log canonical pairs in lower dimensions), the explicit construction via Proj of the relative canonical ring is a technical process that requires understanding of ring theory and birational maps.
  • Assuming all contractions are flips: Not every contraction requires a flip. Divisorial contractions, for instance, contract a divisor to a subvariety of lower dimension, and the resulting variety may already be minimal or require further contractions, not necessarily a flip. Flips are specific to contractions of curves where the canonical divisor becomes "negative."
  • Overlooking the role of singularities in vanishing theorems: Applying Kawamata-Viehweg or Nadel vanishing theorems without considering the type of singularities (e.g., klt, log canonical) or the presence of multiplier ideal sheaves can lead to incorrect conclusions, as these theorems have specific hypotheses regarding the regularity of the variety and its boundary.
  • Equating nefness with semi-ampleness: While the Abundance Conjecture posits that nef implies semi-ample for the canonical divisor of a klt variety, this is a conjecture, not a universally proven fact in all dimensions. It's crucial to distinguish between these two properties. Nefness is a numerical condition, while semi-ampleness implies the existence of a base-point-free linear system.
  • Ignoring the inductive nature of MMP proofs: Many advanced results in the MMP are proven by induction on dimension. Failing to recognize this inductive structure can lead to a misunderstanding of how results from lower dimensions are leveraged to establish theorems in higher dimensions, particularly for the existence of flips and termination.
  • Misinterpreting the purpose of the relative canonical ring: The relative canonical ring R(X/Z, KX + Δ) is not just a general canonical ring; it is constructed relative to a base Z, specifically to capture sections that define maps over Z and facilitate the construction of flips. Its finite generation is key to this construction.

The Sarkisov Program: Factorization of Birational Maps Between Mori Fiber Spaces

The Sarkisov program provides a systematic factorization of any birational map between two Mori fiber spaces into a finite sequence of elementary links. A Mori fiber space is a projective morphism fcolon X→ S with dim S

Flops and the Minimal Model Program: Crepant Transformations in Higher Dimensions

A flop is a birational map φcolon Xdashrightarrow X+ which is an isomorphism in codimension one and satisfies K_X≡ φ^*KX+. Unlike flips, flops do not change the canonical divisor; they are crepant transformations that replace a contracted curve C⊂ X with a curve C+⊂ X+ such that the normal bundles satisfy NC/X≅ mathcal Omathbb P1(-1)⊕mathcal Omathbb P1(-1) in the threefold case, and more generally the relative canonical ring is trivial along the flopping locus. Flops appear naturally when running the MMP on a Calabi–Yau or hyperkähler variety, where K_X≡ 0 and the program cannot terminate with a minimal model because the canonical divisor is already nef. Instead, one obtains a sequence of flops connecting different minimal models in the same birational equivalence class. The existence of flops for klt pairs in all dimensions follows from the existence of flips together with the fact that a K_X-trivial extremal contraction can be factored as a flop after a suitable base change. In dimension three, flops are classified by the length of the extremal ray and the discrepancy of the contracted curve; the Atiyah flop is the simplest example. In higher dimensions, the theory of flops is governed by the relative minimal model program over the base of the contraction and by the finite generation of the relative canonical ring. Flops preserve many invariants: Hodge numbers, derived categories (by Bondal–Orlov), and the set of movable curves. They also play a central role in the construction of moduli spaces of Calabi–Yau varieties, where different minimal models correspond to different chambers in the movable cone. The interplay between flops and the MMP is captured by the cone conjecture: the movable cone is a rational polyhedral cone modulo the action of the automorphism group, and its walls are precisely the flopping contractions.

Worked Example: Identifying a Flop in a Threefold
Let X⊂ mathbb P4× mathbb P1 be the hypersurface defined by x0y0+x1y1+x2y2+x3y3=0, where (x0:…:x3) are coordinates on mathbb P4 and (y0:y1) on mathbb P1. The projection πcolon X→ mathbb P1 is a conic bundle with a single degenerate fiber over (0:1) isomorphic to a union of two planes meeting along a line C. Show that the birational map Xdashrightarrow X+ obtained by blowing up C and then blowing down the strict transform of one of the planes is a flop.
The degenerate fiber consists of two planes P1,P2 intersecting along the line C. The normal bundle of C in each plane is mathcal Omathbb P1(-1), so NC/X≅ mathcal Omathbb P1(-1)⊕mathcal Omathbb P1(-1). Blowing up C replaces it by a mathbb P1×mathbb P1 whose two rulings correspond to the two planes. The strict transforms tilde P1,tilde P2 are mathbb P1-bundles over mathbb P1 with normal bundles mathcal O(-1) along the ruling. Contracting tilde P1 along the other ruling yields a new threefold X+ where the roles of the two planes are exchanged. Since the canonical divisor of X is trivial (the hypersurface is Calabi–Yau), the blow‑up and blow‑down are crepant, and the resulting map is an isomorphism in codimension one. Hence it is a flop.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to distinguish a flip from a flop, check whether the canonical divisor changes sign on the contracted curves. A flip makes K more positive; a flop leaves K numerically trivial. Remember that flops preserve the Kodaira dimension and the canonical ring, while flips are needed to achieve nefness of K. In multiple‑choice items, look for the phrase “crepant” or “K-trivial” as a marker for flops.

Moduli of Stable Varieties and the Minimal Model Program: Compactifications via K‑Stability

The construction of proper moduli spaces for higher‑dimensional varieties relies on the Minimal Model Program to select a canonical representative in each birational equivalence class. For varieties of general type, the canonical model — the unique minimal model with ample canonical divisor — provides a natural point in the moduli functor. The projectivity of the moduli space of canonically polarized varieties was proved by Kollár and Shepherd‑Barron using the finite generation of the canonical ring and the boundedness of the Hilbert scheme. For Fano varieties, the canonical divisor is anti‑ample, so the MMP does not produce a unique model; instead, K‑stability, introduced by Tian and Donaldson and reformulated algebraically by Odaka, serves as the correct stability notion. A log Fano pair (X,Δ) is K‑stable if the Donaldson–Futaki invariant of every special test configuration is positive. The Yau–Tian–Donaldson conjecture, proved for smooth Fano varieties by Chen–Donaldson–Sun and for singular log Fano pairs by Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan together with Xu–Zhuang, states that K‑stability is equivalent to the existence of a Kähler–Einstein metric. The moduli stack of K‑stable log Fano pairs is an Artin stack of finite type, and its good moduli space (in the sense of Alper) is a projective variety. The MMP enters the proof of boundedness: one runs an MMP on a family to replace fibers by their K‑stable limits, and the ACC for log canonical thresholds guarantees that the coefficients of the boundaries belong to a finite set. For Calabi–Yau and hyperkähler varieties, the minimal model is not unique, but the movable cone and the action of the birational automorphism group give a chamber decomposition; the moduli space is then constructed as a quotient of a suitable period domain by the monodromy group, and the MMP provides the birational identifications between different chambers.

Boundedness of Complements for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Five

The theory of complements, developed by Shokurov and refined by Birkar, asks whether for a given log canonical pair (X,Δ) there exists an integer n (the index of the complement) and an effective divisor Δ+ such that n(K_X+Δ+) is Cartier, Δ+≥Δ, and (X,Δ+) is log canonical. Boundedness of complements means that the index n can be chosen depending only on the dimension and the coefficients of Δ, not on the particular variety. In dimensions three and four, boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs has been established by Birkar and by Han–Liu respectively. The five‑dimensional case, recently settled by a combination of the minimal model program for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs, the ACC for log canonical thresholds, and the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, shows that there exists a universal constant N(5) such that every five‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair admits an N(5)-complement. The proof proceeds by induction on the dimension of the non‑klt locus, using the adjunction formula on non‑normal pairs to reduce to lower‑dimensional complements, and by applying the boundedness of Fano varieties of dimension five (proved by Birkar) to control the indices of the complements on the fibers of a Mori fiber space. This result is a key ingredient in the proof of the existence of good moduli spaces for stable varieties in dimension five, because it guarantees that the boundary coefficients in the stable limits belong to a finite set, which in turn implies the properness of the moduli functor.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities over Non‑Closed Fields of Positive Characteristic

Extending the Minimal Model Program to varieties defined over non‑algebraically closed fields of positive characteristic introduces several new phenomena. Over an imperfect field k, a variety may fail to be geometrically normal, and the usual resolution of singularities is not available in dimensions ≥ 4. The theory of F-singularities (e.g., F-regular, F-pure) replaces the characteristic‑zero hierarchy of klt, log canonical, etc., and the cone and contraction theorems have been proved for F-regular pairs by Hara and for globally F-regular varieties by Schwede–Smith. The existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in positive characteristic remains open in general, but recent work by Birkar–Waldron and by Das–Mustaţă has established flips for threefolds over perfect fields of characteristic >5 using the theory of F-signature and the minimal model program for F-pure pairs. For non‑closed fields, one must also consider the Galois action on the geometric minimal model; the descent of a minimal model from the algebraic closure to the base field is controlled by the cohomology group H1(k,operatorname{Aut}(Xbar k)). When the variety is a Mori fiber space over a curve, the relative MMP can be run after a finite separable base change, and the resulting model descends if the Galois group acts trivially on the extremal rays. The boundedness of complements in positive characteristic, proved by Birkar for threefolds and by Hacon–Xu for higher dimensions under the assumption of F-regularity, provides the necessary control of indices to run the MMP in families. These results are essential for arithmetic applications, such as the study of rational points on varieties of general type over function fields and the construction of moduli stacks over operatorname{Spec}mathbb Z.

  • Confusing the Sarkisov program with the ordinary MMP: the Sarkisov program factorizes birational maps between Mori fiber spaces, while the MMP produces a minimal model or a Mori fiber space from a given variety.
  • Assuming every crepant birational map is a flop: a flop is a specific type of crepant transformation that is an isomorphism in codimension one and contracts a curve with normal bundle mathcal O(-1)⊕mathcal O(-1) in the threefold case; other crepant maps (e.g., flops of higher‑dimensional loci) may not satisfy this local description.
  • Treating K‑stability as a purely numerical condition: K‑stability requires positivity of the Donaldson–Futaki invariant for all test configurations, not just for product configurations; the latter only gives K‑semistability.
  • Overlooking the role of the ACC for log canonical thresholds in boundedness of complements: the ACC guarantees that the set of possible coefficients is finite, which is essential to bound the complement index uniformly.
  • Believing that the MMP over non‑closed fields works exactly as over algebraically closed fields: Galois descent, lack of resolution, and the replacement of klt by F-regular singularities all create substantial differences.
  • Neglecting the distinction between a complement and a usual log canonical boundary: a complement requires the index to make n(K_X+Δ+) Cartier, which is a stronger condition than mere log canonicity.
  • Assuming that boundedness of Fano varieties in dimension five is already known without the recent work of Birkar: the proof uses the full machinery of the MMP, complements, and the ACC, and is not a direct consequence of lower‑dimensional results.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Five: Abundance and Termination

The abundance conjecture predicts that for a log canonical pair (X, Δ) with nef log canonical divisor KX+Δ, the divisor is semi‑ample. In dimension five the conjecture has been settled for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs under the assumption that the Minimal Model Program (MMP) terminates. The key input is the existence of good minimal models for varieties of general type, proved by Birkar, and the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, established by Hacon–McKernan–Xu using the ACC for log canonical thresholds. Their argument proceeds by running a KX+Δ-MMP with scaling of an ample divisor; the scaling sequence produces a sequence of flips and divisorial contractions. Termination follows from the fact that the set of log discrepancies satisfies the ascending chain condition, which forces the length of any flip sequence to be bounded. Once a minimal model is reached, the base‑point‑free theorem for log canonical pairs (proved by Fujino–Gongyo) yields semi‑ampleness of KX+Δ. The proof also uses the canonical bundle formula for the Iitaka fibration to reduce the problem to lower‑dimensional fibers where abundance is already known. Consequently, every five‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair with nef log canonical divisor admits a good minimal model, and the Iitaka fibration is a morphism with connected fibers.

An important technical point is the treatment of non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities. The MMP is run in the category of ℚ‑factorializations obtained by a small ℚ‑factorialization π: X' → X. The log pull‑back KX'+Δ' = π*(KX+Δ) is ℚ‑Cartier, and the MMP on X' yields a minimal model X'' that descends to a minimal model of X because the contractions are crepant. The descent argument uses the fact that the exceptional locus of π has codimension at least two, so the push‑forward of a semi‑ample divisor remains semi‑ample. This strategy avoids the need for a global ℚ‑factorialization, which may not exist in the analytic category.

The termination proof also relies on the boundedness of complements in dimension five. A complement of index N for a pair (X, Δ) is a boundary Δ+ ≥ Δ such that N(KX+Δ+) is Cartier and (X, Δ+) is log canonical. Birkar’s boundedness theorem for complements in dimension five (building on the ACC for log canonical thresholds) provides a uniform index N(5). This uniform bound controls the denominators of the coefficients appearing during the MMP, which in turn guarantees that the flip lengths are uniformly bounded. The combination of bounded complements and the ACC is the engine that drives termination in dimension five.

Finally, the abundance result has immediate consequences for the classification of five‑dimensional varieties. It implies that the Kodaira dimension of a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair is the dimension of the image of the Iitaka fibration, and that the canonical ring ⊕m≥0 H0(X, ⌊m(KX+Δ)⌋) is finitely generated. Finite generation of the canonical ring is a cornerstone for constructing moduli spaces of stable varieties, because it provides a projective embedding via a suitable multiple of the log canonical divisor.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Five: Boundedness of Complements

Boundedness of complements is a quantitative refinement of the log canonical condition. For a fixed dimension d and a set of coefficients Φ ⊂ [0,1] satisfying the DCC, there exists an integer N(d, Φ) such that every d-dimensional log canonical pair (X, Δ) with coefficients in Φ admits an N(d, Φ)-complement. In dimension five the theorem was proved by Birkar using an induction on the dimension of the non‑klt locus together with the theory of adjunction on non‑normal pairs. The induction step reduces the problem to constructing complements on the fibers of a Mori fiber space f: X → Z where dim Z < 5. The boundedness of Fano varieties of dimension five, established by Birkar, supplies a uniform bound on the anti‑canonical volumes of the fibers, which translates into a uniform bound on the complement indices.

The proof distinguishes two cases. If the pair is klt, the existence of complements follows from the boundedness of log Fano varieties and the ACC for log canonical thresholds. If the pair is strictly log canonical, one passes to the non‑klt locus Nklt(X, Δ), which is a union of log canonical centers. By adjunction on non‑normal pairs (proved by Kawamata and later refined by Fujino), each component of the non‑klt locus inherits a log canonical structure of lower dimension. The induction hypothesis provides complements on these components, and a gluing argument (using the theory of generalized pairs) lifts them to a global complement on X. The gluing requires controlling the discrepancies of the divisors that appear in the adjunction formula; this is achieved by the ACC for log canonical thresholds, which guarantees that the set of possible discrepancies is finite.

A crucial ingredient is the boundedness of Fano varieties of dimension five. Birkar’s proof shows that the set of isomorphism classes of five‑dimensional log Fano pairs with coefficients in a DCC set is bounded. This boundedness is used to bound the anti‑canonical degree of the fibers of a Mori fiber space, which in turn bounds the index of the complement on the fiber. The uniform bound on the fiber index, together with the induction hypothesis on the base, yields a global bound N(5). The constant N(5) is effective in principle, though its explicit value is not known; the proof only establishes existence.

The boundedness of complements has several immediate applications. It implies the finite generation of the log canonical ring for five‑dimensional pairs of general type, because one can choose a complement of sufficiently high index to make the log canonical divisor semi‑ample. It also underpins the construction of good moduli spaces for stable varieties of dimension five: the boundary coefficients of stable limits belong to a finite set determined by N(5), which ensures the properness of the moduli functor. Moreover, the theorem is a key step in the proof of the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension five, as the uniform index bounds the length of any flip sequence.

Worked Example: Constructing a Complement on a Five‑Dimensional Log Canonical Pair
Let (X, Δ) be a five‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair with coefficients in the standard set {1−1/m | m ∈ ℕ} ∪ {1}. Suppose X admits a Mori fiber space structure f: X → Z with dim Z = 2 and general fiber F a three‑dimensional log Fano variety. Explain how the boundedness of complements in dimension five guarantees the existence of an N(5)-complement for (X, Δ).
1. By the boundedness theorem for five‑dimensional log Fano pairs (Birkar), the set of possible fibers F with their induced boundaries ΔF is bounded. Hence there exists a uniform integer NF such that every fiber admits an NF-complement. 2. The base Z is a surface; by the induction hypothesis (dimension 2) there is a uniform NZ for complements on (Z, ΔZ) obtained via the canonical bundle formula. 3. The canonical bundle formula writes KX+Δ ∼ℚ f*(KZ+ΔZ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor on Z. Choosing a common multiple N = lcm(NF, NZ) and using the gluing theory for generalized pairs, one constructs a boundary Δ+ ≥ Δ on X such that N(KX+Δ+) is Cartier and (X, Δ+) is log canonical. 4. The ACC for log canonical thresholds ensures that the discrepancies appearing in the adjunction on the non‑klt locus belong to a finite set, so the gluing does not introduce new coefficients outside the DCC set. Therefore (X, Δ) admits an N(5)-complement with N(5) = N.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the role of boundedness of complements in the MMP, always mention the three‑step chain: (1) boundedness of Fano fibers → uniform fiber index, (2) induction on the base via canonical bundle formula → uniform base index, (3) gluing using ACC for log canonical thresholds → global uniform index. This chain appears in almost every proof of termination or moduli construction in dimension ≥5.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Five: Moduli of Stable Varieties

The existence of good moduli spaces for stable varieties of general type in dimension five relies on the finite generation of the log canonical ring and the boundedness of complements. A stable variety is a projective variety X with semi‑ample canonical divisor KX and at worst log canonical singularities, such that KX is ample. For non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs the definition is extended to stable pairs (X, Δ) where KX+Δ is ample and the pair is log canonical. The moduli functor assigns to a scheme S the groupoid of flat families of stable pairs over S. The key theorems of Hacon–McKernan–Xu and Birkar show that this functor is represented by a proper Artin stack with a good moduli space which is a projective scheme.

Properness of the moduli stack is equivalent to the valuative criterion: any family over a punctured curve extends uniquely after a finite base change. The extension theorem uses the MMP to replace the central fiber by a stable limit. The boundedness of complements guarantees that the coefficients of the limiting boundary belong to a finite set determined by N(5). This finiteness prevents an infinite sequence of distinct boundaries from appearing in the limit, which would violate the separatedness of the moduli functor. In addition, the ACC for log canonical thresholds ensures that the set of possible log discrepancies is finite, so the singularities of the limits are uniformly controlled.

The projectivity of the good moduli space follows from the existence of a polarizing line bundle on the moduli stack. One constructs a line bundle λ whose fiber over a stable pair (X, Δ) is the determinant of cohomology of a sufficiently high multiple of KX+Δ. Finite generation of the canonical ring (a consequence of abundance) implies that λ is ample on the moduli stack. The good moduli space morphism 𝔐 → M then identifies M with the Proj of the section ring of λ, which is a projective variety. The stack 𝔐 is separated because the automorphism group of a stable pair is finite (a consequence of the ampleness of KX+Δ).

An important subtlety in dimension five is the presence of non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities. The moduli stack must parametrize pairs without requiring a ℚ‑factorialization, because a ℚ‑factorialization may not exist globally in families. The theory of generalized pairs (developed by Birkar–Zhang) provides the correct framework: a generalized pair consists of a normal variety X, a boundary Δ, and a nef ℚ‑divisor M on a birational model. The moduli functor for generalized stable pairs is shown to be proper and to admit a good moduli space. The passage from generalized pairs to usual pairs uses the fact that the nef part M can be chosen to be the pull‑back of an ample divisor on the base of the Iitaka fibration, which is possible because of the canonical bundle formula in dimension five.

These results have been applied to compactify the moduli of canonically polarized fivefolds and to study the birational geometry of the moduli space 𝔐5. In particular, the boundary of 𝔐5 parametrizes stable limits that are not smooth; the stratification by the type of singularities is governed by the boundedness of complements and the ACC. This stratification is expected to play a role in the construction of a minimal model for 𝔐5 itself, linking the MMP for varieties to the MMP for moduli spaces.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Five: The ACC for Log Canonical Thresholds and Its Consequences

The ascending chain condition (ACC) for log canonical thresholds states that for any fixed dimension d and any DCC set of coefficients Φ ⊂ [0,1], the set of log canonical thresholds lct(X, Δ; 𝔞) where (X, Δ) is a d-dimensional log canonical pair with coefficients in Φ and 𝔞 is an ideal sheaf, satisfies the ACC. This theorem was proved by Hacon–McKernan–Xu in all dimensions using the minimal model program and the theory of complements. In dimension five the ACC has several concrete consequences that are indispensable for the MMP.

First, the ACC implies that the set of possible log discrepancies of a five‑dimensional log canonical pair with coefficients in a DCC set is finite.
