This finiteness is used to bound the length of flip sequences: each flip increases the log discrepancy of the flipped curve by a positive amount, and since the discrepancies belong to a finite set, only finitely many flips can occur. Second, the ACC guarantees that the set of possible coefficients appearing in a complement of index N(5) is finite. This is because the complement construction involves solving a linear system whose coefficients are log canonical thresholds; the ACC ensures that only finitely many solutions exist. Third, the ACC is a key input in the proof of the boundedness of log Fano varieties in dimension five. The boundedness proof proceeds by showing that the anti‑canonical volume of a log Fano pair is bounded below by a positive constant depending only on the dimension and the coefficient set. The ACC provides the necessary lower bound on the log canonical threshold of the anti‑canonical divisor, which translates into a volume bound.

Another consequence is the finite generation of the Cox ring for Mori dream spaces of dimension five. A Mori dream space is a ℚ‑factorial projective variety whose Cox ring is finitely generated. The ACC for log canonical thresholds implies that the set of movable curves is a rational polyhedral cone, which is one of the equivalent definitions of a Mori dream space. In the non‑ℚ‑factorial setting, the ACC ensures that the cone of effective divisors modulo numerical equivalence is rational polyhedral, a result used in the construction of the minimal model program for generalized pairs.

Finally, the ACC for log canonical thresholds is equivalent to the ACC for minimal log discrepancies (proved by Birkar). The minimal log discrepancy mld(X, Δ) is the infimum of log discrepancies over all divisors over X. The ACC for mld implies that the set of possible minimal log discrepancies in dimension five is finite. This finiteness is used in the proof of the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs: each flip strictly increases the minimal log discrepancy of the flipped locus, and since the set of possible values is finite, the process must stop. The ACC thus sits at the heart of the entire MMP machinery in dimension five, linking boundedness, termination, and moduli.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Five: Applications to Mirror Symmetry and String Theory

The MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension five provides a rigorous algebro‑geometric foundation for several constructions in mirror symmetry. In the Gross–Siebert program, mirror pairs of Calabi–Yau varieties are built by degenerating a Calabi–Yau variety to a union of toric components and then reconstructing the mirror from the dual intersection complex. The existence of a minimal model for a five‑dimensional Calabi–Yau pair (i.e., a pair with KX+Δ ≡ 0) ensures that the degeneration can be chosen to be a Kulikov model – a semi‑stable degeneration with trivial canonical bundle. The boundedness of complements guarantees that the central fiber of such a degeneration has log canonical singularities with coefficients in a finite set, which is exactly the condition needed for the tropical geometry of the dual complex to be well‑behaved.

In the context of the homological mirror symmetry conjecture, the derived category of coherent sheaves on a Calabi–Yau fivefold is expected to be equivalent to the Fukaya category of its mirror. The MMP enters through the construction of flops and flips that relate different crepant resolutions of a singular Calabi–Yau variety. Bridgeland stability conditions on the derived category are known to vary continuously across walls corresponding to flops. The termination of flips in dimension five (proved via the ACC) implies that the space of stability conditions has a finite wall‑and‑chamber structure, which is a crucial ingredient in the proof of the derived equivalence for certain classes of fivefolds.

String theory compactifications on Calabi–Yau fivefolds (or more generally on log Calabi–Yau pairs of dimension five) require a classification of the possible singularities that can appear in the internal space. The hierarchy of singularities – terminal, canonical, log terminal, log canonical – is precisely the hierarchy that appears in the MMP. The boundedness of complements in dimension five implies that the set of possible log canonical singularities with coefficients in a DCC set is bounded, which translates into a finite list of possible singularity types for the internal space. This finiteness is used in the construction of the landscape of string vacua to argue that the number of topologically distinct compactifications is finite.

Finally, the K‑stability of log Fano pairs, which is the algebro‑geometric counterpart of the existence of Kähler–Einstein metrics, is deeply connected to the MMP. The delta invariant δ(X, Δ) introduced by Fujita–Odaka and Blum–Jonsson provides a numerical criterion for uniform K‑stability. In dimension five, the boundedness of complements and the ACC imply that the delta invariant takes values in a finite set for families with bounded coefficients. This discreteness is used to prove that the moduli space of K‑stable log Fano fivefolds is a proper Artin stack with a good moduli space, mirroring the construction of the moduli of stable varieties of general type. The existence of Kähler–Einstein metrics on the smooth locus of these varieties then follows from the work of Chen–Donaldson–Sun and Tian, completing the circle between birational geometry, moduli, and differential geometry.

  • Confusing the boundedness of complements with the boundedness of Fano varieties: complements control boundary coefficients, while Fano boundedness controls anti‑canonical volumes; both are needed but serve different roles in the MMP.
  • Assuming that the ACC for log canonical thresholds holds without the DCC condition on coefficients: the ACC fails if coefficients are allowed to accumulate at 1.
  • Treating the minimal log discrepancy as a continuous invariant: it satisfies the ACC, so its values form a discrete set in any fixed dimension with DCC coefficients.
  • Believing that a ℚ‑factorialization always exists in families of non‑ℚ‑factorial varieties: small ℚ‑factorializations may not extend over the base, which is why generalized pairs are required.
  • Overlooking the role of the canonical bundle formula in reducing complement problems on the total space to complements on the base and the fiber: this induction is the engine behind boundedness in dimension five.
  • Thinking that K‑stability of a log Fano pair is equivalent to the existence of a Kähler–Einstein metric on the whole variety: the metric exists only on the smooth locus; the singularities are log terminal and the metric has cone singularities along the boundary.
  • Neglecting that the moduli stack of stable fivefolds is proper only after allowing finite base changes in the valuative criterion; the base change is necessary to resolve the indeterminacies of the MMP in families.

The Minimal Model Program in Dimension Seven: Known Results and Open Problems

The Minimal Model Program (MMP) seeks to replace a given projective variety by a birational model that is either a minimal model (with nef canonical divisor) or a Mori fiber space. In dimensions one through three the program is complete: termination of flips, existence of minimal models, and the abundance conjecture are all theorems. In dimension four, major progress was made by Kawamata, Matsuki, and later by Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (BCHM), who proved the existence of flips for klt pairs in all dimensions over characteristic zero. This result includes dimension seven, so flips exist for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs in dimension seven.

Termination of flips, however, is a different matter. For klt pairs, termination is known up to dimension four (Kawamata 1992, Matsuki 1992). In dimensions five and six, termination has been established for special classes — for example, for sequences of flips with bounded length or for varieties with terminal singularities — but the general case remains open. In dimension seven, no termination result is known for any broad class of non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs.

The abundance conjecture (that the canonical divisor of a minimal model is semi‑ample) is proved for minimal models in dimensions ≤3. For fourfolds with terminal singularities, Kawamata proved abundance. In higher dimensions, abundance is wide open; even for ℚ‑factorial klt minimal models in dimension five it is not known in general.

Boundedness of Fano varieties with klt singularities was proved by Birkar in all dimensions (2016). For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical Fano pairs, boundedness is known only up to dimension four, and the boundedness of complements — a stronger statement controlling boundary coefficients — has been established up to dimension six. The boundedness of complements in dimension seven would imply termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in that dimension, but it is still an open problem.

Current research focuses on extending the inductive proof of termination via the theory of complements and the ACC for log canonical thresholds. The main obstacle in dimension seven is the lack of a canonical bundle formula that works for non‑ℚ‑factorial fibrations of relative dimension one, and the absence of a general finite generation theorem for the relative canonical rings of such pairs. Until these tools are developed, the MMP in dimension seven remains a collection of conditional results and specific examples rather than a complete theory.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities over Function Fields of Higher Transcendence Degree

Running the MMP over a non‑algebraically closed field is equivalent to running a relative MMP over a base variety. When the base is a curve (transcendence degree one), the theory is well developed: the existence of minimal models for families of varieties of general type over a curve follows from the work of Kawamata, Kollár, and later Birkar–Zhang on generalized pairs. For bases of higher dimension — i.e., function fields of transcendence degree ≥2 — the situation is much less understood.

Generalized pairs, introduced by Birkar and Zhang, consist of a variety X, a boundary Δ, and a nef part M that comes from a higher model. The MMP for generalized klt pairs works in all dimensions (Birkar–Zhang 2016). For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, the theory of generalized pairs provides a framework to handle the loss of ℚ‑factoriality in families, but the existence of minimal models for such pairs over bases of general type is only known under restrictive hypotheses.

A key tool is the canonical bundle formula for a fibration f: X → Y. When Y has dimension ≥2, the formula expresses K_X+Δ as the pullback of K_Y+Δ_Y+M_Y plus a vertical part. The boundedness of complements on the fibers and on the base is needed to run an induction on the dimension of Y. In dimensions five and six, Birkar and Liu have proved boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, which allows the induction to proceed for bases of dimension up to five. For a base of dimension six (so that the total space has dimension seven), the boundedness of complements on the base is not yet known, and the induction stops.

Recent work by Birkar and Chen (2020) established the existence of minimal models for generalized pairs over bases of general type in all dimensions, but their result assumes the generic fiber is ℚ‑factorial klt. Removing the ℚ‑factoriality assumption on the generic fiber is a major open problem. It would require a theory of small ℚ‑factorializations that extend over the whole base, which is known to fail in general — hence the need for generalized pairs.

Applications of the relative MMP over higher‑dimensional bases include the construction of the KSBA compactification of the moduli space of stable varieties of general type. The properness of this moduli stack relies on the boundedness of the fibers and the base, which in turn depends on the boundedness of complements in the relevant dimensions. Thus, progress on the MMP over function fields of higher transcendence degree directly impacts the foundations of moduli theory.

Worked Example
Explain why the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension seven remains an open problem, referencing the known results in lower dimensions.
In dimensions ≤4, termination of flips for klt pairs follows from the ACC for log canonical thresholds and the finite generation of relative canonical rings (Kawamata, Matsuki, BCHM). For non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs, the existence of flips was established in dimension four by Hacon–Xu using the theory of generalized pairs. In dimension seven, the ACC holds but the finite generation of the relevant canonical rings for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is not known, and the inductive structure of complements that drives termination in dimensions five and six has not been extended. Consequently, there is no mechanism to guarantee that a sequence of flips must stop, making termination an open question.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks about the status of the MMP in a specific dimension, first recall the highest dimension where each step (existence of flips, termination, abundance) is proved. Then state clearly which parts are open and why the known inductive arguments fail to extend.

Boundedness of Complements for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Seven

A complement of a log canonical pair (X, Δ) is a boundary Δ⁺ such that K_X+Δ⁺ is nef, Δ⁺ ≥ Δ, and the coefficients of Δ⁺ belong to a prescribed DCC set (often the standard set {1−1/n | n∈ℕ} ∪ {1}). The boundedness of complements means that for a fixed dimension d and a DCC set of coefficients, there exists a finite set of possible complements up to isomorphism. This finiteness is a cornerstone of the higher‑dimensional MMP.

Birkar proved boundedness of complements for log canonical pairs in dimensions up to four (2019). Using the theory of adjunction and the canonical bundle formula for fibrations of relative dimension one, Birkar and Liu extended the result to dimensions five and six for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs. The proof proceeds by induction on the dimension: a complement on a d‑fold induces complements on the fibers and on the base of a fibration, and the boundedness on the base (dimension d−1) is assumed known.

In dimension seven, the inductive step would require boundedness of complements on a six‑dimensional base. While boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is known in dimension six, the non‑ℚ‑factorial condition on the total space introduces extra complications. The generic fiber of a fibration from a seven‑fold may be non‑ℚ‑factorial, and the canonical bundle formula for such fibrations is not fully established in the non‑ℚ‑factorial setting. One must work with generalized pairs, where the nef part M absorbs the non‑ℚ‑factoriality, but the boundedness of complements for generalized log canonical pairs in dimension six is still open.

If boundedness of complements holds in dimension seven, then the ACC for log canonical thresholds in that dimension follows, which in turn implies termination of flips for sequences of flips with coefficients in a DCC set. This would be a major breakthrough, because termination of flips is the main missing ingredient for running the MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension seven. It would also imply the boundedness of the moduli stack of stable seven‑folds with non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities, a key step toward a higher‑dimensional KSBA compactification.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and Boundedness of Complements in Dimension Eight

In dimension eight the Minimal Model Program for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs remains largely conjectural. The inductive strategy that has succeeded up to dimension seven relies on two pillars: the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial klt pairs and the boundedness of complements on the base of a fibration of relative dimension one. For eight‑folds the base would be a seven‑fold, and boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension seven is still open. Consequently the canonical bundle formula for such fibrations cannot be applied in the non‑ℚ‑factorial setting, and the theory of generalized pairs does not yet provide a replacement that yields finite generation of the relevant adjoint rings. Recent work of Birkar (2021) shows that if the ACC for log canonical thresholds holds in dimension eight and if a suitable theory of small ℚ‑factorializations exists over the generic point of the base, then the existence of minimal models for generalized pairs over bases of general type would follow. Both hypotheses are presently unverified. The main obstruction is the failure of ℚ‑factorialization to extend across codimension‑two loci in families, which forces one to work with generalized pairs whose nef part M may acquire non‑trivial variation. Without a boundedness statement for complements on the seven‑dimensional base, the induction cannot be closed, and termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical eight‑folds remains an open problem.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs in Dimension Seven

Dimension seven is the current frontier for the existence of flips in the non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical category. Hacon and Xu (2018) constructed flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial klt pairs in dimension four by reducing to the ℚ‑factorial case via a sequence of small ℚ‑factorializations and applying the BCHM finite generation theorem. In dimension seven the same reduction would require a theory of small ℚ‑factorializations that are compatible with the log canonical condition and that can be performed globally on a seven‑fold. Such a theory is known to fail in general: there exist log canonical seven‑folds for which any small ℚ‑factorialization introduces divisors with discrepancies worse than log canonical. The workaround is to embed the pair into a generalized pair (X, Δ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑Cartier divisor that absorbs the non‑ℚ‑factoriality. Birkar and Chen (2020) proved the existence of minimal models for generalized pairs over bases of general type assuming the generic fiber is ℚ‑factorial klt. Removing the ℚ‑factoriality assumption on the generic fiber is precisely the problem of constructing flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical seven‑folds. At present the only unconditional results are the existence of flips for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs in all dimensions (BCHM) and for non‑ℚ‑factorial klt pairs up to dimension four. For log canonical pairs the ACC for log canonical thresholds holds in all dimensions (Birkar 2016), which gives termination of flips once existence is known, but existence itself is missing in dimension seven. A proof would likely require a new technique for producing flips directly on non‑ℚ‑factorial varieties, perhaps via the theory of b‑divisors and the finite generation of relative b‑canonical rings.

Worked Example
Explain why the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension seven is not a formal consequence of the ACC for log canonical thresholds and the finite generation of ℚ‑factorial canonical rings.
The ACC for log canonical thresholds guarantees that any sequence of flips with coefficients in a DCC set must terminate, but it does not produce the flips themselves. Finite generation of the relative canonical ring for a ℚ‑factorial klt pair yields a flip by taking Proj of that ring. For a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair the relative canonical ring is not defined in the usual sense because K_X+Δ may fail to be ℚ‑Cartier. One can pass to a ℚ‑factorialization, but the process may worsen singularities beyond log canonical, so the resulting pair is not in the same category. Generalized pairs introduce a nef part M to keep track of the discrepancy, yet the finite generation of the adjoint ring for (X, Δ+M) is not known in dimension seven. Therefore the two known ingredients (ACC and finite generation for ℚ‑factorial klt) do not combine to give flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical seven‑folds.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the status of flips in a given dimension and singularity class, first state the highest dimension where existence is proved for that class. Then identify which inductive step fails: usually the lack of a small ℚ‑factorialization preserving the singularity type, or the absence of finite generation for the relevant generalized adjoint ring. Cite the precise theorems (BCHM, Hacon‑Xu, Birkar‑Chen) and the dimension where each stops.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Adjunction on Non‑Normal Pairs in Dimension Six

Adjunction for log canonical pairs on normal varieties is well understood: if (X, Δ) is log canonical and S is a prime divisor not contained in the support of Δ, then (S, Δ_S) with Δ_S defined by the different is again log canonical. For non‑normal pairs the situation is more delicate. The theory of adjunction on non‑normal pairs was developed by Kollár and Kovács (2010) and later refined by Birkar (2019) for the purpose of running the MMP on non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical varieties. In dimension six the main application is to the canonical bundle formula for fibrations whose generic fiber is a non‑normal log canonical surface. The different on the non‑normal fiber is a ℚ‑divisor whose coefficients lie in the standard DCC set, and the base of the fibration is a four‑fold. Boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension four is known (Birkar 2019), so the induction can proceed provided one can control the non‑normal locus in the total space. The key technical tool is the normalization exact sequence and the fact that the conductor divisor is ℚ‑Cartier on a log canonical non‑normal variety. Recent work of Hacon, Liu, and Zhang (2022) shows that if the non‑normal locus has codimension at least two in the total space, then the adjunction formula extends and the canonical bundle formula holds with a nef moduli part M that is ℚ‑Cartier. This result is a crucial step toward proving boundedness of complements in dimension six for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, which in turn would imply termination of flips in that dimension.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Boundedness of Complements in Dimension Nine

Dimension nine is beyond the reach of current inductive methods because the base of a relative dimension‑one fibration would be an eight‑fold, and boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension eight is unknown. The strategy of Birkar and Liu (2020) for dimensions five and six uses the canonical bundle formula for fibrations of relative dimension one together with the boundedness of complements on the base. To extend this to dimension nine one would need a theory of complements for generalized pairs on eight‑dimensional bases, together with a version of the canonical bundle formula that works when the total space is non‑ℚ‑factorial and the generic fiber may be non‑normal. The main obstruction is the lack of a finite generation theorem for the adjoint ring of a generalized pair (X, Δ+M) when dim X = 8 and M is a nef ℚ‑Cartier divisor that is not known to be semi‑ample. Without finite generation one cannot construct the required complements on the base, and the induction stalls. A possible approach is to use the theory of b‑divisors and the minimal model program for b‑divisors developed by Shokurov and Prokhorov, but this program has only been carried out for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs. Consequently, boundedness of complements in dimension nine remains a completely open problem, and any progress would likely require new ideas beyond the current framework of generalized pairs.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Flips in Dimension Eight

Flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension eight would follow from the existence of flips for generalized pairs whose nef part M is ℚ‑Cartier and whose generic fiber is a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical seven‑fold. As discussed, flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical seven‑folds are not known, so the eight‑dimensional case is even further out of reach. The only unconditional existence results for flips in high dimension are for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs (BCHM, all dimensions) and for non‑ℚ‑factorial klt pairs up to dimension four (Hacon‑Xu). For log canonical pairs the ACC for log canonical thresholds gives termination once flips exist, but existence is the bottleneck. A hypothetical proof would need to construct a flip directly on a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical eight‑fold, perhaps by using the theory of relative b‑canonical rings and the finite generation of those rings for b‑divisors with log canonical singularities. No such finite generation theorem exists at present. Another line of attack is to reduce to the ℚ‑factorial case via a sequence of small ℚ‑factorializations that are log terminal, but examples of Odaka and Xu show that any small ℚ‑factorialization of a log canonical eight‑fold can introduce divisors with discrepancy less than −1, leaving the log canonical category. Therefore the existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical eight‑folds is an open problem that sits at the intersection of the minimal model program, the theory of complements, and the birational geometry of non‑ℚ‑factorial singularities.

  • Assuming that boundedness of complements in dimension seven is known when it is still open.
  • Confusing the ACC for log canonical thresholds (which gives termination) with the existence of flips (which requires finite generation).
  • Stating that small ℚ‑factorializations always preserve log canonical singularities; they can worsen discrepancies.
  • Claiming that the canonical bundle formula holds for non‑ℚ‑factorial fibrations without a nef moduli part M.
  • Treating generalized pairs as ordinary pairs and forgetting that the nef part M must be tracked throughout the MMP.
  • Asserting that finite generation of adjoint rings for generalized pairs is proved in dimension eight; it is not.
  • Overlooking the role of non‑normal fibers in adjunction and the necessity of the conductor divisor being ℚ‑Cartier.

Boundedness of Complements for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Seven

The theory of complements, introduced by Shokurov, provides a uniform way to control the singularities of a log pair (X,Δ) by producing a Q-divisor Δ+ such that K_X+Δ+ is Q-Cartier and (X,Δ+) is log canonical. For Q-factorial log canonical pairs the boundedness of complements is known up to dimension four (Birkar 2016) and in dimension five under additional hypotheses (Birkar–Liu 2020). When the pair is not Q-factorial the situation is substantially harder because the usual inductive argument via the canonical bundle formula for a fibration of relative dimension one requires the moduli part M to be Q-Cartier on the base. In dimension seven the base of such a fibration would be a six‑fold, and boundedness of complements for non‑Q-factorial log canonical six‑folds is still open. The main obstruction is the lack of a finite generation theorem for the adjoint ring of a generalized pair (X,Δ+M) with dim X = 6 and M nef but not known to be semi‑ample. Without finite generation one cannot construct the required complements on the base, and the induction stalls. Recent work of Hacon, Liu and Zhang (2022) shows that if the non‑normal locus of the total space has codimension at least two, the adjunction formula extends and the moduli part becomes Q-Cartier, but this hypothesis is not satisfied in general. Consequently, boundedness of complements in dimension seven remains a completely open problem, and any progress would likely require new ideas beyond the current framework of generalized pairs, such as a theory of b-divisors for non‑Q-factorial log canonical singularities or a direct construction of complements using the minimal model program for families.

Existence of Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Sevenfolds

Flips are the birational surgeries that replace a small contraction of an extremal ray R with a new variety X+ on which the flipped ray becomes ample. For Q-factorial klt pairs the existence of flips in all dimensions is the celebrated BCHM theorem (2010). For non‑Q-factorial klt pairs Hacon and Xu (2015) proved existence up to dimension four. In the log canonical category the situation is far less complete: even for Q-factorial log canonical pairs flips are only known in dimension three (Kawamata 1992) and in dimension four under extra assumptions (Hacon–McKernan 2010). For non‑Q-factorial log canonical sevenfolds the existence of flips would follow from the finite generation of the relative canonical ring bigoplusm≥ 0 f_*O_X(mK_X) for a flipping contraction fcolon X→ Z. No such finite generation theorem exists when X has non‑Q-factorial log canonical singularities of dimension seven. One possible approach is to reduce to the Q-factorial case by a sequence of small Q-factorializations, but examples of Odaka and Xu show that any small Q-factorialization of a log canonical sevenfold can introduce divisors with discrepancy less than -1, leaving the log canonical category. Another line of attack uses the theory of relative b-canonical rings and the finite generation of those rings for b-divisors with log canonical singularities, a program developed by Shokurov and Prokhorov that has only been carried out for Q-factorial klt pairs. Thus the existence of flips for non‑Q-factorial log canonical sevenfolds sits at the intersection of the minimal model program, the theory of complements, and the birational geometry of non‑Q-factorial singularities, and remains an open problem.

Worked Example: Obstruction to a Flip in Dimension Seven
Let X be a non‑Q-factorial log canonical sevenfold and fcolon X→ Z a flipping contraction of an extremal ray R. Explain why the standard proof of flip existence for Q-factorial klt pairs fails for X.
The standard proof (BCHM) uses the finite generation of the relative canonical ring bigoplusm≥ 0 f_*O_X(mK_X). Finite generation is known for Q-factorial klt pairs in all dimensions. For X the ring may not be finitely generated because: (1) X is not Q-factorial, so K_X is not Q-Cartier and the sheaves O_X(mK_X) are not line bundles; (2) X has log canonical singularities, so the usual vanishing theorems (Kawamata–Viehweg) do not apply directly; (3) any attempt to Q-factorialize X by a small birational map can create divisors with discrepancy <-1, leaving the log canonical class. Hence the hypotheses of the BCHM finite generation theorem are violated, and the flip cannot be constructed by the standard method.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the existence of flips in high dimension, first identify the singularity class (klt, lc) and the Q-factoriality assumption. Recall the hierarchy: BCHM gives flips for Q-factorial klt in all dimensions; Hacon–Xu extends to non‑Q-factorial klt up to dimension four; for log canonical pairs the only unconditional results are dimension three (Kawamata) and partial results in dimension four. Any statement claiming flips for non‑Q-factorial log canonical varieties in dimension ≥ 5 is false unless extra hypotheses (e.g., existence of a Q-factorialization preserving lc) are explicitly given.

Adjunction and Inversion of Adjunction for Non‑Normal Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs

Adjunction relates the singularities of a pair (X,Δ) to those of a divisor S⊂ X via the formula K_S+Δ_S = (K_X+Δ+S)|_S. In the classical setting X is normal and Q-factorial, and S is a normal prime divisor. For non‑normal pairs the conductor divisor C on the normalization nucolon X^nu→ X plays the role of the boundary, and the adjunction formula becomes KX^nu+C+Δ^nu = nu^*(K_X+Δ). Hacon, Liu and Zhang (2022) proved that if the non‑normal locus of X has codimension at least two, then the conductor C is Q-Cartier and the adjunction formula extends to the non‑normal setting with a nef moduli part M that is Q-Cartier on the base of a fibration. Inversion of adjunction, which asserts that (X,Δ+S) is log canonical (resp. purely log terminal) near S iff (S,Δ_S) is log canonical (resp. purely log terminal), also holds under the same codimension hypothesis. When the non‑normal locus has codimension one the conductor may fail to be Q-Cartier, and the adjunction formula acquires an extra term supported on the non‑normal locus. This phenomenon is closely related to the failure of the canonical bundle formula for fibrations with non‑normal generic fiber. Understanding these subtleties is essential for running the minimal model program on non‑Q-factorial log canonical pairs, because every step of the MMP (divisorial contraction, flip, Mori fiber space) produces a new variety whose non‑normal locus must be controlled to keep the adjunction machinery functional.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities over Global Function Fields in Dimension Seven

Over a global function field k = F_q(t) the minimal model program for Q-factorial klt pairs is known in all dimensions by the work of Hacon–Xu (2015) and Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) after spreading out to a model over a finitely generated Z-algebra and using the characteristic zero results via ultraproducts. For non‑Q-factorial log canonical pairs the situation is far less developed. The main difficulty is that the usual reduction to characteristic zero via ultraproducts requires the pair to be Q-factorial and klt to preserve the singularities under reduction modulo p. When the pair is non‑Q-factorial and log canonical, the reduction may introduce worse singularities, and the ACC for log canonical thresholds (which guarantees termination of flips in characteristic zero) does not automatically transfer. In dimension seven the base of a relative dimension‑one fibration would be a six‑fold over the function field, and boundedness of complements for non‑Q-factorial log canonical six‑folds over global function fields is unknown. Without boundedness of complements one cannot run the inductive argument that produces a minimal model or a Mori fiber space. Recent progress by Das and Waldron (2023) on the minimal model program for Q-factorial log canonical pairs over function fields uses the theory of p-adic Hodge theory to control the moduli part, but their methods do not extend to the non‑Q-factorial case. Consequently, the existence of a minimal model program for non‑Q-factorial log canonical sevenfolds over global function fields remains an open problem, and any breakthrough would likely require a new theory of complements for generalized pairs in mixed characteristic together with a robust notion of Q-factorialization that preserves log canonical singularities.

K‑Stability and the Delta Invariant for Log Fano Pairs with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities in Dimension Seven

K‑stability provides an algebro‑geometric criterion for the existence of Kähler–Einstein metrics on Fano varieties. For a log Fano pair (X,Δ) the delta invariant δ(X,Δ) is defined as the infimum of the log canonical thresholds of effective Q-divisors D∼Q -(K_X+Δ). When X is Q-factorial and klt, the work of Blum–Jonsson (2020) and Fujita–Odaka (2018) shows that uniform K‑stability is equivalent to δ(X,Δ) > 1. For non‑Q-factorial log canonical pairs the definition of δ still makes sense, but the equivalence with uniform K‑stability is not known. In dimension seven the moduli space of K‑stable log Fano pairs with non‑Q-factorial singularities is expected to be a proper Artin stack of finite type, but the boundedness of such pairs is open. The main obstruction is that the usual proof of boundedness (via the boundedness of complements and the ACC for log canonical thresholds) requires the pair to be Q-factorial or at least to admit a Q-factorialization that does not worsen the singularities beyond log canonical. As noted earlier, small Q-factorializations of log canonical sevenfolds can introduce discrepancies <-1. Moreover, the delta invariant for non‑Q-factorial pairs may fail to be a rational number with bounded denominator, complicating the construction of a moduli stack. Recent work of Liu–Xu–Zhuang (2022) on the delta invariant for log canonical pairs uses the theory of b-divisors to define a valuation‑theoretic delta invariant that works without Q-factoriality, but the connection to K‑stability remains conjectural. Thus the study of K‑stability and the delta invariant for non‑Q-factorial log Fano sevenfolds sits at the frontier of birational geometry, Kähler geometry, and moduli theory.

  • Assuming boundedness of complements in dimension seven is known when it is still open.
  • Confusing the ACC for log canonical thresholds (which gives termination) with the existence of flips (which requires finite generation).
  • Stating that small Q-factorializations always preserve log canonical singularities; they can worsen discrepancies.
  • Claiming that the canonical bundle formula holds for non‑Q-factorial fibrations without a nef moduli part M.
  • Treating generalized pairs as ordinary pairs and forgetting that the nef part M must be tracked throughout the MMP.
  • Asserting that finite generation of adjoint rings for generalized pairs is proved in dimension eight; it is not.
  • Overlooking the role of non‑normal fibers in adjunction and the necessity of the conductor divisor being Q-Cartier.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Boundedness of Complements in Dimension Eight

The extension of the boundedness of complements to dimension eight for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs represents a frontier where the inductive strategy that succeeds in lower dimensions encounters fundamental obstructions. In dimensions ≤ 6 the boundedness of complements for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs was established by Birkar (2016) using the ACC for log canonical thresholds and the existence of good minimal models. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the theory of complements developed by Birkar–Zhang (2021) and later refined by Han–Liu (2022) reduces the problem to controlling the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus through a sequence of small ℚ‑factorializations. In dimension seven the base of a relative dimension‑one fibration would be a six‑fold, and boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical six‑folds is known conditionally on the termination of flips for such pairs. In dimension eight the base becomes a seven‑fold, and the termination of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical seven‑folds is open. The core difficulty is that a small ℚ‑factorialization of a log canonical eightfold can introduce discrepancies worse than log canonical, breaking the induction. Recent work by Chen–Jiang (2023) on the boundedness of complements for generalized pairs with non‑nef moduli part suggests that a theory of complements for generalized non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs might bypass this by keeping track of the nef part M throughout the MMP, but the finite generation of the adjoint ring for such generalized pairs in dimension eight remains unproven. Consequently, the boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical eightfolds is currently an open problem, and any proof would likely require a new invariant that controls the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus without worsening singularities, together with a termination argument for flips in the generalized pair setting.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs in Dimension Eight

The existence of flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension eight is the central technical bottleneck for running the MMP in this category. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the existence of flips in all dimensions follows from the finite generation of the relative canonical ring established by Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010). When the pair is non‑ℚ‑factorial and log canonical, the standard approach is to take a small ℚ‑factorialization, run the MMP on the ℚ‑factorial model, and then descend the result. In dimension eight a small ℚ‑factorialization of a log canonical pair may not exist without introducing discrepancies worse than log canonical; even when it exists, the MMP on the ℚ‑factorial model may produce a sequence of flips that does not terminate because the ACC for log canonical thresholds does not directly apply to the non‑ℚ‑factorial setting. The work of Hacon–Xu (2018) on the MMP for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs uses the theory of dlt modifications and the ACC for log canonical thresholds to prove termination. For non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs the dlt modification may not be ℚ‑factorial, and the usual induction on dimension fails because the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus can have codimension two. Birkar (2021) introduced the notion of a generalized pair (X,Δ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor coming from a moduli part, and showed that if the generalized pair is ℚ‑factorial and the moduli part is b‑nef, then the MMP with scaling works. In dimension eight the existence of a b‑nef moduli part for a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair is not known, and the finite generation of the generalized canonical ring R(X,K_X+Δ+M) is open. Without finite generation one cannot construct the flip as Proj of the relative canonical ring. The most promising direction is the theory of complements for generalized pairs developed by Birkar–Zhang (2021), which aims to produce a bounded complement that forces the moduli part to become b‑nef after a bounded number of steps, but this program has not yet been carried out in dimension eight.

Technique: Tracking the Moduli Part in Generalized Pairs
Let (X,Δ) be a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair of dimension eight, and let f: X → Z be a contraction of an extremal ray R with (K_X+Δ)·R < 0. Explain why the standard construction of the flip via the relative canonical ring R(X/Z,K_X+Δ) fails, and describe how the generalized pair formalism (X,Δ+M) attempts to circumvent this failure. What additional hypothesis on M is required for the MMP with scaling to produce a flip?
Failure of the standard construction: The relative canonical ring R(X/Z,K_X+Δ) = ⊕m≥0 f_* O_X(⌊m(K_X+Δ)⌋) requires K_X+Δ to be ℚ‑Cartier to define the round‑down. In a non‑ℚ‑factorial pair K_X+Δ is not ℚ‑Cartier, so the sheaves O_X(⌊m(K_X+Δ)⌋) are not well‑defined as line bundles; the ring is not finitely generated in any meaningful sense, and Proj cannot be formed. Generalized pair formalism: One replaces (X,Δ) by a generalized pair (X,Δ+M) where M is a nef ℚ‑divisor on X (the moduli part) such that K_X+Δ+M is ℚ‑Cartier and the pair is generalized log canonical. The relative canonical ring becomes R(X/Z,K_X+Δ+M) = ⊕m≥0 f_* O_X(⌊m(K_X+Δ+M)⌋), which is well‑defined because K_X+Δ+M is ℚ‑Cartier. If this ring is finitely generated, the flip is Proj_Z R(X/Z,K_X+Δ+M). Required hypothesis on M: For the MMP with scaling to produce a flip, M must be b‑nef (i.e., for every prime divisor E over X, the coefficient of E in the pullback of M is ≥ 0). Birkar (2021) proves that if (X,Δ+M) is a ℚ‑factorial generalized klt pair with M b‑nef, then the MMP with scaling of an ample divisor terminates with a minimal model or Mori fiber space, and flips exist as Proj of the finitely generated relative canonical ring. In the non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical setting, one must first produce a ℚ‑factorialization where the induced moduli part remains b‑nef; this is the core open problem in dimension eight.
Exam‑Day Strategy: Distinguish ℚ‑Factorial vs Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Obstacles
When a question asks about the existence of flips or boundedness of complements in high dimension (≥ 7), first identify whether the pair is assumed ℚ‑factorial. If it is, cite BCHM (2010) for flips and Birkar (2016) for complements. If it is non‑ℚ‑factorial, the answer must discuss: (1) the failure of K_X+Δ to be ℚ‑Cartier, (2) the need for a small ℚ‑factorialization and the risk of worsening discrepancies, (3) the role of the moduli part M in generalized pairs, and (4) the open status of finite generation for the generalized canonical ring. Never claim that results for ℚ‑factorial pairs automatically extend to the non‑ℚ‑factorial case; this is the most common trap in advanced birational geometry questions.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities in Dimension Seven: Abundance and Termination

The abundance conjecture for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfolds asserts that if (X,Δ) is a log canonical pair with K_X+Δ pseudo‑effective, then K_X+Δ is ℚ‑effective up to numerical equivalence, i.e., some multiple is linearly equivalent to an effective divisor. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs abundance is known in dimensions ≤ 3 (Kawamata 1992, Miyaoka 1988) and conditionally in higher dimensions via the minimal model program. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the conjecture is open in dimension four and above. In dimension seven the main obstruction is the lack of a good minimal model: even if one can run the MMP to obtain a minimal model (X',Δ') with KX'+Δ' nef, the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may prevent the basepoint‑free theorem from applying because the usual proof requires KX'+Δ' to be ℚ‑Cartier. The work of Hacon–Xu (2018) on the MMP for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs proves termination of flips using the ACC for log canonical thresholds, but their argument relies on the pair being ℚ‑factorial to ensure that the discrepancies of the log canonical centers are controlled. For non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs the ACC does not directly give termination because a sequence of flips on a ℚ‑factorialization may correspond to an infinite sequence of pseudo‑flips on the original variety. The theory of pseudo‑flips introduced by Birkar (2012) and developed by Hacon–Xu (2019) provides a framework where one works with b‑divisors and does not require ℚ‑factoriality, but the existence of pseudo‑flips in dimension seven for log canonical pairs is not known. Recent progress by Das–Waldron (2023) on the MMP for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over function fields uses p‑adic Hodge theory to control the moduli part, but their methods require ℚ‑factoriality. Consequently, both termination of flips and abundance for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfolds remain open, and any breakthrough would likely require a new invariant that measures the complexity of the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus and decreases under pseudo‑flips.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of Adjunction on Non‑Normal Pairs in Higher Dimensions

Adjunction for non‑normal pairs is a technical cornerstone for running the MMP on varieties whose non‑normal locus has codimension one. Let (X,Δ) be a log canonical pair where X is not necessarily normal, and let ν: X^ν → X be the normalization. The conductor divisor C on X^ν is defined by the ideal sheaf AnnOX^ν(ν_* OX^ν/O_X). When X is ℚ‑factorial, C is ℚ‑Cartier and the adjunction formula reads KX^ν+Δ^ν = ν^*(K_X+Δ) + C, where Δ^ν is the different. For non‑ℚ‑factorial X the conductor C may fail to be ℚ‑Cartier, and the different Δ^ν may not be well‑defined as a ℚ‑divisor. The theory of adjunction on non‑normal pairs developed by Kollár (2013) and extended by Filipazzi (2020) shows that if (X,Δ) is log canonical and the non‑normal locus has codimension one, then there exists a ℚ‑divisor Δ^ν on X^ν such that (X^ν,Δ^ν) is log canonical and KX^ν+Δ^ν = ν^*(K_X+Δ) + C holds as an equality of b‑divisors. The conductor C is then a b‑divisor that is ℚ‑Cartier on a high model. This b‑divisor formalism allows one to run the MMP on non‑normal pairs by lifting to the normalization, performing the MMP on (X^ν,Δ^ν), and descending the result. In higher dimensions (≥ 5) the main difficulty is that the non‑normal locus can have components of varying dimensions, and the conductor b‑divisor may not be nef on the relevant models. The work of Birkar–Zhang (2021) on complements for generalized pairs introduces a moduli part M that captures the failure of C to be nef, leading to a generalized adjunction formula KX^ν+Δ^ν+M = ν^*(K_X+Δ) + C. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension six and above, the finite generation of the adjoint ring for the generalized pair (X^ν,Δ^ν+M) is open, which blocks the construction of flips on the normalization and hence on the original non‑normal variety.

The Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Singularities and the Theory of the Minimal Model Program for Varieties with Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Singularities over Finite Fields in Higher Dimensions

The minimal model program over finite fields and, more generally, over global function fields k = F_q(t) for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimensions ≥ 5 is constrained by the failure of resolution of singularities and the lack of a characteristic‑zero reduction that preserves non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical singularities. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the MMP over function fields is known in all dimensions by the work of Hacon–Xu (2015) and Birkar–Cascini–Hacon–McKernan (2010) via spreading out to a model over a finitely generated Z‑algebra and using ultraproducts to reduce to characteristic zero. The ultraproduct argument requires the pair to be ℚ‑factorial and klt because these properties are preserved under reduction modulo p for sufficiently large p. When the pair is non‑ℚ‑factorial and log canonical, reduction modulo p can introduce non‑log‑canonical singularities (e.g., wild quotient singularities in positive characteristic), and the ACC for log canonical thresholds — which guarantees termination of flips in characteristic zero — does not transfer automatically. In dimension five the base of a relative dimension‑one fibration would be a four‑fold over the function field, and boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical four‑folds over global function fields is unknown. Without boundedness of complements one cannot run the inductive argument that produces a minimal model or a Mori fiber space. Recent progress by Das and Waldron (2023) on the MMP for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over function fields uses p‑adic Hodge theory to control the moduli part, but their methods do not extend to the non‑ℚ‑factorial case because the p‑adic comparison isomorphisms require the pair to be ℚ‑factorial to define the relevant period maps. Consequently, the existence of a minimal model program for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over finite fields in dimensions ≥ 5 remains an open problem, and any breakthrough would likely require a new theory of complements for generalized pairs in mixed characteristic together with a robust notion of ℚ‑factorialization that preserves log canonical singularities under reduction modulo p.

  • Assuming boundedness of complements in dimension eight is known when it is still open.
  • Confusing the ACC for log canonical thresholds (which gives termination) with the existence of flips (which requires finite generation).
  • Stating that small ℚ‑factorializations always preserve log canonical singularities; they can worsen discrepancies.
  • Claiming that the canonical bundle formula holds for non‑ℚ‑factorial fibrations without a nef moduli part M.
  • Treating generalized pairs as ordinary pairs and forgetting that the nef part M must be tracked throughout the MMP.
  • Asserting that finite generation of adjoint rings for generalized pairs is proved in dimension eight; it is not.
  • Overlooking the role of non‑normal fibers in adjunction and the necessity of the conductor divisor being ℚ‑Cartier.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Varieties in Dimension Seven: Abundance and Termination

The abundance conjecture predicts that for a log canonical pair (X,Δ) with nef log canonical divisor K_X+Δ, the divisor is semi‑ample. In dimension three the conjecture is a theorem for klt pairs (Kawamata, 1992) and for log canonical pairs (Fujino, 2000). In dimension four and higher the conjecture remains open even for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the situation is more delicate: the lack of ℚ‑factoriality prevents the direct application of the base‑point‑free theorem, and the minimal model program may produce models that are not ℚ‑factorial. In dimension seven no general abundance result is known. Partial progress exists for special classes: for example, abundance holds for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs whose non‑ℚ‑factorial locus has codimension at least three (by work of Hacon–Xu on ℚ‑factorialization preserving log canonical singularities in low codimension). Termination of flips is another major obstacle. The termination of flips for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs is known in all dimensions (BCHM, 2010) via the ACC for log canonical thresholds. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the ACC does not directly apply because the log canonical thresholds of non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs are not known to satisfy ACC in high dimension. Recent work of Birkar (2021) on the ACC for generalized pairs suggests a possible route, but the required finite generation of adjoint rings for generalized pairs in dimension seven is still open. Consequently, the existence of a minimal model or a Mori fiber space for arbitrary non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfolds is an open problem.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Varieties in Dimension Seven: Boundedness of Complements

Boundedness of complements is a key inductive tool in the minimal model program. A complement of a log canonical pair (X,Δ) is an effective ℚ‑divisor Γ such that K_X+Δ+Γ is log canonical and n(K_X+Δ+Γ) ∼ 0 for some positive integer n. Birkar proved boundedness of complements for log canonical pairs in dimension three (2019). In dimension four the boundedness of complements for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is known (Birkar–Zhang, 2021), but the non‑ℚ‑factorial case remains open. For dimension seven the problem is substantially harder: the induction on dimension requires a good theory of adjunction on non‑normal pairs and a control of the moduli part M appearing in the canonical bundle formula for generalized pairs. The work of Filipazzi (2020) on adjunction for non‑normal log canonical pairs provides a b‑divisor formalism for the conductor, but the nefness of the moduli part M on high models is not established in dimension seven. Without boundedness of complements one cannot run the inductive argument that produces a minimal model or a Mori fiber space for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfolds. Current research focuses on extending the theory of complements to generalized pairs with pseudo‑effective nef part, a program initiated by Birkar–Zhang (2021) and still incomplete in dimensions ≥5.

Worked Example: Checking Complement Boundedness in Dimension Seven
Given a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair (X,Δ) of dimension seven, outline the steps needed to verify whether a complement of index n exists, assuming the moduli part M is nef on a log resolution.
1. Take a log resolution π: Y → X such that the exceptional divisors and the strict transform of Δ have simple normal crossings. 2. Write K_Y+Δ_Y = π^*(K_X+Δ)+E where Δ_Y is the sum of the strict transform of Δ and the exceptional divisors with coefficients given by discrepancies. 3. Because (X,Δ) is log canonical, all discrepancies are ≥ −1. 4. If the moduli part M coming from the canonical bundle formula for the generalized pair (Y,Δ_Y+M) is nef, then by the theory of complements for generalized pairs (Birkar–Zhang) there exists an integer n depending only on the dimension such that n(K_Y+Δ_Y+M) is base‑point‑free. 5. Push forward to X: n(K_X+Δ) is then represented by an effective divisor Γ = π_*(n(K_Y+Δ_Y+M) − nE). 6. Verify that (X,Δ+Γ) is log canonical and that n(K_X+Δ+Γ) ∼ 0. The existence of such n uniformly for all seven‑dimensional non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is precisely the boundedness of complements conjecture in this setting.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the status of a conjecture in a specific dimension, first state the highest dimension where the result is proved, then name the key paper, and finally explain the obstruction that prevents the extension to the next dimension. This structure shows both knowledge of the literature and understanding of the technical barriers.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Varieties over Global Function Fields in Dimension Seven

Running the minimal model program over a global function field k = 𝔽_q(t) for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs requires a reduction‑mod‑p argument that preserves the singularity type. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the MMP over function fields is known in all dimensions (Hacon–Xu, 2015; BCHM, 2010) via spreading out to a finitely generated ℤ‑algebra and using ultraproducts to reduce to characteristic zero. The ultraproduct argument relies on the fact that ℚ‑factoriality and klt singularities are preserved under reduction modulo p for sufficiently large p. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, reduction modulo p can introduce wild quotient singularities that are not log canonical, and the ACC for log canonical thresholds — which guarantees termination of flips in characteristic zero — does not automatically transfer. In dimension seven the base of a relative dimension‑one fibration would be a six‑fold over the function field, and boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical six‑folds over global function fields is unknown. Recent progress by Das and Waldron (2023) on the MMP for ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over function fields uses p‑adic Hodge theory to control the moduli part, but their methods require ℚ‑factoriality to define the relevant period maps. Consequently, the existence of a minimal model program for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs over finite fields in dimension seven remains open, and any breakthrough would likely need a new theory of complements for generalized pairs in mixed characteristic together with a robust ℚ‑factorialization that preserves log canonical singularities under reduction modulo p.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Varieties in the Non‑Archimedean Setting for Dimension Seven

The non‑archimedean minimal model program, developed by Boucksom–Jonsson (2020) and extended by others, uses the Berkovich analytification of a variety over a non‑archimedean field to study degenerations and K‑stability. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the non‑archimedean MMP is known in all dimensions (by work of Xu–Zhuang, 2022). For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the main difficulty is that the non‑archimedean valuation theory does not automatically respect the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus. The theory of non‑archimedean log canonical thresholds and the ACC for them is not established in high dimension. In dimension seven the construction of non‑archimedean flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs would require a finite generation result for the non‑archimedean relative canonical ring, which is open. Moreover, the valuative criterion for K‑stability (Fujita–Odaka, 2018) is formulated for ℚ‑Gorenstein log Fano pairs; extending it to non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs involves controlling the discrepancy of valuations centered on the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus. Recent work of Liu–Xu (2024) on non‑archimedean MMP for generalized pairs introduces a moduli part M in the non‑archimedean setting, but the nefness of M on the relevant non‑archimedean models is not known in dimension seven. Thus the non‑archimedean MMP for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfolds is currently out of reach.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Varieties on Non‑Projective Kähler Manifolds in Dimension Seven

The Kähler minimal model program, initiated by Campana–Peternell (1991) and advanced by Höring–Peternell (2016), establishes the existence of minimal models for projective varieties with klt singularities in all dimensions. For non‑projective Kähler manifolds the MMP is known only in dimension three (by work of Fujino, 2015) and for special classes such as Kähler Calabi–Yau threefolds. In dimension seven the obstacles are twofold: first, the lack of a projective embedding prevents the use of the cone and contraction theorems in their standard algebraic form; second, non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical singularities on a Kähler manifold may not admit a Kähler ℚ‑factorialization that preserves the log canonical property. The analytic version of the base‑point‑free theorem (Demailly–Păun, 2004) requires the divisor to be nef and big, but for log canonical pairs the log canonical divisor may fail to be ℚ‑Cartier, so the theorem does not apply directly. Recent progress by Cao–Höring (2022) on the Kähler MMP for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs uses the analytic minimal model program with scaling, but the scaling technique relies on the existence of a Kähler current with analytic singularities, which is not guaranteed for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs. Therefore, the minimal model program for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical Kähler sevenfolds remains completely open, and even the existence of a single flip in this setting is unknown.

  • Assuming boundedness of complements in dimension seven is known when it is still open.
  • Confusing the ACC for log canonical thresholds (which gives termination) with the existence of flips (which requires finite generation).
  • Stating that small ℚ‑factorializations always preserve log canonical singularities; they can worsen discrepancies.
  • Claiming that the canonical bundle formula holds for non‑ℚ‑factorial fibrations without a nef moduli part M.
  • Treating generalized pairs as ordinary pairs and forgetting that the nef part M must be tracked throughout the MMP.
  • Asserting that finite generation of adjoint rings for generalized pairs is proved in dimension seven; it is not.
  • Overlooking the role of non‑normal fibers in adjunction and the necessity of the conductor divisor being ℚ‑Cartier.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Seven: Abundance and Termination

The abundance conjecture predicts that for a log canonical pair (X, Δ) with a nef log canonical divisor KX + Δ, the divisor is semi‑ample. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the conjecture is known in dimensions up to three (Kawamata, 1992) and in dimension four for certain special cases (Hacon–McKernan, 2010). When the pair is non‑ℚ‑factorial and only log canonical, the situation is dramatically more difficult. In dimension seven no general abundance theorem exists. The main obstruction is the lack of a finite generation result for the adjoint ring ⊕m≥0 H⁰(X, ⌊m(KX+Δ)⌋) without assuming ℚ‑factoriality. The standard proof of abundance uses the base‑point‑free theorem, which requires the divisor to be ℚ‑Cartier. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the log canonical divisor may fail to be ℚ‑Cartier on the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus, so the base‑point‑free theorem does not apply directly. Recent work on the minimal model program for generalized pairs (Birkar–Zhang, 2022) introduces a nef moduli part M to compensate, but the nefness of M on a minimal model for a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfold is not established. Termination of flips, which is equivalent to the ascending chain condition for log canonical thresholds, is known for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs in all dimensions (BCHM, 2010). For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the ACC for log canonical thresholds is open in dimension seven; without it one cannot guarantee that any sequence of flips terminates. Consequently, both abundance and termination remain open problems for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension seven.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Seven: Boundedness of Complements

The theory of complements, initiated by Shokurov and developed by Birkar, provides a uniform way to bound the indices of log canonical divisors. For ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the boundedness of complements is known in dimensions up to three (Birkar, 2019) and in dimension four for certain classes (Birkar–Cascini, 2021). In dimension seven the boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is completely open. A complement of index n for a pair (X, Δ) is an effective ℚ‑divisor Γ ∼ℚ −(KX+Δ) such that KX+Δ+Γ is log canonical and n(KX+Δ+Γ) ∼ 0. The existence of a uniform bound N(d, ε) depending only on dimension d and a lower bound ε on the coefficients of Δ would imply the existence of complements of bounded index. For non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs the difficulty is twofold: first, the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus may contain strata where the different is not ℚ‑Cartier, so the usual adjunction formula does not produce a ℚ‑divisor on the stratum; second, the induction on dimension used in the ℚ‑factorial case breaks down because a ℚ‑factorialization of a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair can worsen discrepancies, potentially creating non‑log‑canonical singularities. Recent progress on the boundedness of complements for generalized pairs (Birkar, 2023) introduces a nef part M and requires the pair to be generalized log canonical. Even in that setting the boundedness in dimension seven is not proved. Therefore, the boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical sevenfolds remains an open frontier.

Technique: Identifying the Obstruction to Abundance
Explain why the base‑point‑free theorem cannot be applied directly to a nef log canonical divisor KX+Δ on a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair (X, Δ) of dimension seven.
The base‑point‑free theorem (Kawamata–Shokurov) requires the divisor to be ℚ‑Cartier. On a non‑ℚ‑factorial variety there exist Weil divisors that are not ℚ‑Cartier; the log canonical divisor KX+Δ may fail to be ℚ‑Cartier precisely along the non‑ℚ‑factorial locus. Since the theorem’s hypothesis is not satisfied, one cannot conclude semi‑ampleness. Any proof of abundance must first replace X by a ℚ‑factorial model (a ℚ‑factorialization) or work with generalized pairs where a nef moduli part M compensates for the non‑ℚ‑Cartier part. In dimension seven neither a log canonical ℚ‑factorialization nor the nefness of M on the resulting model is known, so the obstruction persists.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the status of abundance or boundedness of complements for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in a given dimension, first state the known results for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs, then explicitly note which hypotheses fail (ℚ‑Cartier condition, ACC for log canonical thresholds, existence of ℚ‑factorializations preserving log canonicity). Cite the key theorems (BCHM 2010 for flips, Kawamata 1992 for abundance in low dimension, Birkar 2019 for complements in dimension three) and conclude with the current open status. This structure shows both breadth and precision.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Seven: Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs

The existence of flips is a cornerstone of the minimal model program. For ℚ‑factorial klt pairs the existence of flips in all dimensions was proved by Birkar, Cascini, Hacon, and McKernan (2010) using the finite generation of the relative canonical ring. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs the situation is far less developed. A flip is a birational map that replaces a small contraction of an extremal ray with another small contraction such that the flipped divisor becomes ample. The construction of a flip requires the finite generation of the algebra ⊕m≥0 f*𝒪X(⌊m(KX+Δ)⌋) over the base of the contraction. In the non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical setting the log canonical divisor may not be ℚ‑Cartier, so the sheaves 𝒪X(⌊m(KX+Δ)⌋) are not necessarily invertible, and the usual Proj construction does not apply directly. One approach is to pass to a ℚ‑factorialization π: Y → X and run the MMP on Y. However, a ℚ‑factorialization of a log canonical pair can introduce discrepancies worse than log canonical, breaking the induction. Another approach uses the theory of generalized pairs, where one adds a nef moduli part M to the boundary. The finite generation of the adjoint ring for generalized pairs is known only in low dimensions (up to three) and under strong assumptions on M. In dimension seven no finite generation theorem for the relative canonical ring of a non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pair is available, and consequently the existence of a single flip is unknown. This makes the flip problem the primary bottleneck for any MMP in this category.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Six: Flips for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Pairs

Dimension six is the highest dimension where some partial results on flips for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs have been announced, but a complete theorem is still missing. The main known result is the existence of flips for ℚ‑factorial dlt pairs in all dimensions (BCHM, 2010). For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, recent work by Hacon–Xu (2021) on the MMP for generalized pairs establishes the existence of flips under the assumption that the nef part M is abundant and that the pair is generalized log canonical. In dimension six these hypotheses are not known to hold in general. The obstruction is again the finite generation of the relative canonical ring. Some progress has been made using the theory of b‑divisors and the canonical bundle formula for fibrations with non‑ℚ‑factorial fibers (Filipazzi, 2022), but the formula requires the moduli part to be nef, which is not guaranteed. Moreover, the ACC for log canonical thresholds in dimension six for non‑ℚ‑factorial pairs is open, so termination of any potential flip sequence cannot be assured. As a result, while the flip problem in dimension six is slightly more tractable than in dimension seven, it remains an open problem in full generality.

The Minimal Model Program for Non‑ℚ‑Factorial Log Canonical Pairs in Dimension Eight: Abundance and Termination

Moving to dimension eight, the difficulties compound. The abundance conjecture for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs is known only up to dimension three in full generality, with conditional results in dimension four assuming the minimal model program. For non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs, even the formulation of abundance requires a suitable notion of semi‑ampleness for Weil divisors that are not ℚ‑Cartier. One possible framework is the theory of b‑semi‑ampleness for b‑divisors, but this has not been developed for log canonical pairs in high dimension. Termination of flips is equivalent to the ACC for log canonical thresholds. The ACC is known for ℚ‑factorial klt pairs in all dimensions (BCHM, 2010) and for log canonical pairs in dimension up to three (Birkar, 2019). In dimension eight the ACC for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs is completely open. Without ACC one cannot guarantee that any sequence of flips (if they existed) would terminate. Furthermore, the boundedness of complements, which often provides the inductive step for ACC, is unknown in dimension eight. Consequently, both abundance and termination for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs in dimension eight are wide open, and any progress would likely require new techniques beyond the current generalized pair machinery.

  • Assuming that a ℚ‑factorialization of a log canonical pair always preserves log canonicity; it can create worse singularities.
  • Confusing the ACC for log canonical thresholds (which gives termination) with the existence of flips (which requires finite generation of canonical rings).
  • Stating that the base‑point‑free theorem applies to nef log canonical divisors on non‑ℚ‑factorial varieties without checking the ℚ‑Cartier condition.
  • Treating generalized pairs as ordinary pairs and forgetting to track the nef moduli part M throughout the MMP.
  • Claiming that boundedness of complements is known in dimension seven for non‑ℚ‑factorial log canonical pairs; it is only known up to dimension three.
  • Overlooking that the canonical bundle formula for non‑ℚ‑factorial fibrations requires the moduli part to be nef, which is not established in high dimension.
